# Mukhtasar al-Athar: The Summary of the Narrations

<!-- edition-intro:begin -->

## Introduction

This is a manual of religious practice, laid out topic by topic the way a working handbook of law and worship is built. Its nine chapters move in the settled order such books follow: they open with the duty to seek knowledge, then turn to purity and the washing that readies the body, to the standing of formal prayer, to supplication in its many forms, to the rites owed the dead, to the alms due from wealth, to the fast of Ramadan, to the pilgrimage to the House, and finally to the wider business of the community — warfare, governance, and the doing of justice. Read straight through, it is a compact tour of the obligations a believer is expected to carry, from the most private act of cleansing to the ordering of a state.

The title carries a numeral, which places this volume as the first of a set rather than a whole standing alone; what follows here is its own opening, translated and printed directly below.

A word about who is speaking. This is a transmitted report: the text relays received teaching rather than voicing a single named author's opinions. When you meet a first person on the page, it belongs to the narrator carrying the material forward, not to an "I" who is confiding in you — read it as testimony being passed along a chain, not as memoir. Mistaking the one for the other will make the book sound more personal than it is.

Much of the working vocabulary stays in Arabic because the concepts have no clean English equivalent. The names of the obligations themselves — zakat, the pilgrimage, the fast, jihad — recur as fixed technical terms, each pointing to a defined act with its own conditions rather than to a loose English gloss. Treat these words as anchors: when one returns, it means the same precise thing it meant before, and the chapters are in large part an unfolding of what each one requires.
<!-- edition-intro:end -->

## 1. The First Obligation: Seeking Knowledge

In the name of God, the Most Merciful, the Most Compassionate.

God, mighty and majestic, said: ﴿يَرْفَعِ اللَّهُ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا مِنكُمْ وَالَّذِينَ أُوتُوا الْعِلْمَ دَرَجَاتٍ﴾, "Allah will raise those of you who believe and those who have been given knowledge by degrees" [Surah al-Mujadalah: 11]. And the Almighty said: ﴿هَلْ يَسْتَوِي الَّذِينَ يَعْلَمُونَ وَالَّذِينَ لَا يَعْلَمُونَ إِنَّمَا يَتَذَكَّرُ أُولُو الْأَلْبَابِ﴾, "Are those who know equal to those who do not know? Only those possessed of understanding take heed" [Surah al-Zumar: 9].

And He said: ﴿بَلْ هُوَ آيَاتٌ بَيِّنَاتٌ فِي صُدُورِ الَّذِينَ أُوتُوا الْعِلْمَ﴾, "Rather, it is clear signs preserved in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge" [Surah al-Ankabut: 49].

The Messenger of God (صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم) said: "Whomever God intends good for, He grants understanding of religion." And he said: "Four things are obligatory upon every person of reason and intellect among my community:

1. To listen to knowledge
2. To preserve it
3. To act upon it
4. To spread it"

And he said: "Learn from the scholar of my household, for whoever learns from the scholar of my household will be saved from the Fire." And he said: "There is no rest in life save for one who speaks with knowledge or one who listens with understanding." And he said: "This knowledge is borne in every generation by its upright ones, who repel from it the distortions of the ignorant, the false interpretations of those who go to excess, and the misappropriations of the fabricators." And he said: "When a man goes out in search of knowledge, God records good deeds for his every footstep; and when he meets the scholar and the two recall together something of the command of God, the angels spread their wings over them, and they are called from above: God has forgiven you both." And he said: "The first stage of knowledge is silence; the second is listening; the third is spreading it; and the fourth is acting upon it."

Ali (عليه السلام) said: "Seeking knowledge is an obligation upon every Muslim, man and woman." He also said: "Whoever buys and sells without gaining understanding of religion has plunged into usury — and then plunged into it again." And he said: "Four things are such that, were riding-beasts driven hard toward them until worn out, that would be a small price:

1. That the servant place his hope in none but his Lord.
2. That he fear nothing but his own sin.
3. That the ignorant not be ashamed to learn.
4. That the scholar, when asked about what he does not know, say, *I do not know.*"

It is reported from Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) that he said: "Seek knowledge, and adorn it with forbearance and dignity. Be humble toward those whom you teach, and do not be tyrannical scholars, lest your falsehood undo your truth." He also said: "Were a young man of our Shia brought to me who had not gained understanding in religion, I would discipline him well." And he said: "The believer does not cease to bequeath to his household knowledge and righteous conduct until he brings them all into Paradise, so that not one of them is lost — neither young nor old, neither servant nor neighbor. And the disobedient servant does not cease to bequeath to his household ignorance and evil conduct until he brings them all into the Fire, so that not one of them is lost — neither young nor old, neither servant nor neighbor."

## 2. Purity: Water, Ablution, and the Body Made Ready

**On the merits of purity**

God Almighty said: *"Indeed, God loves those who constantly repent and loves those who purify themselves"* [Surah al-Baqarah: 222]. And He said: *"Within it are men who love to purify themselves, and God loves those who purify themselves"* [Surah al-Tawbah: 108]. And He said: *"And He sent down upon you rain from the sky to purify you thereby, and to remove the harm of Satan from you, and to fortify your hearts and make your feet firm thereby"* [Surah al-Anfal: 11].

> خُذْ مِنْ أَمْوَلِهِمْ صَدَقَةًۭ تُطَهِّرُهُمْ وَتُزَكِّيهِم بِهَا

The Messenger of God (ص) said: "God does not accept prayer except with purity." He also said: "God will gather my community on the Day of Resurrection among the nations, radiant and distinguished by the traces of ablution." And he said: "When I was taken up to the heavens, I was asked concerning what the denizens of the highest assembly were disputing. I said, I do not know, and then it was made known to me: the thorough completion of ablution in times of cold, the walking of feet to the congregations, and the awaiting of prayer after prayer."

From Ali (ع) it is reported that he said: "Ablution is half of faith." He also said: "Whoever purifies himself and performs the purification well, then walks to the mosque, is in a state of prayer so long as nothing occurs to break it." Ali would perform ablution before every prayer even when nothing had occurred to break it, seeking thereby the merit of purity and its reward, in light of the excellence that had been reported concerning it.

**On the purity and impurity of water**

> وَأَنزَلْنَا مِنَ ٱلسَّمَآءِ مَآءًۭ طَهُورًۭا

God Almighty said: *"And We sent down from the sky pure water"* [Surah al-Furqan: 48]. He also said: *"And He sent down upon you from the sky water to purify you thereby"* [Surah al-Anfal: 11]. And again: *"Have you not seen that God sent down water from the sky, then caused it to flow as springs in the earth?"* [Surah al-Zumar: 21]. Through these verses, God — exalted be His praise — established that the water of the earth originates from the sky, and the transmitted reports confirm its purity.

> أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ صَيْدُ ٱلْبَحْرِ وَطَعَامُهُۥ مَتَعًۭا لَّكُمْ وَلِلسَّيَّارَةِ

The Messenger of God (ص) said: "Water is a purifier; nothing renders it impure." Concerning the sea, he said: "Its water is a purifier and its dead are lawful." What he meant by this is whatever is extracted from the sea alive and then dies after being brought out — not what dies while still within it. God Almighty said: *"Lawful to you is the game of the sea and its food, as provision for you and for travelers"* [Surah al-Maidah: 96], meaning what is drawn from the sea and eaten outside it, not what is consumed beneath the water or at its floor. "Its dead," then, refers to what dies after having been brought out alive. Whatever dies within the water before it is caught is not lawful to eat, according to the position of the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) — and that is the fish which floats. This matter will be addressed in the chapter on foods, God willing.

Natural water — water that arises without human making — is pure. God Almighty made this clear in His Book, and His Messenger (ص) made it clear in his speech: if something is cast into water and alters it, changing it away from the nature of water, the ruling of water no longer applies to it. It is reclassified according to whatever has been cast in and has prevailed. Water into which dates, raisins, or honey are thrown illustrates this: if those additions do not change the water and remain imperceptible within it, its ruling remains the ruling of water — until what was cast in becomes evident and manifest, altering the water's color or taste. At that point it becomes a fermented drink, the name of water ceases to apply to it, and purification with it is no longer permitted.

> وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ

No disagreement is known among Muslims on this matter, except for the position held by Abu Hanifah alone and those of his followers who agreed with him. He held that performing ablution with date wine specifically is permissible, though not with other cooked beverages. His companion Abu Yusuf disagreed and maintained that ablution is valid with nothing but water. Muhammad ibn al-Hasan said: "If you do not find water, perform ablution with date wine, then perform tayammum." This position is without meaning. God Almighty said: وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ — *"And if you find no water, then betake yourselves to clean earth"* [Surah al-Nisa: 43]. If date wine is not water, there is no sense in using it for purification; and if it is water, there is no sense in the tayammum. What led Abu Hanifah to this view was a report that reached him: that the Messenger of God (ص) performed ablution with date wine on the Night of the Jinn. That hadith is not established. Even were it established, the drink in question would not be what people know today as date wine. The Prophet (ص) used to steep dates in the waterskin from which he drank, in order to sweeten water that was brackish. He would drink from it and perform ablution with it so long as it had not changed. As already noted, there is no harm in this, and it does not strip water of its ruling unless the water itself is altered and departs from its essential character.

The same principle applies when milk mixes with water. If the milk is not discernible within it, the water retains its ruling — until the milk prevails, at which point the ruling of milk takes over and the name of water no longer applies. The same holds for whatever other drinks or substances mix with water.

This principle governs impurity as well. When an impurity enters water and becomes apparent — altering its color, its taste, or its smell — the water is impure and takes on the ruling of that impurity. But if the impurity does not manifest and cannot be discerned within it, the water remains in its original state: lawful to drink and lawful to use for purification.

This is the agreed position of the Ahl al-Bayt, established on their authority. Those among the companions of al-Shafi'i who lay claim to reasoned inquiry, the people of hadith, and many who are counted among the jurists of the broader scholarly body have all concurred with them on it.

When water changes in color, smell, or taste through something other than impurity — stagnant water that has stood long in one place and altered by that stillness alone — it remains pure, because no impurity brought about the change. Ali said: "Nothing renders water impure," meaning so long as the water still answers to the ruling of water, as has been described. He was asked about running water that passes by a carcass, excrement, and blood — whether one may perform ablution from it and drink from it. He said: "Yes," meaning so long as none of those things is discernible within it. He was asked about a pool in which beasts had urinated and dropped dung and in which a person in a state of janabah had bathed. He said: "There is no harm in it. The Messenger of God (ص) once halted with his companions during a journey at a pool. Their mounts would urinate in it and drop dung in it, and they would bathe in it, perform ablution from it, and drink from it."

> وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ

He was asked about a small ablution basin near the mosque, into which a menstruating woman and a boy had each placed a hand. He said: "Perform ablution from it, for the water is defiled by nothing."

He was asked about a pool beside a village into which a child and beasts would urinate and drop dung. He said: "If something troubles your heart concerning it, do this" — and he gestured with his hand, meaning stir the water — "then drink and perform ablution, for the religion is not a narrow thing." God Almighty said: *"And He has laid upon you no hardship in religion"* [Surah al-Hajj: 78].

He was asked about a pool in which a carcass lay. He said: "If the water is dominant and no smell is found in it, then perform ablution from it."

He also said: "If the water is two cubits by two cubits at a depth of two cubits, nothing renders it impure except what prevails over it and changes its color, its smell, or its taste" — meaning from impurities.

Reports of this kind, narrated from them, are many. The same ruling applies to a well in which an animal has died:

- If the water has been altered, it is drawn out until the alteration is gone and the water prevails again.
- If nothing has been altered, there is no harm.

In either case, whatever died in the well is removed and cast away, lest it alter the water. There is no sound ruling regarding water beyond this.

If those who oppose this position were held to their own analogy, no purification would ever be valid on their own principle. When a person cleanses himself and touches his private part with water, that water mingles with the impurity there by the very act of contact. By their words and their analogy, that water would become impure. The further water flowing toward it would then be defiled by contact with that moisture, so that on their analogy it could never purify anything.

The same follows for a garment washed in a tub that holds impurity: every portion of water poured over it mingles with the moisture already present, and on their analogy none of it could ever render the garment pure.

The same follows when a creature possessing blood dies in a well. They hold that a fixed number of buckets must be drawn out, and that the well water is impure until that number has been reached. Should any of it fall into another well before then, that second well is defiled by their reckoning. When all but the final bucket has been drawn, the well is still impure by their account. When that last bucket is drawn, the well becomes pure — yet the water in the bucket itself remains impure. And as it rises, much of it spatters back: into the well, and into the bucket. That bucket was plunged into the very water they declared pure at the final draw, and it carries the moisture of impure water. On their own analogy it has corrupted the well again, and on their own analogy purification could never be achieved.

The proofs along these lines are many. The constraint of brevity prevents exhausting them here.

God Almighty said: *"O you who believe, when you rise to pray, wash your faces and your hands to the elbows, and wipe your heads and your feet to the ankles. And if you are in a state of janabah, then purify yourselves. But if you are ill, or on a journey, or one of you has come from the place of relieving himself, or you have touched women and find no water, then betake yourselves to clean earth and wipe your faces and hands with it"* [Surah al-Maidah: 6].

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: His words "when you rise to pray" refer to rising from sleep; His words "or one of you has come from the place of relieving himself" are an indirect expression for the occurrence of ritual impurity — the *ghait* being the low-lying ground, the place to which people would withdraw to attend to their bodily need; and His words "or you have touched women" mean intercourse, expressed indirectly through the word *touching*, since touching is contact.

Once a person has purified himself, that purity is not broken except by sleep that overcomes him and comes between him and his awareness of what may have issued from him. One who dozes lightly while still perceiving what is happening to him, without sinking into heavy sleep, does not break his ablution.

Ablution is broken by:

- Urine
- Excrement
- Wind issuing from the posterior
- Anything else that issues from the posterior or the front — whether blood, discharge, pus, worms, moisture, or anything of that kind

What issues from other than these two outlets — whether blood, vomit, discharge, or anything else — does not break ablution.

Ablution is not broken by:

- Touching with the hand
- Contact short of intercourse
- A kiss
- Direct touching
- Touching the male organ or the private part
- Food or drink

Whatever impurity has reached the body is washed away from it. It is recommended to cleanse oneself of grease and other things that carry an odor, though this is not obligatory in a binding sense that would render prayer invalid without it; cleanliness and purity in these matters are recommended.

Whoever is certain that he is in a state of purity and then doubts whether he has broken it — without being certain that he has — owes no fresh purification.

Whoever has broken his purity and then doubts whether he has purified himself must purify himself. All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt: in their school, nothing breaks ablution except what issues from the two outlets, front and posterior.

As for al-madhi — the thin fluid that issues from the male organ through sexual desire — it requires only ablution, not the greater washing. Al-wadhi, the thin fluid that follows urine, likewise requires only ablution and not the greater washing.

If a man enters a state of janabah through intercourse or otherwise, and performs the greater washing before urinating, and water then issues from his urethra after the washing, that discharge is a remnant of the janabah and he must repeat the washing. If, however, he had urinated before washing, the urine has already expelled that remnant; and if anything issues after the urination or the washing, only ablution is required, and no washing is upon him. For this reason, whoever has had intercourse or a nocturnal emission should not perform the greater washing until he has urinated.

Whoever has a nocturnal emission and finds the gushing fluid upon waking must perform the greater washing. If he dreams of having relations but finds no wetness, nothing is required of him. If he supposes that he emitted during sleep, the greater washing becomes obligatory only upon the actual emergence of semen.

Whoever has relations short of penetration while awake and does not emit owes no washing until he does emit. But whoever achieves penetration owes the washing regardless of emission, once the two circumcised parts have met — that is, once the glans, which is the head of the male organ, has disappeared within the private part. When that has occurred, the washing is obligatory whether or not emission follows.

All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt.

**On the manner of ablution and the greater washing and their proper etiquette**

The Ahl al-Bayt transmitted teachings on several matters related to relieving oneself. They commanded that the private parts be covered, that a suitable place be sought for urine and excrement, that whatever concealment is available be used, and that one withdraw as far from people as possible. They forbade urinating or defecating in stagnant water, on the banks of rivers and wells, on roads, beneath fruit-bearing trees, among graves, or anywhere that would cause harm to others. The one who intends to relieve himself must not face the qiblah nor turn his back to it, and must guard against any of it reaching his body or garment.

They commanded whoever enters or intends to use a place of relieving himself to invoke the name of God and to seek refuge from the accursed Satan. Upon finishing, he should praise God and give thanks for what He has made easy — the wholesomeness of food and drink and the removal of their harm from the body.

They permitted urinating and defecating into a basin or vessel. They also commanded the drawing out of the male organ after urination, so that what remains in the shaft may be expelled. If water is at hand, he washes it; if no water is available, he wipes the urethra against clean ground, a stone, a cloth, or something similar.

If he has defecated, he wipes with stones, clods of earth, rags, or the like, repeating the wiping until he is clean, and examining what he last wiped with to see whether anything still clings to it.

They forbade wiping with food, bone, dried dung, fruit stones — meaning the pits and kernels of fruits — and with charcoal.

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوٓا۟ إِذَا قُمْتُمْ إِلَى ٱلصَّلَوٰةِ فَٱغْسِلُوا۟ وُجُوهَكُمْ وَأَيْدِيَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْمَرَافِقِ وَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِرُئُوسِكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْكَعْبَيْنِ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُمْ جُنُبًۭا فَٱطَّهَّرُوا۟ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُم مِّنْهُ

Whoever intends to perform ablution or the greater washing is commanded to form the intention for it. If he does not intend it — washing only to cool himself, or performing ablution merely to instruct another without intending it for himself — it does not suffice him, and he must repeat whatever was made obligatory upon him, this time with an intention formed within himself. It is recommended that whoever intends the ablution invoke the name of God at its beginning. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) said: "Whoever names God over his ablution, his whole body is purified" — meaning purified of sins, God willing. As for its purifying him of janabah before he performs the greater washing, it does not; yet he is still encouraged toward it. Whoever does not name God has not thereby spoiled his ablution.

The washing of the two hands before entering them into the vessel is commanded. If he enters them into the vessel while they are clean, nothing is required of him, though washing is better. He then cleanses himself of excrement and urine and of everything that issues from their two outlets. No obligatory cleansing is required for wind issuing from the posterior, though it is recommended, and some among them used to do it in pursuit of its merit.

He begins the cleansing with the front, then lowers his hand to the posterior and clears away whatever moisture or smear is there until it is gone — pouring the water with his right hand and washing with his left — then rubbing with water after the cleansing until the smell is gone. He does not cleanse with his right hand except on account of some excuse.

He rinses his mouth and draws water into his nose three times — both acts belong to the Sunnah and ought not to be abandoned. If someone omits them without intending to forsake the practice, nothing is required of him. He then washes his face from top to bottom. The man gathers his beard in his hand and wipes over it; if he runs water through it, that too is good. The face is washed three times. Washing it twice, or once completely, suffices, though three is better; there is no merit in going beyond three. The same applies to the washing of the hands. He then washes his arms to the elbows, three times each.

For the hands, as for the face, one washing suffices and three is better; beyond three there is no further merit. If a man wears a ring, he must move it so that water reaches the skin beneath it, and he must leave no part of his face or arms untouched.

The face extends from the hairline of the forehead down to the bottom of the chin, and across from the tip of one set of sideburns to the other, taking in the lobes of the ears.

He then wipes his head, moving forward and then back: he begins at the top and wipes toward the front, then returns from that point toward the nape, using the moisture still on his hands. One such pass suffices; three is better, though not obligatory. He wipes his ears at the same time — their outer and inner surfaces alike — and if he takes fresh water for a second wiping of them, that is better.

He then wipes the tops of his feet with a wiping motion. This is the meaning of the word of Allah Almighty:

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوٓا۟ إِذَا قُمْتُمْ إِلَى ٱلصَّلَوٰةِ فَٱغْسِلُوا۟ وُجُوهَكُمْ وَأَيْدِيَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْمَرَافِقِ وَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِرُئُوسِكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْكَعْبَيْنِ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُمْ جُنُبًۭا فَٱطَّهَّرُوا۟ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُم مِّنْهُ

"فَاغْسِلُوا وُجُوهَكُمْ وَأَيْدِيَكُمْ إِلَى الْمَرَافِقِ وَامْسَحُوا بِرُءُوسِكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُمْ إِلَى الْكَعْبَيْنِ" — "Wash your faces and your hands to the elbows, and wipe your heads and your feet to the heels." [al-Maidah 6:6]

God has made obligatory the washing of the face and the hands to the elbows (إلى المرافق), and the wiping of the head and the feet to the heels (إلى الكعبين). If a man washes his feet in addition to wiping over them, that is good; and if he passes his fingers between his toes, that too is good and carries reward.

He must not take the limbs out of order. He begins where God began: the face, then the hands, then the head, then the feet. If he departs from that sequence and washes an earlier limb after a later one, he returns and rewashes the later limb so that the proper order is restored, first things first.

If, in the midst of his ablution, he breaks off for some matter that did not cause him to lose his way, and then resumes and completes what remained without starting over, that suffices. The interruption does not spoil it. This allowance holds only when the break was made for a genuine excuse — not when it was deliberate.

As for the quantity of water used in ablution, no fixed measure has been set. People vary: some are gentle with water and content with a little, while others are wasteful and are never satisfied with what would have been enough for someone else. In the full and generous performance of ablution there is merit.

The limbs of ablution must be thoroughly wetted with water as commanded; if the water merely passes over them without penetrating, it has no effect. Where a hand or a foot has been amputated, the water is passed over the place of amputation.

It is recommended to perform ablution before the prayer time arrives, so that when the time enters the worshipper is already in a state of purity and can pray at its outset.

The scholars disapproved of wastefulness with water in ablution and counted it among the whisperings of the devils. Even so, they permitted a person to dry himself with a cloth after finishing ablution or ghusl. They also commended supplication and the remembrance of Allah while washing each limb, and again upon completion.

On the matter of wiping over socks, the scholars were strict, and their prohibition was severe. The prayer of one who wipes over his socks after a state of ritual impurity is not valid, nor is the prayer of anyone who follows such a person, because this contradicts the Book of Allah — whose mention is exalted — which commands only the wiping of the feet themselves.

Where a person has an ailment in the feet that washing would harm, wiping water over them suffices. The same allowance extends to feet enclosed in socks, limbs bound with dressings, and any other limb of ablution that is bound, or over which a splint has been set on account of a wound or a fracture — wiping over all of that is sufficient.

Wiping over sandals suffices when the water reaches the tops of the feet. Beyond that, however — a head-cloth, a turban, or anything of that kind — wiping does not suffice, unless there is a genuine excuse of ailment.

Whoever intends the greater washing — whether from janabah or from menstruation — begins by performing ablution as described, then pours water over the head and over the whole body until the hair is wetted and whatever soiling remained on the body is cleansed. He passes his hands over the rest of his body so that no place is left untouched, reaching everything he can reach. When this is done, he is purified. Should any spot remain that water has not reached, he must bring water to it.

No fixed measure governs the water used for ghusl, as was said regarding ablution. Ghusl is valid under falling rain at the time of rain, and it is valid by a single complete immersion in water, provided the bather passes his hands over his body as described and the water reaches every part of it.

Ghusl becomes obligatory in the following cases:

- From janabah
- From menstruation
- When a disbeliever accepts Islam
- When a person who has been unconscious for days, or for a stretch of time he cannot account for, returns to his senses — since he cannot be certain whether a state of janabah befell him during that time; this applies when the unconsciousness was of that severity

Where the unconsciousness was slighter — and its circumstances could be perceived and known, so that a person would have sensed whether a discharge occurred — no ghusl is required on that account.

> وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ

The dead must be washed when they die. When a person performing ghusl from janabah passes his hands over the limbs of ablution while intending both the ghusl and the ablution together, that single act suffices for both. If a woman experiences intercourse in her sleep and a discharge occurs, ghusl becomes obligatory upon her, just as it is upon a man who experiences the same and finds that a discharge has occurred.

One upon whom ghusl is due should perform it as soon as he is able and not delay it — unless he intends to return to intercourse shortly, in which case there is no harm in delaying the ghusl until he has done so and then washing. If he washes between the two acts, that is better. If he engages in intercourse again, he washes again. When the time of a prayer arrives, however, he must not delay the ghusl, regardless of whether he intends to return to intercourse.

The following are disliked for the one in a state of janabah before he has washed:

- eating or drinking
- trimming his nails
- shaving his hair
- being cupped

He should also not sit in the mosque while in that state. Passing through it for some need, without sitting, is permitted, as Allah Almighty says: "وَلَا جُنُبًا إِلَّا عَابِرِي سَبِيلٍ" — "nor while in janabah, except those passing through" [al-Nisa 4:43].

If a woman has had intercourse, or has become junub, and then menstruates before she has washed, one ghusl suffices for both once she sees that she has become pure.

When a woman washes, she need not undo her braids, provided she wets her hair thoroughly enough to know that the water has reached all of it and penetrated to the skin of her head. There is no harm in washing while the hair remains braided, as long as the braid is not so tight that moisture cannot enter — some slackness must allow the water through. If anything binds the hair, she moves it so that the water reaches beneath.

The bather inserts a finger into his navel and passes water within it. Once the ghusl is complete, he supplicates with whatever has been granted to him and asks Allah to purify him of his sins.

**The purity of garments and of the ground**

It is related from the People of the House (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that every animal whose flesh is lawful to eat has pure skin, feathers, hair, wool, fur, bone, and sinew — provided it was slaughtered in the prescribed manner. If it died without proper slaughter, all of those parts are impure. The scholars permitted wearing and making use of such a hide in the way one wears and uses a garment that has been touched by impurity, except that one does not pray in it. As for an animal whose flesh is not lawful to eat, all of those parts are impure as well; the hide may still be put to use, but it is not lawful to pray in it. What is lawful and what is forbidden to eat of such animals is treated in the chapter on foods.

Whatever is purchased from the markets of the Muslims is treated as pure, so long as it is not known to come from a carcass; it retains that presumption of purity at the time of purchase. Meat sold in Muslim markets may be eaten even when the buyer has not verified how the animal was slaughtered, for in such matters the Muslims are to be trusted.

When a pure garment is struck by urine, semen, prostatic fluid, wine, an intoxicant, blood, pus, the blood of a wound, ordure, or the dung of an animal whose flesh or urine is unlawful — or when any of these reaches the body while it is wet — prayer in that garment is not permitted until it has been washed. There is no harm in wearing the garment or making use of it before washing, but prayer must wait until it is clean.

What is slight is treated differently. This includes:

- The blood of fleas and ticks
- Water that splashes up during ablution or ghusl from ground that impurity has touched
- A fly that alights on something impure and then alights on the garment
- Similar small and unnoticed matters

None of these cause harm so long as they do not accumulate to a gross degree. When they do become gross, the garment is washed.

Whoever has a wound or sores that flow continuously, and cannot stop the flow long enough to wash it from his body and garment, should guard against it as best he is able. Whatever overcomes him at the time of prayer does not harm him or invalidate his prayer; he prays in that condition, for Allah is most excusing of the servant whom He has afflicted.

As for the garments of idolaters, prayer in them is not permitted until they have been washed. Prayer in the garments of Muslims is permitted unless it is known that impurity is upon them.

If the garment's impurity is unknown, it is treated as pure, and prayer in it is valid. If a man prays in a garment believing it to be pure, then discovers after the prayer that impurity had been upon it, his prayer stands and nothing further is required of him. If he discovers it while still in the prayer, he removes the garment. Should he be wearing a pure garment beneath it that gives adequate covering, he continues his prayer from where he left off; but if the impure garment was his only covering, he removes it, puts on a pure garment, and begins the prayer again in that.

There is no harm in the sweat of a person in a state of janabah, nor in the sweat of a menstruating woman, nor in whatever moisture they leave behind — provided they are Muslims, for the Muslim is pure. Likewise, if a wet garment clings to such a body, that contact does not render the garment impure. The exception is when a smear of impurity is present on the body itself and adheres to the garment; in that case, the affected spot on the garment is washed.

By the same principle, if a garment carries impurity and the person wearing it sweats, or if his body — while wet — comes into contact with the place of that impurity, so that its moisture transfers to the skin, then that part of the body is washed. As for whatever reaches a garment or the body from a carcass or from impurity while completely dry, leaving nothing behind on the surface it touched, it causes no harm.

The urine of every animal whose flesh is lawful to eat is pure for the purposes of ablution — with one exception: an animal that feeds on filth, or has made a habit of doing so, such as chickens and their like. In that case, whatever its droppings touch is washed. If the animal is kept to clean fodder, its droppings cause no harm.

Rainwater mud is pure so long as impurity has not altered it. If something other than impurity mingles with it and changes its character, it remains pure. If impurity mingles with it and alters it, it becomes impure.

- If a person walking in wet impurity wipes his shoe on the ground until no moisture remains, the shoe is pure; if moisture remains, it is washed.
- If he walks through impurity barefoot and then walks across clean ground until the moisture is gone, no washing is required; if moisture remains, he washes his feet.

If impurity falls on the ground and the sun then draws out all its moisture and its odor, the ground has become pure and prayer upon it is valid.

Prayer is not performed on impure ground, nor in a graveyard. It may, however, be performed in sheep pens and in the resting-places of camels, provided no impurity is known to be present.

---

**The tooth-stick**

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "The tooth-stick is a purifier for the mouth and a means of the Lord's pleasure. Jibril did not come to me but that he charged me with the tooth-stick, until I feared it would be made obligatory upon the front of my mouth." And Ali said: "Three things were given to the Prophets: the tooth-stick, perfume, and wives." And Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali said: "The tooth-stick is half of ablution, and ablution is half of faith, and the tooth-stick drives away phlegm." The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Were it not that I would burden my community, I would have made the tooth-stick obligatory with every ablution."

Whoever is able to use it should not neglect it. He also said: "Use the tooth-stick across, and do not use it lengthwise." It was further held that rubbing with the thumb and the forefinger at the time of ablution — that is, using them in place of the tooth-stick — suffices as a substitute. He forbade using a tooth-stick of reed, of basil, or of pomegranate, and said that doing so stirs up the vein of leprosy.

---

**The dry ablution**

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوٓا۟ إِذَا قُمْتُمْ إِلَى ٱلصَّلَوٰةِ فَٱغْسِلُوا۟ وُجُوهَكُمْ وَأَيْدِيَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْمَرَافِقِ وَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِرُئُوسِكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُمْ إِلَى ٱلْكَعْبَيْنِ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُمْ جُنُبًۭا فَٱطَّهَّرُوا۟ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰٓ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَآءَ أَحَدٌۭ مِّنكُم مِّنَ ٱلْغَآئِطِ أَوْ لَمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا۟ مَآءًۭ فَتَيَمَّمُوا۟ صَعِيدًۭا طَيِّبًۭا فَٱمْسَحُوا۟ بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُم مِّنْهُ

Allah Almighty said: "يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا قُمْتُمْ إِلَى الصَّلَاةِ فَاغْسِلُوا وُجُوهَكُمْ" — "O you who believe, when you rise to pray, wash your faces," [al-Maidah 6:6] down to His word: "فَإِنْ لَمْ تَجِدُوا مَاءً فَتَيَمَّمُوا صَعِيدًا طَيِّبًا فَامْسَحُوا بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ مِنْهُ" — "and if you do not find water, then betake yourselves to clean earth and wipe your faces and your hands with it." [al-Maidah 6:6] From the People of the House it is related that whoever cannot find water — whether a traveler, or one who is ill and fears that washing would harm him or worsen his condition, or one who is simply unable to use it — should perform tayammum with clean earth, meaning pure dust. He places the inner surfaces of his palms upon it, shakes them off lightly, then wipes his face and the backs of his hands with them: the inner surface of his left palm wipes the top of the back of his right hand, and the inner surface of his right palm wipes the back of his left hand.

The extent of this wiping reaches to the same limit at which the hand of a thief is severed, because Allah Almighty said: فَامْسَحُوا بِوُجُوهِكُمْ وَأَيْدِيكُمْ مِنْهُ — "wipe your faces and your hands with it" [al-Ma'idah 6:6] — and He, the Exalted, also said: وَالسَّارِقُ وَالسَّارِقَةُ فَاقْطَعُوا أَيْدِيَهُمَا جَزَاءً بِمَا كَسَبَا — "As for the male thief and the female thief, cut off their hands as a recompense for what they have earned" [al-Ma'idah 5:38]. The limit of tayammum thus corresponds, by the plain sense of the Book of Allah, to the limit of cutting. That limit will be discussed in its proper place, God willing. The wiping begins from the fingertips.

Those who held this position maintained that a person performing tayammum should delay it until the end of the prayer's time. When that time arrives, he performs tayammum and prays — provided he does not break his state and does not find water. Should he find water and be capable of using it, he performs ablution. If he finds water and is able to purify himself but does not, his tayammum is annulled; and when the next prayer comes and he still lacks water or remains unable to use it, he performs tayammum again.

If he performs tayammum and then finds water before he has begun the prayer, he purifies himself with water and does not pray on the strength of his tayammum. If he has already entered the prayer and water then comes to hand, he does not break off; the prayer suffices him when this happens at the end of the time.

If he performs tayammum at the beginning of the time and prays, and then reaches water while enough of the prayer's time remains for him to purify himself and pray again, he purifies himself and prays.

Tayammum is valid only with dry, clean earth. Clay does not suffice, unless it is spread over something and left to dry, after which tayammum may be performed with it. If no clean earth is to be found, he shakes his garment, his felt, or his saddle-pad — whatever carries dust — and performs tayammum with that. A single strike is sufficient: he strikes the clean earth with both hands, once for the face and once for the hands.

Tayammum is not valid at home, with one exception: when a funeral is present and no water can be found in time. In that case, if he fears the funeral prayer will be missed, he performs tayammum and prays over the deceased. A traveler who has a small amount of water and fears that using it for purification will cost him his life may perform tayammum and preserve the water — he is not to destroy himself. Equally, if water exists nearby but reaching it would expose him to destruction, he is not obliged to go to it; he performs tayammum and prays. If water is available at a price and he has the means to pay it, he buys it, unless doing so would cause him ruinous hardship.

When water must be purchased, he is not required to pay beyond a fair price simply because the seller demands an extortionate sum — there is no obligation to meet a ruinous cost. A traveler who has no water with him and wishes to return to his household may do so; he performs tayammum and prays.

**Menstruation**

Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said:

﴿وَيَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْمَحِيضِ قُلْ هُوَ أَذًى فَاعْتَزِلُوا النِّسَاءَ فِي الْمَحِيضِ وَلَا تَقْرَبُوهُنَّ حَتَّى يَطْهُرْنَ فَإِذَا تَطَهَّرْنَ فَأْتُوهُنَّ مِنْ حَيْثُ أَمَرَكُمُ اللَّهُ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ التَّوَّابِينَ وَيُحِبُّ الْمُتَطَهِّرِينَ﴾

"And they ask you about menstruation; say, It is harm, so keep away from women during menstruation, and do not approach (الْمُبَاشَرَة) them until they are purified. And when they have purified themselves, then come to them as Allah has ordained, for Allah loves those who turn in repentance and loves those who purify themselves." [Al-Baqarah: 222]

This shows that during menstruation one is not in a state of purity. It has been related from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that he said: the menstruating woman is not made pure by water until after the menstruation has ceased.

From the Ahl al-Bayt it is related that the sign of purity for the menstruating woman is that she inserts a clean piece of cotton or cloth, and if it comes out white, she has seen the sign of purity. She then washes with water as she washes from janabah, and she performs the prayers that fall due at that time. She does not make up the prayers she missed during the days of her menstruation. As for the days of her menstruation, she makes up the fast but does not make up the prayer.

Her husband may approach her while she is menstruating if he wishes, though it is better that he keep himself from that. The root of mubasharah (الْمُبَاشَرَة) is the placing of one skin (اَلْبَشَرَة) upon another, and from this the word is derived; it is said that a man "approaches" his wife when their skin touches. But if he has intercourse with her while she is menstruating, he has sinned and transgressed what Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has commanded. He must seek Allah's forgiveness, repent to Him, and give charity for that act; and it is better that he not return to it.

When bleeding continues beyond the known duration of ordinary menstruation, it is istihada. The two bloods are distinguished by their character: menstrual blood is turbid, thick, and foul in odor, while the blood of istihada is thin, clear, and without odor. Once the menstrual bleeding stops, the mustahada purifies herself with water, prays, seeks forgiveness, and resumes her ordinary life — and her husband may approach her.

It has been related from the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (ص) that the mustahada should perform ghusl before every prayer, seeking thereby the reward of Allah, Mighty and Majestic; and Allah will record its merit for her if she is sincere in it. If a menstruating woman becomes pure and then notices a faint trace of blood that soon ceases, no ghusl is required of her, for that is treated as the blood of istihada. If, however, it is thick and resembles menstrual blood, she must wash on account of it. Likewise, if she sees blood while pregnant and it resembles menstruation, she and her husband must observe in her case all that is obligatory during menstruation. She washes when her postpartum bleeding has ended, just as she washes from janabah.

The menstruating woman and the one in a state of janabah may not recite the Quran, nor may they sit in the mosque. And if a menstruating woman becomes pure at the time of a prayer but delays the ghusl until that prayer's time has passed, she is obliged to make up that prayer.

## 3. The Pillar of Religion: Standing in Prayer

Allah Almighty has made prayer a decree of specified times. He said: "Prayer was for the believers a decree of specified times." [Al-Nisa: 103] He said: "Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer, and stand up devoutly before Allah." [Al-Baqarah: 238] He said: "Indeed, prayer prohibits immorality and wrongdoing, and the remembrance of Allah is greater." [Al-Ankabut: 45] And He said: "And seek help through patience and prayer, and indeed it is difficult except for the humbly submissive." [Al-Baqarah: 45]

> وَأْمُرْ أَهْلَكَ بِٱلصَّلَوٰةِ

The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "Guard yourselves, and know that the best of your deeds is prayer." He said: "Prayer is the offering of every pious person." He said: "Prayer is half of faith." He said: "Everything has a face, and the face of your religion is prayer." And he said: "Prayer is the pillar of religion and the mainstay of Islam."

He also said: Whoever perfects his ablution, prays well, pays the zakat on his wealth, restrains his anger, imprisons his tongue, extends his kindness to others, seeks forgiveness from his Lord, and gives sincere counsel to the people of his household has fulfilled the realities of faith, and the gates of Paradise stand open for him. He said: The devil remains in awe of the believer so long as he safeguards the five prayers; but when the believer neglects them, the devil grows bold against him and casts him into grave sins.

> إِنَّ ٱلْحَسَنَتِ يُذْهِبْنَ

It is narrated from Ali (ع) that he said: Prayer is the pillar of religion and the mainstay of Islam, and it is the first thing Allah Almighty examines of the deeds of the son of Adam. If it is sound, the rest of his deeds are examined; and if it is not sound, none of his deeds are looked at, and there is no portion in Islam for the one who abandons prayer. He said: The five prayers are an expiation for what falls between them, so long as the major sins are avoided. This is what Allah Almighty meant when He said: إِنَّ ٱلْحَسَنَتِ يُذْهِبْنَ — "Indeed, good deeds remove evil deeds; that is a reminder for those who remember." [Hud: 114]

He said: The likeness of one who does not complete his prayer is the likeness of a pregnant woman who, when her time of delivery draws near, miscarries — leaving her neither one who carries a child nor one who has a child.

He said: Two rakahs performed with composure in the latter part of the night are more precious than standing the whole night without composure.

And he said: Prayer is a balance; whoever gives it in full receives in full.

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: I know of nothing after knowledge — meaning knowledge of Allah and His friends — that is superior to prayer, zakat, fasting, Hajj, and jihad. He said: The nearest a servant comes to Allah is when he is in prayer. And he used to say: O seeker of knowledge, pray before you are no longer able, for there will come a time when you can neither pray by night nor by day. The likeness of prayer for the one who offers it is the likeness of a man admitted before a sultan: he remains silent and attentive until his need is fulfilled. So it is with the Muslim — when he enters into prayer, Allah continues to look upon him until he finishes.

He was asked about the words of Allah Almighty: "And those who carefully maintain their prayers." [Al-Maarij: 34] He said: This is the obligation. Whoever prays within its time, knowing its due, preferring nothing over it — Allah Almighty writes for him by it an immunity from the Fire and does not punish him. Whoever prays outside its time, not knowing its due, preferring other things over it — his affair rests with Allah Almighty: if He wills, He forgives him, and if He wills, He punishes him.

The Master said: The worshipper carries three signs. Mercy showers down upon him from the reaches of heaven to the crown of his head; angels enfold him from the place of his feet to the reaches of heaven; and a caller calls out: "O worshipper, if you knew Whom you address and Who watches you in this prayer, you would not turn away." It is narrated of Ali ibn al-Husayn (ع) that when he rose to pray his color would change and he would tremble. When he was asked about this, he said: "I am about to stand before a great King."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: Whoever safeguards the times of the five prayers and observes their limits will meet Allah on the Day of Resurrection with a covenant that admits him into Paradise. Whoever squanders their times and does not observe their limits will meet Allah with no such covenant: if He wills, He forgives him, and if He wills, He punishes him.

It was said to him: What of one who is in his prayer when a pressing need comes upon him urgently?

He said: First let him know that the fulfillment of the need lies in the hand of the One before Whom he prays.

The Master said: The most beloved of deeds to Allah is prayer, and it is the last counsel the prophets left behind. Nothing is more excellent than that a man should perform his ablution completely, then withdraw to a place where no companion can see him — while Allah looks upon him as he bows and prostrates. When the servant prostrates, Iblis cries: "Woe to me! This one obeyed while I disobeyed; this one prostrated while I refused." The Master said: The nearest a servant comes to Allah is in the moment of prostration. And he said: When the Muslim servant devotes himself fully to his prayer, Allah Almighty turns His face toward him and appoints an angel to gather the Quran from his mouth. He remains so for as long as the servant is turned toward his prayer; but when the servant turns away from it, Allah Almighty turns away from him and consigns him to the angel.

> وَلَهُ ٱلْحَمْدُ فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ

As for the times of prayer, Allah Almighty said: *"So exalt Allah when you reach the evening and when you reach the morning, and to Him is all praise in the heavens and the earth, and in the late afternoon and when you reach the time of noon."* [Al-Rum: 17–18] And He said: *"and after the Isha prayer."* [Al-Nur: 58] And the Almighty said: *"Establish prayer at the decline of the sun until the darkness of the night, and the Quran of Fajr; indeed, the Quran of Fajr is ever witnessed."* [Al-Isra: 78]

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: the decline of the sun is its departure from the zenith — that is the first time of Zuhr. The darkness of the night is its midpoint. Together these encompass four prayers, and the Quran of Fajr is the Fajr prayer.

On the words of Allah Almighty, *"So exalt Allah when you reach the evening"* [Al-Rum: 17], he said: that is the Maghrib and Isha prayers. *"And when you reach the morning"* [Al-Rum: 17] is the Fajr prayer. *"And in the late afternoon"* [Al-Rum: 18] is the Asr prayer. *"And when you reach the time of noon"* [Al-Rum: 18] is the Zuhr prayer.

These are the times of the prayers as they stand in the Book of Allah Almighty, stated in summary. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ then interpreted and clarified them, as Allah Almighty had commanded him to make plain what was revealed to him.

The Master said: Jibril came to him when the light of dawn broke across the eastern horizon and spread to the right and to the left. Dawn is of two kinds. The true dawn is this spreading light, and it marks the first time of the Fajr prayer — the moment at which food and everything else forbidden to the fasting person becomes forbidden to him. Before it comes the first dawn, called the wolf's tail: a thin light that rises from the place of sunrise and climbs upward without spreading across the horizon as the second dawn does.

The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: Jibril said to him, "Rise and pray Fajr," and he did. Jibril came again on the second day, after the light had spread and people could see one another, and said to him, "Rise and pray Fajr."

He came for Zuhr when the sun had passed its zenith and the shadow of everything had become equal to it — that is, after the decline. For Asr, when the shadow of everything had become twice its length. For Maghrib, when the disc had set — meaning when the sun had gone down — and he came at that same time the following day. For the later Isha, when the twilight had vanished. Jafar ibn Muhammad said that this is the redness that lingers on the western horizon after the setting of the sun. On the second night he came when a third of the night had passed.

The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said that Jibril then said to him: "The time is what lies between your prayer yesterday and your prayer today."

This is a well-attested report from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, set down here in brief. It took place at Makkah, for the report states that Jibril (ع) came to him there; the Messenger of Allah ﷺ later established at Madinah a Sunnah concerning the prayer times. Many reports on this have been narrated from the Imams of his household (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), and from many others among the general body of Muslims as well. Only what suffices within the scope of this book is mentioned here.

What has been narrated from the Imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ is that every prayer has two times, except the Maghrib, whose time is the setting of the sun and is not to be delayed beyond it. The most excellent times are their beginnings — except in the case of the later Isha, whose merit lies in delaying it, on account of the hardship this places on one who wishes to sleep. The one who waits for the prayer is himself in prayer, as has come from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "One of you prays the prayer, and what has passed him of its time is a graver loss to him than to be deprived of his family and his wealth."

Ali said: Render the rights of your Lord at their due moments; pray the prayer at the first of its times. He said: The first of the time is the most excellent, so hasten to good as much as you are able. He said: The first of the time is the pleasure of Allah, and the end of the time is the pardon of Allah, and pardon is only for a sin or a shortcoming. And Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) said: The first of the time is ever the more excellent, so hasten to the merit as much as you are able. He said: The first of the time is the time of Allah, and it is the better of the two. He said: The first of the time is the most excellent, and what follows it remains open until the end of the time.

It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad that he said: It is not for anyone to take the later of the two times as his time except for an infirmity or an excuse; the later time is only for one who is occupied, or forgets, or sleeps, for it was made for the sick and the one driven to it by necessity. He said: When the sun declines, the gates of heaven are opened, and I do not wish anyone to outstrip me in works, for I love that my scroll be the first scroll in which a righteous deed is written. And he said: How can one of you feel secure from the sudden calamities that may befall him if he neglects the prayer when its time has already entered?

The Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) are reported to have enjoined watching for the zawal — the moment the sun departs from the midpoint of the sky and begins its descent toward the west. Many instruments have been devised to determine this moment, and the soundest and most reliable works as follows.

A circle is drawn with a compass on a board or tile fixed in a place that receives direct sunlight. The surface must be perfectly level, with no edge higher than another. To test this, water is poured over it: if one side is low, the water runs toward it, and the surface is adjusted until it lies flat, then fastened in place. The circle is then drawn upon it. At the center — the point where the compass foot rested — a small, even nail is fixed, its height calculated so that the tip of its shadow falls precisely on the circle's line at the time of the sun.

Observation then begins at the start of the day. When the tip of the nail's shadow reaches the circle's line on the western side, that point is marked on the line. In the late afternoon, the same observation is made: where the shadow again touches the circle on the eastern side is likewise marked. The compass then measures the arc between the two points along the southern side, and its midpoint is marked; the same is done along the northern side. A straight edge is laid from the southern mark to the northern mark, passing through the center of the nail, and a line is drawn along it. That line runs directly opposite the midpoint of the sky, marking the moment of zawal.

When the shadow lies straight along that line, the sun stands at the midpoint of the sky. When the shadow begins to shift from it, the sun has declined — and that is the first moment of Zuhr, at which one prays the Sunnah and then the obligatory prayer.

Ali said: "The prayer of the decline is the prayer of the oft-returning." Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "At that hour the gates of heaven are opened, supplication is answered, the winds stir, and Allah looks upon His creation."

It is narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that when the sun declines, the time of both prayers — Zuhr and Asr — enters together. Rabia held the same view, maintaining that the time of Zuhr and Asr, whether for the traveler or the one at home, begins at the moment of decline. Ibn al-Mubarak expressed a position close to this, saying: "If a man were to rise when the shadow of everything equals its length after the decline and pray Zuhr, and another were to pray Asr at that same time, each would have prayed his prayer within its proper time." He was asked: "How can one time belong to two prayers, with neither travel nor excuse?" Ibn al-Mubarak replied: "Does this trouble you? It is Jibril who brought it. Had he brought a single time for three prayers, we would have observed it so for them."

According to the Ahl al-Bayt, the time of Asr begins once the worshipper has completed the Zuhr prayer — its obligatory portions and its Sunnah. The latest point they named for this is roughly two hours into the afternoon, placing Asr at the start of the ninth hour; practice follows accordingly, and it ought not to be delayed. Should delay occur through excuse or infirmity, the outer limit of its time is the yellowing of the sun. The time of Maghrib is the moment the sun passes below the western horizon; it has no other time, as already stated. The times of the later Isha and of Fajr are as previously set out.

The rising of the sun is known by the rise itself. Where a mountain or other obstruction stands before the point of sunrise, the sign that the sun has risen is the reddening of the western horizon, and the sign that it has set is the darkening of the eastern horizon. The night prayer follows the later Isha, and Witr follows the night prayer. No prayer is performed after Witr until dawn breaks, at which point one prays the two rakkahs of Fajr and then Fajr after them.

Supererogatory prayer after sunrise is forbidden until the sun has climbed, with one exception: a person who enters the mosque may pray two rakkahs before sitting. Similarly, no supererogatory prayer is offered after Asr until the sun sets, though an obligatory prayer missed at either of these times may be made up without harm. Prayer is also forbidden at the precise moment of the sun's rising and at its setting. When there is danger of an obligatory prayer being lost, one begins with it before any supererogatory prayer. The Zuhr prayer is delayed a little in intense heat — past the sun's decline — in search of some coolness, for those who pray in congregation at a mosque. The Friday prayer is prayed when the sun declines and must not be delayed.

Zuhr and Asr may be joined, as may Maghrib and Isha, at the end of the time belonging to the first of each pair. This joining is permitted while traveling, when there is need, and in residence at the mosques when there is a valid excuse — rain, wind, or darkness. The two prayers are joined with one adhan and two iqamahs: when the imam finishes the first prayer he remains in his place, the muezzin rises and gives the iqamah, and then the imam rises and leads the second.

When a person forgets a prayer and does not recall it until the time of a currently due prayer is nearly gone — and fears that starting with the missed prayer will cost him the present one — he begins with the present prayer. If he knows he can perform the missed prayer and still catch the present one in time, he begins with the missed prayer. When several prayers have been missed, he begins with as many of those as he can make up while still reaching the prayer whose time is current, and defers the remainder to afterward.

When a person forgets a prayer and does not recall it until he has already entered another, the ruling depends on which prayer was forgotten. If it was Zuhr, and he did not recall it until he had begun Asr, he forms the intention of Zuhr and then makes up Asr. In any other case, he completes the prayer he is already in and then makes up what he missed. Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the difference between these two cases. He said: because the Asr prayer has no prayer after it.

Whoever misses a supererogatory prayer may make it up if he wishes, and there is merit in doing so, though it is not obligatory. Whoever prays a prayer before its time has entered has not discharged it and must repeat it once the time has come.

Once the worshipper has attained the purity that prayer requires, what summons him to stand before his Lord is the call to prayer, and the place appointed for that standing is the mosque. The tradition has much to say about both.

### The Call to Prayer

Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about the origin of the call to prayer. He replied that the Messenger of Allah (ص) had been taught it on the night of al-Isra, and he recounted the hadith of al-Isra at length: how the angel called the adhan to the Messenger of Allah (ص), and how he led the Prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) in prayer.

He was then told that some people claimed the call to prayer had arisen from a vision seen by a man of the Ansar. At this he grew very angry and said, "إِنَّا لِلَّهِ وَإِنَّآ إِلَيْهِ رَجِعُونَ. To Allah we belong and to Him we shall return. They have set their hand to the very face of their religion and fixed it upon a vision that a man among the people saw, as though the Messenger of Allah (ص) had followed him in it." Yet Allah, Mighty and Majestic, had said to His Messenger: "Say: I follow only what is revealed to me."

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "There are three things which, if my ummah knew what reward lay in them, they would draw lots to attain them: the call to prayer, going early to the Friday prayer, and the first row." He was asked, "O Messenger of Allah, do you not fear that your nation will contend over this after you, even with swords, when they hear it?" He said, "Only the weak among them will stoop to contend over it."

> وَلَوْ تَرَىٰٓ إِذِ ٱلْمُجْرِمُونَ نَاكِسُوا۟ رُءُوسِهِمْ عِندَ رَبِّهِمْ

The Master said, "The muezzins will be raised on the Day of Resurrection as the longest-necked of people, calling out the testimony that there is no god but Allah." By the length of their necks on that day, he (ص) meant their yearning toward the mercy of their Lord and their security from His punishment — a state set in sharp contrast to the one Allah, Mighty and Majestic, described for the guilty: "And if you could see the criminals bowing their heads before their Lord" [al-Sajdah: 12]. The usage is well established in Arabic speech: when something a man detests befalls him, it is said that his head has fallen low.

Ali said, "I grieve over nothing except that I wish I had asked the Messenger of Allah (ص) for the adhan for al-Hasan and al-Husayn."

Narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, they directed that:

- The adhan be given with measured deliberation, and the iqamah briskly.
- Both be given in paired phrases, with the final testimony of the iqamah pronounced singly.
- The adhan be given with "حَيَّ عَلَى خَيْرِ الْعَمَلِ, Come to the best of deeds," and the iqamah likewise.

The phrase "Come to the best of deeds" formed part of the call to prayer in the time of the Messenger of Allah (ص), in the time of Abu Bakr, and through the early portion of Umar's caliphate. Umar then decided to remove it, reasoning that if the ignorant heard prayer declared the best of deeds, they would abandon jihad. This position has been transmitted by many among the general body of scholars. Yet Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has set out the merit of prayer in more than one place in His Book, and has set out the merit of jihad there as well. The possibility that jihad might suffer is no ground for cutting away what Allah commanded and what the Sunnah of His Messenger (ص) established. The ignorant ought instead to be taught what they do not know and brought to a clear understanding of it, so that their ignorance of a matter is never made the cause for its removal. Were that principle accepted, most of the obligations of the religion would fall away, for people are ignorant of the greater part of them and confused about them in general.

The scholars also transmitted that the muezzin, when giving the adhan, should stand facing the qiblah. When he reaches "حَيَّ عَلَى الصَّلَاةِ, Come to prayer," he turns his face to the right; and when he reaches "حَيَّ عَلَى الْفَلَاحِ, Come to success," he turns his face to the left, keeping his feet in place throughout. A traveler giving the adhan while mounted incurs no harm, but the iqamah must be given only while standing on the ground. The adhan should not be given except in a state of purity; if it is given without purity there is no harm, but the prayer itself may not be led except in purity.

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) heard the muezzin, he would repeat each phrase after him — until the muezzin reached "Come to prayer," "Come to success," and "Come to the best of deeds," at which point he would say: "لَا حَوْلَ وَلَا قُوَّةَ إِلَّا بِالله, There is no power and no strength except with Allah." He said that whoever utters these words seeking the pleasure of Allah has from Allah a covenant that the Fire shall never touch him.

When the muezzin announces that the prayer is ready, silence and rising for prayer become obligatory upon all who are present in the mosque. Speaking before that announcement incurs no harm. The muezzin must not interrupt the adhan with speech or anything else; if ignorance or necessity forces him to speak, he resumes the adhan from the point where he left off, and nothing further is required of him. The same rule applies to the iqamah. Both the adhan and the iqamah are to be given in every mosque in which prayer is performed.

Neither the adhan nor the iqamah is to be omitted. Even one who prays alone should give both. Abu Jafar ibn Muhammad said, "Whoever gives the adhan and the iqamah and then prays has two rows of angels praying behind him; and whoever gives the iqamah without the adhan and prays has one row of angels behind him." Giving both for every prayer is the better course. If someone gives only the iqamah, that suffices; and if someone prays with neither, the prayer need not be repeated, for the call of the town stands in their place. Women are not required to give either the adhan or the iqamah. If they do so, it is good and meritorious; if they rely on the call of the town, that is equally permitted.

Whoever forgets the adhan, the iqamah, or both, and realizes this only after entering the prayer, owes nothing further. The prayer continues and is not broken off, for the prayer itself is an obligation while the adhan is a Sunnah — and an obligatory prayer is not to be interrupted for a Sunnah whose place has already been fulfilled without it. If, before entering the prayer, a person is uncertain whether he gave the adhan or the iqamah and cannot recall either way, then giving them is good; if he proceeds to pray without them, nothing further is required of him.

The adhan must not be given before the prayer's time has arrived. Whoever gives it early must repeat it once the time enters; if he does not, nothing further is required of him, and his case is that of one who prays without the adhan. An exception was permitted for the Fajr prayer alone: the adhan may be given before dawn in order to rouse those who are sleeping, though the adhan at the actual time of dawn is better, and it is the practice the Messenger of Allah (ص) established. The earlier call carries a risk: one who is ignorant of the dawn's true time may pray upon hearing it before the time has entered, and such a prayer does not count. Upon the adhan at its proper time the imams call today, the muezzin greeting the imam and announcing the prayer to him after the adhan is given.

The adhan and iqamah may lawfully be given by a slave or by a boy who has not yet reached the age of dreams, and the prayer may be led in that manner. When a muezzin gives the adhan on behalf of a mosque and remains, that adhan and iqamah stand for every prayer performed in that mosque. Those present in the mosque when the adhan was given should not leave until they have prayed. Exceptions apply:

- One who leaves for a need, intending to return and knowing he will still reach the prayer.
- One who is without ablution and goes out to perform it, striving to return in time.

Even if he does not return in time, he may pray alone or join another congregation in another mosque, and no blame falls upon him.

The adhan may be given in the mosque before the imam arrives, even at the very beginning of the prayer time, and there is no harm in this. If the mosque's appointed imam then arrives, no one else should be put forward to lead the congregation — he is an imam installed by the Imam. The prayer waits until the end of the time; if he comes, all is well, and if he does not, the congregation prays without him. When the people of a locality dispute over who should give the adhan, the worthiest among them takes precedence; if they are equal in merit, then the one with the loudest voice. A blind man's adhan is permitted provided he follows the call of another who knows the time. There is no adhan or iqamah for a supererogatory prayer. A muezzin must not ascend a minaret so high that he looks down upon the private quarters of the people and sees into their homes.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "Whoever has a child born to him, let him give the adhan in the child's right ear and the iqamah in the left, and let him recite the Opening of the Book, the Verse of the Throne, the end of Surat al-Hashr, Surat al-Ikhlas, and the two surahs of refuge." He continued, "Whoever does this for his child, the child is guarded from the accursed devil and his licking." The licking refers to the devil touching the newborn's tongue — defiling it with his filth at the moment of birth. Performing these acts over the child is a protection against it.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) also said, "If the desert demons beset you, give the call to prayer." The ghilan are the saali — they are known as the enchanters of the jinn. His words "the desert demons beset you" mean that the road has led the traveler astray. The Messenger of Allah (ص) thus directed that whoever loses his way and cannot find the road should give the call to prayer, for it will guide him, God willing.

### On the Mosques

Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said: "The mosques of Allah are maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, and establish prayer, and give the zakat, and fear none but Allah; it may be that those will be among the rightly guided" [al-Tawbah: 18]. And He, Exalted, said: "And the places of worship are for Allah alone, so call not upon anyone alongside Allah" [al-Jinn: 18]. And He, Exalted, said: "The polytheists are but impurity, so let them not approach the Sacred Mosque after this year of theirs" [al-Tawbah: 28].

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "Do not set out on a journey to any mosque save three: the Sacred Mosque, my mosque, and the mosque of Jerusalem." He taught that prayer in the Sacred Mosque equals a hundred thousand prayers; prayer in the mosque of Medina, ten thousand; prayer in the mosque of Jerusalem, a thousand; prayer in the great mosque — meaning the mosque in which the Friday prayer is gathered in each region — a hundred; prayer in the mosque of one's tribe, twenty-five; prayer in the market mosque, twelve; and a man's prayer alone in his house, a single prayer. He also said that sitting in the mosque to await the prayer is itself an act of worship. He said, "Whoever takes the Quran as his companion and the mosque as his home, Allah builds for him a house in Paradise and raises him a degree below the middle station." And he said that the believer's place is his mosque, and his home is his hermitage.

He forbade the uncovering of the private parts in the mosque — that being what lies between the navel and the knee. He forbade the carrying out of prescribed punishments in the mosques: retaliation for killing, stoning, and the penalties for adultery, for false accusation, and for drinking. Matters of correction and discretionary discipline, however, may be addressed in the mosque without objection. He forbade:

- raising voices in the mosque
- drawing weapons in it
- buying and selling in it
- crying out for lost property in it
- sitting in it while in a state of janabah

It was narrated from Ali that he said, "There is no prayer for a neighbor of the mosque except in the mosque." He was asked who a neighbor of the mosque was, and he replied, "Whoever hears the call" — meaning the adhan. This, and Allah knows best, is by way of stern admonition and discipline. The prayer of one who lives near the mosque yet prays at home is defective, and he is bound to repeat it; the merit lies in praying in the mosque. It may also be that his saying "there is no prayer" means there is no prayer worthy of the name — that is, no prayer possessing the merit of one performed in the mosque. The multiplication of reward for praying in the mosque has already been addressed.

It was narrated from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that a man came to him and said, "O Messenger of Allah, I go out to my camels with my family, my children, and my servant-boys, and I give the adhan and the iqamah and lead them in prayer — are we a congregation, with the merit of one?" He said, "Yes." The man said, "Sometimes the servant-boys go off to graze the camels, so I give the adhan and the iqamah and lead my family and my children in prayer — are we a congregation?" He said, "Yes." The man said, "Sometimes the children go off tracking the traces of rain, so I give the adhan and the iqamah and pray with my family — are we a congregation?" He said, "Yes." The man said, "Sometimes the household is occupied with its labor, so I give the adhan and the iqamah and pray alone — am I a congregation?" The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "The believer alone is a congregation."

It has already been noted that whoever gives the adhan and the iqamah and then prays will have two rows of angels praying behind him. What is commended for the worshipper who intends to pray is that he pray in the great mosque, on account of the multiplied reward that has been transmitted concerning prayer there. If that is not possible, let him pray in the mosque of his tribe; if that too is beyond him, let him pray in congregation in a market or wherever a congregation may be formed. If even that cannot be managed, let him pray in his house and gather his family and children with him, for in that lies the merit of congregation and the reward of keeping his household constant in prayer. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said: "And enjoin the prayer upon your household, and be steadfast in it" [Taha: 132]. And if he cannot do that, let him give the adhan and the iqamah and pray alone — the merit of this, and how the reward diminishes degree by degree, has already been noted.

Ali said, "Waiting for the prayer after the prayer is a kind of frontier-guarding." It is also of the Sunnah, when sitting in the mosque, to face the qiblah — that is, when one is able to do so; if he turns aside from it, there is no harm. He said, "When afflictions descend, the people of the mosques are kept safe." And he said, "Reclining at ease in the mosques is the monasticism of the Arabs."
