The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "Keep your mosques clear of your buying and selling, your disputes, your quarrels, your weapons, and your madmen." He forbade the raising of voices in them and the carrying out of prescribed punishments. He directed that mosques be perfumed with incense every seven days, and that the means of purification be kept within them.

Whoever keeps the mosque free of his spittle will meet Allah on the Day of Resurrection with a smiling face, his record placed in his right hand. The mosque recoils from spittle as a morsel of flesh recoils in fire, writhing at it as a man writhes from the stroke of a cane. Spitting in the mosque is a sin; its expiation is to bury the spittle. Whoever spits in the mosque is to pray two rakahs.

Regarding what is forbidden within the mosque, the prohibition extends to whoever casts anything onto its wall, its floor, or its carpet. Should something impure come upon a man, he is to cover it with his garment, wipe it away with the underside of his sandal, or bury it so that it cannot be seen and will harm no one — and in doing so, he incurs nothing wrong.

Ali is also reported to have said: "Do not make the mosques a place for idle talk." He also related the account of a man from Yemen who entered the mosque of the Messenger of Allah having eaten garlic, its odor filling the air. The Messenger of Allah was troubled by it and said: "Whoever eats this plant, let him not draw near the mosque."

The teaching holds that upon entering the mosque one should recite: بِسْمِ اللَّهِ وَبِاللَّهِ، السَّلَامُ عَلَيْكَ أَيُّهَا النَّبِيُّ وَرَحْمَةُ اللَّهِ وَبَرَكَاتُهُ، السَّلَامُ عَلَيْنَا وَعَلَى عِبَادِ اللَّهِ الصَّالِحِينَ, and then greet the people of the mosque.

The Master said: "It is among the rights of the mosque that when you enter it, you pray two rakahs therein" — meaning during those times in which voluntary prayers are permitted, as has already been set forth.

Ali said that for one who does not know the precise direction, the qibla lies between east and west, and that the qibla faces the Sacred House. Allah, mighty and majestic, said: ﴿فَوَلِّ وَجْهَكَ شَطْرَ الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ وَحَيْثُ مَا كُنْتُمْ فَوَلُّوا وُجُوهَكُمْ شَطْرَهُ﴾ [البقرة: ١٤٤].

The Messenger of Allah said that one should pray only in clean, dry clothing, and that to pray without a barrier before oneself is a mark of coarseness. He said: "If one of you prays in an open field, let him set before him something like the back of a saddle, if he can find it."

It is related from Ali that he disliked a man's praying toward a sleeping person placed in front of him, or toward a woman's shoes, unless he first sets a barrier before him. He said: "If you pray toward a barrier, draw near to it, and let the space between you and it be no more than the measure of your prostration when you prostrate." The Messenger of Allah said: "When one of you prays, let him draw near to the barrier and not leave a gap between himself and the qibla, lest the devil take it as a passage." He also forbade the display of images in the place of prayer.

Regarding the mosque, or the room in a man's house set aside for prayer: if he does not turn to face the people but wishes only to make room and put the space to use, there is nothing wrong in that.

On the imamate, the Messenger of Allah said: "Set forward the best among you in your prayer, for the imam stands as the delegate of the people." It is related from Ali that the sick should not lead the sound in prayer — that being particular to the Messenger of Allah alone — and that one who is bound should not lead those who are free.

The Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have disliked the imamate of the following:

- the leper
- the one wasted by leprosy
- the child of fornication
- the insane
- the enslaved
- the blind

This applies unless such a person leads when there is genuine need of him and is not appointed as a standing imam. Similarly, they did not hold that a bedouin of full standing, a man who has undergone a prescribed punishment, or a madman should lead others — though they permitted such men to lead one another, each leading those in a like condition.

They did not consider prayer permissible behind those who bear enmity toward the family of Muhammad and openly declare their hatred of them, except in a state of dissimulation — and refuge from such affliction is sought in Allah.

Whoever is tried with this circumstance prays behind such a man only when he fears for himself. He forms the intention for himself alone and does not follow the man as imam. He bows when the man bows, prostrates when he prostrates, and departs when he departs — intending all of it for himself, as though he prayed alone with no imam.

If the one who has performed tayammum is more deserving of the imamate, he may lead those who have performed ablution. Whoever leads a people in prayer and afterward learns that he was not in a state of purity must repeat the prayer — and so must they. Umar once led the people, and when he had finished he said: "O people, Umar has led you while he was in a state of major impurity." They asked, "What then do you hold?" He said: "In my view, I must repeat, but there is no repetition upon you." Ali, however, said: "Rather, the repetition is upon you and upon them, for the people bow and prostrate by their imam."

The most deserving of the imamate is the most excellent — the best in recitation, the most learned. If they are equal in that, the eldest in years takes precedence. When a man leads two others, he stands before them and they form a row behind him. When he leads one, he places him at his right; and if another comes and joins the prayer, the newcomer steps forward and stands behind the imam. There is no harm in the imam leading with a light barrier between himself and the one behind him.

The scholars disliked an imam lengthening the prayer when leading a congregation, for among them may be the weak and those with pressing needs; the one who prays alone does better to lengthen it. As for a woman leading other women, she does not stand ahead of them but rather in their midst, in a single row with them.

If the imam errs or slips — passing from one surah to another, omitting something of the Quran, or moving to a different passage — there is no harm in that, and those praying behind him are not obliged to correct him so long as he continues to recite. But if he falters, halting and falling silent, they prompt him, opening for him from behind the place where he stumbled; once he resumes, they fall silent again.

Even if two men stand in prayer, each intending to lead the other, and they complete their prayers before learning of this, their prayers remain valid — for the imam's prayer is not spoiled if no one stands behind him, or if all who stood behind him turn away. But if each had intended to follow the other, their prayers are void, since each held himself to be a follower praying behind an imam when there was no imam over him.

The scholars disliked an imam offering voluntary prayer, after the obligatory one, in the very spot where he had led the congregation, and preferred that he move to another place. They recommended that, when he turns to depart, he turn to his right, and that he neither break through the rows nor walk between them. They also recommended that he remain seated in his place for a time after the salutation, calling upon Allah, so that anyone who missed part of the prayer may complete what he missed.

The Messenger of Allah said of the congregation and the rows: "My community will not cease to be shielded from affliction until they deal in usury, bribery appears among them, they sever the ties of kinship, they abandon prayer in congregation, and they no longer make for this House. When they do that, they will be left without regard." He also said that whoever prays the five prayers in congregation is to be thought well of in every good and his testimony accepted, so long as no ill is known of him.

Abu Jafar ibn Ali was asked whether prayer in congregation is obligatory. He said: "The prayer is obligatory, but the congregation is not obligatory in every prayer — it is Sunnah. Yet whoever forsakes it out of aversion, turning from it and from the community of the believers with neither excuse nor cause, in disdain of it, has no prayer."

It is related of Ali that he once stood the entire night in prayer, and when dawn came he slept and missed the Fajr in the mosque. The Messenger of Allah, not seeing him, came to the house of Fatimah after finishing the prayer and asked: "Daughter, what has kept your cousin from witnessing the prayer with us?" She told him what had happened, and he said: "What he missed of the Fajr in congregation is better than a whole night's standing." He then roused Ali and said as much to him directly. He also said: "O Ali, the earth cries out to Allah from the sleeper upon it at the rising of the sun."

Ali said: "That I should pray the Isha and the Fajr in congregation is dearer to me than that I should spend all that lies between them in worship."

And he said: "Beneath the shade of the Throne, on a Day when there is no shade but its shade, is a man who goes out from his house, having perfected his purification, walking to one of the houses of Allah to fulfill therein one of the obligations of Allah, and who perishes upon that path."

The Messenger of Allah said: "The best of the rows in prayer is the first, and the best of the rows in funeral prayer is the last, and the best of the rows for women is the rearmost." Ali held that the best position in the first row is at the imam's right, while Jafar ibn Muhammad held that it is whatever lies nearest to the imam. The Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have commanded that rows be completed and gaps closed.

A worshipper who stands in a row and sees a gap in the row before him may walk forward to fill it. One who feels crowded in his row may step back to a gap in the row behind him and stand there. There is no harm in a man praying alone behind the row when he finds no place within it, and the Ahl al-Bayt permitted one who cannot find a place in the first row to advance and stand level with the imam when no other option is open to him.

The minimum space between two rows is the measure that allows the worshipper to move into prostration, complete it, and rise — for when those in a row cannot reach the place of prostration, their prayer is not valid. Forming rows between the pillars was also permitted when nothing else can be found.

The Messenger of Allah said: "Straighten your rows, align your shoulders, and do not differ, lest your hearts differ and the devil slip in among you as the little ones of the hadhaf slip among the flock."

The hadhaf are small black sheep. He likened their entering among the large sheep to the devil's entering between the rows when he finds a breach in them, corrupting the prayer of its people and occupying their hearts against it. May Allah grant refuge from the devil and his works.

On the rows of prayer and their foundation, the Messenger of Allah said: "Deeds are only by intentions, and to each man belongs only what he intended." Ali said: "Whoever prays with his intention set upon the prayer alone, nothing else entering into it, has it accepted from him when it is sound in what is outward and what is inward."

Jafar ibn Muhammad held that the worshipper should not enter prayer until he has formed the intention, settled his heart upon it, and determined which prayer it is — whether an obligation, a Sunnah, or a voluntary act. If it is an obligation, he must intend which of the five it is, holding this firmly as he enters.

He opens the prayer with the takbir, raising his hands with palms open and fingers together and extended, his fingertips level with his ears, then letting his hands fall along his sides and thighs. He does not lay one hand upon the other, nor hold them beneath his breast as some of the common people do, claiming this practice to derive from the words of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَانْحَرْ﴾ [الكوثر: ٢].

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about this. He said: "The sacrificing is after the prayer of the Eid, and the sacrifice within the prayer is uprightness in standing — that the worshipper hold his spine and his chest erect."

He further said that the Messenger of Allah forbade laying one hand upon the other in prayer, declaring it the practice of the Jews, and commanded instead that the hands be left free. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali also said that a woman, when she stands in prayer, places her hands upon her breast to cover it.

The worshipper then turns and says: وَجَّهْتُ وَجْهِيَ لِلَّهِ نَاظِرِ السَّمَوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ حَنِيفًا مُسْلِمًا وَمَا أَنَا مِنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ، إِنَّ صَلَاتِي وَنُسُكِي وَمَحْيَايَ وَمَمَاتِي لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ لَا شَرِيكَ لَهُ، وَبِذَلِكَ أُمِرْتُ وَأَنَا مِنَ الْمُسْلِمِينَ، عَلَى مِلَّةِ إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَدِينِ مُحَمَّدٍ وَوِلَايَةِ عَلِيِّ بْنِ أَبِي طَالِبٍ أَمِيرِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ، وَأَبْرَأُ إِلَى اللَّهِ مِنْ أَعْدَائِهِ الظَّالِمِينَ، أَعُوذُ بِاللَّهِ السَّمِيعِ الْعَلِيمِ مِنَ النَّارِ وَمِنَ الشَّيْطَانِ الرَّجِيمِ.

He utters this quietly and with reverence — not aloud where the recitation is silent, nor silently where it is aloud.

He then opens the recitation with بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, saying it quietly where the recitation is quiet and aloud where it is aloud. He must not do otherwise, for it is a verse of the Book of Allah, mighty and majestic. Some have claimed it does not belong to the Book of Allah, yet it stands established at the head of every surah, revealed in that position by Jibril to the Messenger of Allah. The Ahl al-Bayt, together with many among the generality, agreed upon this and transmitted that it was so revealed — that the completion of one surah and the beginning of the next were known by the descent of بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Dissimulation is my religion and the religion of my fathers, save in three matters: the drinking of intoxicants, the wiping over the leather socks, and the abandoning of the recitation of بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ."

His meaning is that he himself neither drinks an intoxicant, nor wipes over the socks, nor omits the recitation of بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ at the head of every surah — whether in prayer or otherwise — and that he neither enjoins its omission nor permits it to anyone, whether in a state of dissimulation or not.

After بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, the worshipper recites the Opening of the Book and a surah, opening that surah likewise with بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ. This applies to the two rakahs of Fajr, the first two of Dhuhr, Asr, and Maghrib, and the last rakah of Isha. In the remaining rakahs, no particular surah is fixed and no Quranic recitation is required.

It is nevertheless meritorious for a person praying alone to lengthen the recitation as much as he is able, though he may shorten it if he wishes. One who leads a congregation, on the other hand, is commended to lighten the prayer — calibrated to what he knows those behind him can bear — for among them there may be the weak and the one pressed by need. The Messenger of Allah rebuked Muadh ibn Jabal on precisely this point: when word reached him that Muadh had led a people in prayer and made it long upon them, he said to him: "Do not be one who drives people away; pray a prayer suited to the weakest of your companions." The Messenger of Allah would lighten the prayer — while keeping it complete — when he led the people, yet would lengthen it when he prayed alone, until his feet swelled from the long hours of standing.

"O Messenger of Allah, you are bringing hardship upon yourself, though Allah has forgiven you your past and future sins." He answered, "Should I not then be a thankful servant?"

On the measure of standing in prayer, what has been transmitted from the Ahl al-Bayt assigns the longest standing to the Fajr prayer. After it comes Isha, which is somewhat shorter than Fajr, then Dhuhr, then Asr, and finally Maghrib, whose standing is the briefest of all. No fixed measure is laid down for this.

In an obligatory prayer, two surahs after the Opening of the Book may not be joined within a single rakah, though in voluntary prayers there is no harm in doing so. If a worshipper has begun one surah and wishes to move to another, he may do so only before he has passed the midpoint of the surah he started — with three exceptions: Surah al-Jumuah on Friday, Surah al-Ikhlas, and Surah al-Kafirun. Once he has begun any of these three, he must not break off from it.

The worshipper recites aloud in the Fajr prayer and in the first two rakahs of Maghrib and Isha, whether he prays alone or leads a congregation, and recites quietly in all the rest. In the remaining rakahs — the final two of Dhuhr, Asr, and Isha, and the last rakah of Maghrib — he recites the Opening of the Book alone, in a low voice.

After completing the Opening of the Book, the worshipper does not say "Amin," as the common people do, nor does the one praying behind the imam say it, for the Messenger of Allah ﷺ forbade it. He and the Imams of his descendants said, "My nation will remain upon the right path of its religion so long as they do not raise a clamor with Amin in their prayer" — and the rest of the hadith follows. Jafar ibn Muhammad said that only the Jews used to do this, and that it was the ignorant among the people who imitated them in it.

The worshipper turns to his prayer with his heart and empties his mind for it as fully as he is able. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said, "When the servant turns toward his prayer, Allah, mighty and majestic, turns toward him with His face and His mercy; and when he turns away from it, Allah turns away from him and entrusts him to His angels." Jafar ibn Muhammad said, "Of the worshipper's prayer only that is raised up which he offered with presence of heart, and this may be a half of it, or a quarter, or less, or more." Allah, mighty and majestic, completes what falls short through the voluntary prayers. All of this is the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt.

They command the worshipper to be humble in his prayer, to magnify the majesty of Allah within it, and to make his own soul feel that majesty. When Ali ibn al-Husayn was about to enter into prayer, his color would change and he would tremble, so that those around him would look at him and ask what had befallen him. He would say, "I am about to stand before a great King."

They forbade turning about in prayer and letting the gaze wander, and commanded the worshipper to keep his eyes on the place of his prostration and to hold his limbs still as much as he was able. Should he grow weary, there is no harm in shifting his weight from one foot to the other, or in attending to some need of his body or his clothing, even reaching for it with his hands — yet if he can leave all of this undone and remain still, that is better.

It was narrated of Ali ibn al-Husayn that during prayer his robe slipped from him and he did not reach out to restore it. When he was asked about this afterward, he said, "Do you not know before Whom I was standing, and with Whom I was in intimate speech? By Allah, what I was engaged in occupied me from what you saw."

Where a garment falls from a worshipper during prayer and lays bare some part of his nakedness, he must hasten to restore it as far as he can and cover what has been exposed. The nakedness of a man is what lies between the navel and the knee, while the whole body of a woman is nakedness, save her face and her hands. As for Ali ibn al-Husayn, whose robe fell and who did not replace it, his robe at that time was an outer covering worn over the garment beneath it, and when it fell he was content with what remained.

The worshipper should not spread his feet apart when he stands in prayer, unless there is some infirmity in him. Rather, he stands in a balanced posture, leaving between his feet only a moderate space, enough to stand at ease.

There is no harm in a worshipper's repeating a verse or a word of the Quran for the sake of admonition and the stirring of awe. If he weeps out of fear of Allah, this does not sever his prayer; on the contrary, there is merit and reward in it. He should not, however, force weeping upon himself. Such tears are proper only when they arise unbidden and he cannot hold them back. If he contrives them, his prayer is severed, and the act carries ostentation — a quality that is detestable.

It is held that a worshipper should not break off his recitation and fall silent in order to attend to something he hears nearby — a groan, a cry, or anything of that kind. He does not interrupt his prayer for such a cause.

Beyond that, however, a report has come down: Ibn al-Kawwa entered the mosque in Kufa while the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, was leading the people in prayer and reciting. From behind him, Ibn al-Kawwa raised his voice with the verse, *"And it has already been revealed to you and to those before you: if you associate others with Allah, your work will surely come to nothing, and you will surely be among the losers."* The Commander of the Faithful (ع) fell silent, then resumed his recitation. Ibn al-Kawwa repeated the verse, and he fell silent again. This happened three times. Then the Commander of the Faithful recited, *"So be patient; indeed the promise of Allah is true, and let not those who have no certainty of faith unsettle you."*

It is further commanded that the Quran be pondered as the reciter recites it, whether within his prayer or outside of it.

The scholars held that the minimum by which a prayer is valid — its irreducible core — consists of five elements: opening with the takbir of consecration, standing for those able to do so, bowing, prostrating, and sitting for the tashahhud. Whoever performs these five pillars, while forgetting or being unaware of what lies beyond them, has nonetheless completed a valid prayer. One who remains ignorant of the remaining pillars, however, is obliged to learn them and observe them without delay or deferral, exerting every effort in learning and committing them to memory.

For one who does know the pillars, it is not permissible to omit any of them deliberately — not the recitation within the prayer, not the remaining takbirs, not the tashahhud, not the tasbih, and not the concluding salutation. No obligatory or established pillar of the prayer may be abandoned by choice. Whoever does so deliberately has no prayer, and is obliged to repeat whatever he abandoned in this manner.

Whoever prays behind an imam and follows him should make the takbir when the imam makes the takbir, and remain silent when the imam recites aloud. When the imam says, "Allah hears the one who praises Him," the follower says, "Our Lord, to You be praise," and continues making the takbir throughout the prayer in the same manner — quietly, without raising his voice. He performs the tashahhud when the imam performs it and gives the salutation when the imam gives it.

If, however, the imam is someone whose state the follower does not approve of — one he would not genuinely take as his leader — yet he prays behind him out of taqiyyah, then he opens the prayer for himself, intending that he prays alone and does not take that imam as his leader. He recites, bows, and prostrates alongside the imam without intending to follow him, and he departs when the imam departs. Throughout, he conducts himself exactly as he would were he praying alone.

Whether leading the prayer or not, the worshipper raises his hands when he intends to bow, and raises them again when he lifts his head from the bow — just as he raises them at the takbir of consecration — doing so with the takbir of bowing and with the words "Allah hears the one who praises Him." In the bow itself, he grasps his knees with his palms and spreads his fingers across them, and he levels his back until his shoulders and spine are flat and even, for the Messenger of Allah ﷺ held this position so steadily that, were water poured upon his back, it would settle there and not run off. He neither raises his head nor lets it drop, but holds it level with his back. In the bowing position he says, "Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent, and praise be to Him," three times, at an unhurried and composed pace — whether he leads, prays alone, or follows an imam. In prostration he says, "Glory be to my Lord, the Most High, and exalted is He," likewise three times, without haste, his heart attending throughout to the magnifying of Allah, mighty and majestic.

When praying alone, with no congregation to lead, a worshipper may add to the magnifying of Allah whatever further glorification he wishes, and there is merit in doing so. It has been narrated of a company of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that before the three glorifications in the bowing they would say, "O Allah, to You have I bowed, to You have I humbled myself, in You have I believed, and to You have I submitted; and You are my Lord. My hearing and my sight, my hair and my skin, my flesh and my blood, my sinews and my bones, and all that my two feet bear up, have humbled themselves to You — not disdaining, nor arrogant, nor weary of Your worship, but in reverence toward You and lowliness before Your obedience. Blessed is Allah, Lord of the worlds." And in the prostration they would say, "To You have I prostrated, to You have I humbled myself, in You have I believed, and to You have I submitted; and You are my Lord. My face has prostrated to the One who created it and fashioned it, who brought forth its hearing and its sight and shaped it in due proportion; blessed is Allah, the best of creators. O Allah, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins and Your mercy is greater than my wrongdoings, so forgive me and have mercy upon me, O Most Merciful of the merciful. There is no god but You, truly and in truth.

"I have prostrated to You, O Lord, in worship and in servitude; I prostrate to none but You and I worship none, O Lord, but You alone." They would also increase the glorifications of the bowing and the prostration, making them seven, and there is merit in this for one who prays alone. As for the one who leads a congregation, it has already been noted that the sunnah in his case is to keep it light while still completing it properly.

They said that when the worshipper raises his head from the bowing and says, "Allah hears the one who praises Him," he should say quietly, upon his tongue, "Our Lord, to You be all praise" — whether he is an imam or not — and then stand upright and erect. If he is an imam he proceeds directly into prostration, and the same applies if he prays alone. If he prays alone and adds more at this point, that is good, for it has come down from them that here too there is an addition, as there is in the bowing and the prostration, and that they would say thereafter: "Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds, Possessor of might and majesty, of greatness, of grandeur, and of power. O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, restore me, raise me, and provide for me, for I stand in need of whatever good You send down to me." He then makes the takbir of the prostration and goes down to prostrate.

When the worshipper goes down to prostrate, he places the inner surfaces of his palms on the ground before his knees, then kneels and prostrates. He holds his elbows somewhat away from his sides, extends his neck, and touches his forehead and nose to the ground. His forearms do not rest on the earth but are held up from it. He draws his fingers together, their tips level with his ears yet not touching them, and presses through the inner surfaces of his palms against the ground, not pulling them back into his sleeves.

> لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا

When he raises his head from the first prostration, he sits until every joint has settled back into place. He places his hands on his thighs, spreads his left leg beneath his right thigh without sitting on it, and, if he is able, sets his right foot upright on its toe; if not, he lays it on its side. He then performs the second prostration and sits in this same manner for the tashahhud — both the first and the last. Between the two prostrations he says, "O Allah, forgive me and have mercy upon me." If the prayer is prolonged and he prays alone, he says, "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, restore me, raise me, guide me, pardon me, grant me well-being, and provide for me, for I stand in need of whatever good You send down to me."

When he rises from the first rakah, he rises directly from the prostration without first lowering himself to sit. Nothing of the Quran is recited during bowing or prostration.

He concludes the prayer with two salutations, turning his face first to the right and then to the left, and with each he says, "Peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings." If he prays in congregation, these salutations are directed to those around him; if he prays alone, they are directed to the recording angels.

When the prayer is complete and he intends to leave, he turns away to his right. If he is the imam, he waits until the congregation has turned to their voluntary prayers before departing, and then he passes straight through the rows by an opening rather than walking between them. He does not walk in front of anyone still engaged in prayer.

## 4. Supplication and the Many Forms of Prayer

On the tashahhud in prayer, on supplication, and on what is longed for when calling upon Allah, the teaching begins with the word of Allah Himself.

> وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ٱدْعُونِىٓ أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْۚ إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِى سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ

﴿وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِي سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ﴾ — "And your Lord said: Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled" [Ghafir: 60].

God, blessed and exalted, said: ﴿وَإِذَا سَأَلَكَ عِبَادِي عَنِّي فَإِنِّي قَرِيبٌ أُجِيبُ دَعْوَةَ الدَّاعِ إِذَا دَعَانِ فَلْيَسْتَجِيبُوا لِي وَلْيُؤْمِنُوا بِي لَعَلَّهُمْ يَرْشُدُونَ﴾ — "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be rightly guided" [al-Baqarah: 186].

It is narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Whoever sits after the second rakah, remembering Allah the Almighty and calling upon Him, Allah honors him. Good deeds are written for him, evil deeds are wiped away from him, and he is raised in degrees, and an angel is charged over him who says to him, Increase in honor, until he departs." He (ص) also said: "By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, a man's supplication after the Fajr prayer until sunrise is more effective in securing his needs than the one who travels the earth trading with his wealth."

He (ص) said: "Shall I not direct you to a weapon by which Allah will fortify you against your enemy and by which He will make your provision easy?" They said, "Yes, O Messenger of Allah." He said: "You call upon your Lord by night and by day, for the weapon of the believer is supplication."

He (ص) also said: "The most beloved glorification to Allah is the tasbih of talk, سُبْحَة الحديث." It was said, "O Messenger of Allah, and what is the tasbih of talk?" He said: "People are plunged in the world, its vanities and its amusements, and a man seizes that very moment and calls upon Allah the Almighty, and remembers Him, and glorifies Him."

The Prophet (ص) said: "Allah the Almighty has not opened for a servant a door of supplication except that He has opened for him the door of response. He has not opened for him a door of seeking forgiveness except that He has opened for him the door of pardon. He has not opened for him a door of gratitude except that He has opened for him the door of increase. And He has not opened for him a door of deed except that He has opened for him the door of acceptance."

The Book of Allah bears witness to this. Allah, blessed be His name, says: ﴿ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ﴾, "Call upon Me; I will answer you" [Ghafir: 60]. And He said: ﴿ثُمَّ يَسْتَغْفِرِ اللَّهَ يَجِدِ اللَّهَ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا﴾, "Then seeks the forgiveness of Allah, he will find Allah Forgiving, Merciful" [al-Nisa: 110]. And He said: ﴿لَئِن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ﴾, "If you are grateful, I will surely increase you" [Ibrahim: 7]. And He said: ﴿فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُ﴾, "So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it" [al-Zalzalah: 7]. And He said, "Who is it that will lend Allah a goodly loan?"

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Supplication turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained." As he spoke, he clasped his fingers together, palm against palm — thumb against thumb, little finger against little finger — as though demonstrating the point by hand. An affliction descends toward a servant from the heaven, yet the servant calls upon Allah and asks Him for well-being, and the supplication ascends. The two meet and contend with one another until the Day of Resurrection.

He (ص) also said: "Whoever looks upon one stricken with affliction and says, *Praise be to Allah who has kept me sound from what He has tried you with, and has favored me over you and over much of His creation with great favor* — it becomes a right upon Allah that He not strike him with that affliction."

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: "Whoever supplicates, let him send prayers upon Muhammad in his supplication, for when the servant supplicates, his supplication is not raised to the heaven until he sends prayers upon the Prophet and upon the people of his house." The Prophet (ص) said: "Place me at the beginning of the supplication, and in its middle, and at its end." And he (ص) said: "Four are made intercessors in the heavens and the earth: Paradise, the Fire, the pure-eyed maidens, and an angel at my head in my grave when I have died. When the servant says, O Allah, I ask You for Paradise, Paradise says, O Allah, make him among my dwellers. When he says, O Allah, deliver me from the Fire, the Fire says, O Allah, deliver him from me. When he says, O Allah, wed me to the pure-eyed maidens, the pure-eyed maidens say, O Allah, wed him to us. And when he says, O Allah, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, the angel at my head says to me, O Muhammad, so-and-so has sent prayers upon you, and Allah says, O Allah, send prayers upon him as he has sent prayers upon Me, so I say, O Allah, send prayers upon him as he has sent prayers upon me."

He (ص) also said that the supplication of the believer is answered so long as he is not hasty. It was asked, "And what is haste, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "He supplicates but sees no trace of his supplication being answered, and so he says, I called upon Allah, and Allah has not answered me."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that Allah the Almighty grants the believing servant whatever He wills of what is asked, and defers whatever He wills of it, according to what is better for that servant — and Allah the Almighty is truer to His word than any who makes a promise. ﴿وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ﴾, "And your Lord said: Call upon Me; I will answer you" [Ghafir: 60]. He, exalted be His praise, undertakes the reckoning of His believing servant on the Day of Resurrection, and at that reckoning there appear before the servant good deeds he never performed. He is asked, "Do you recognize these good deeds?" He says, "No." He is told, "You called upon Allah on such a day in such a matter, and He answered you and deferred that answer until this day, for it was better for you." Then, when the believer sees on that Day the greatness of the reward his supplication has earned, he says, "O Lord, would that You had hastened nothing of my supplication for me, but had deferred it all to this day."

Jafar ibn Muhammad then said that Allah raises up, by supplication, the matter He already knows His servant will supplicate for, and answers it. Were it not for Allah's granting that supplication to the servant, an affliction would have struck him and uprooted him from the face of the earth. There is no Muslim who calls upon Allah but that He either answers him, or turns away from him — by means of that supplication — an affliction greater than what he asked for, or stores the reward for him until a day and hour when it will be more beneficial, so long as he does not supplicate for wrongdoing or the severing of kinship.

He (ع) said: "Supplication turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained, so multiply supplication, for it is the key to every good and the success of every need. What is with Allah is not attained except by supplication, and there is no door that is knocked upon much but that it is soon opened to the one who knocks."

A man asked Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) about the saying of the Messenger of Allah (ص) concerning the black seed — al-shuniz. He replied, "Yes, the Messenger of Allah (ص) said of it, *In it is a cure for every disease save death.* But shall I direct you to that for which the Messenger of Allah (ص) gave you no cure?" The man asked, "And what is it?" He said: "Supplication, for it turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained." At the words "though it has been firmly ordained," Abu Jafar ibn Ali clasped his fingers together before him. Then he said: "There is nothing more beloved to Allah the Almighty than that He be asked, and nothing turns back the decree except supplication."

He was then asked about the word of Allah the Almighty, ﴿فَإِذَا فَرَغْتَ فَانصَبْ وَإِلَى رَبِّكَ فَارْغَب﴾, "So when you have finished, then labor, and to your Lord direct your longing" [al-Sharh: 7–8]. He said: "That is supplication after the obligatory prayer. Beware of forsaking it, for its excellence over all other supplication is like the excellence of the obligatory prayer over the voluntary." He also said: "Supplication comes before the prayer, within the prayer, and after the prayer." And he said: "Supplication after the obligatory prayer is more excellent than the voluntary prayer."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Supplication is worship," and he recited the word of Allah the Almighty: ﴿ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِي سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ﴾, "Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled" [Ghafir: 60]. Then he said: "It, by Allah, is worship."

When he prayed the two rakʿahs of Fajr, he would call upon God with this supplication:

"I have taken hold of the firmest handhold of Allah, which has no breaking, and I have clung to the strong rope of Allah. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of the devils of mankind and jinn, all together. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of the corrupt among the Arabs and the non-Arabs. Allah is sufficient for me. I rely upon Allah. I lean my back upon Allah. I seek my need from Allah. There is no might nor power except in Allah. O Allah, make for me a light in my heart, a light in my hearing, a light in my sight, a light in my tongue, a light in my hair, a light in my skin, a light in my flesh, a light in my blood, a light in my bone, a light in my sinews, a light before me, a light behind me, a light on my right, a light on my left, a light above me, a light beneath me, and a light in my grave. O Allah, make my light great, and abundant, and full of joy, and a blessing, and a delight."

He then recites five verses from the end of Sūrah Āl ʿImrān, from "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth" to His word, "You do not break the appointment." He then says, three times: "Glory be to the Lord of the morning, the Cleaver of the dawn, who made the night and the day a repose, and the sun and the moon a reckoning." He then says: "O Allah, make the first of this day of mine righteousness, and its middle prosperity, and its last success. O Allah, whoever wakes with his need and his request directed to a creature among Your creation, my need and my request are to You alone, who has no partner." He then recites the Verse of the Throne and the two suras of refuge, and afterward says, one hundred times: "Glory be to my Lord the Magnificent, and by His praise. I seek the forgiveness of Allah and turn to Him in repentance." If this is missed, it is to be made up.

In the first tashahhud, the worshipper says: "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and all the beautiful names belong to Allah. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, alone, having no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger (ص), and the Imams from the people of his house, the righteous." Then he rises.

When he sits in the final tashahhud — the one in which he gives the salutation and from which he departs — he says: "In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and all the beautiful names belong to Allah. The greetings belong to Allah, and the pure prayers, the good, the untainted, the tender, the ample, the morning-going, the flowing, belong to Allah. Whatever is good and pure and increasing and honored and exalted and refined belongs to Allah. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, alone, having no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger, the excellent Messenger and the excellent Guardian, and that Paradise is true, and the Fire is true, and the Resurrection is true, and that the Hour is coming, no doubt in it, and that Allah will raise up those who are in the graves. O Allah, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and bless and have mercy upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You sent prayers and mercy and blessing upon Ibrahim and upon the family of Ibrahim in all the worlds; indeed You are Praiseworthy, Glorious."

One whose parents are Muslim adds: "And forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place not in our hearts any rancor toward those who believe; indeed You are Kind, Merciful." After that, he supplicates with whatever he wishes of supplication for this world and the hereafter, then sends prayers upon the Prophet and upon his family, and says: "Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings. Peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah."

The whole of the tashahhud is said under the breath, uttered with the tongue in a slight murmur. Then the salutation is given: the imam announces it aloud, while one praying behind an imam lowers his voice. Raising the voice a little in the latter case causes no harm. One praying alone may lower his voice or announce it, as he wishes.

When rising from the first tashahhud, the worshipper leans upon his hands and says quietly, "O Allah, by Your power I rise and I sit," and then pronounces the takbir for standing.

Once the worshipper has given the salutation at the close of the obligatory prayer, he turns the backs of his hands upon his thighs and raises them — and this is the more excellent course — placing the inner surfaces of his palms toward his face. He then recites:

"O Allah, I draw near to You through You, and through Muhammad Your Prophet, and Ali Your friend, and Fatima al-Zahra the daughter of Your Prophet, and al-Hasan and al-Husayn, and Ali ibn al-Husayn, and Muhammad ibn Ali, and Jafar ibn Muhammad" —

and he names the Imams one by one down to the Imam of the Age. Then he says:

"O Allah, I draw near to You through them and turn toward You by them, so make me through them honored in Your sight in this world and the hereafter, and among those brought near, and make certainty firm in my heart, and increase me in guidance and light."

After that, he supplicates with whatever he wishes.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "When you rise to the prayer, or enter the mosque intending the prayer, say: In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and from Allah, and to Allah, and as Allah wills, and there is no might nor power except in Allah. O Allah, make me among Your visitors and among those who frequent Your mosques. Open for me, O Lord, the door of Your forgiveness, and close against me the doors of Your disobedience. Praise be to Allah who has made me among those who commune with Him. O Allah, turn toward me with Your Face; exalted is Your praise and blessed is Your name. Then begin the prayer."

Ali (ع) said: "Whoever would be pleased to be measured with a full measure that falls not short, let him say, when he has turned away from his prayer: Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Might, above what they describe; and peace be upon the messengers; and praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds." And he said: "I heard the Messenger of Allah (ص) say: Whoever recites after every prescribed prayer, 'Say: He is Allah, One,' one hundred times, will cross the Sirat on the Day of Resurrection, and on his right will be eight cubits and on his left eight cubits, and Gabriel will take hold of his waistband while he gazes into the Fire to his right and to his left; and whoever he sees within it, among those he knows who entered by a sin other than shirk, he takes him by the hand and enters him into Paradise by his intercession."

This — and Allah knows best — is a report given in summary. It is particular, not general. The intercession it describes does not extend to all people, such that anyone who performs this recitation would automatically have earned what he (ص) said. Intercession belongs only to the one whom Allah the Almighty has appointed for it: "and they possess no power of intercession except one who has taken with the Most Merciful a covenant."

Ali said: "Whoever sits in the place where he prayed the Fajr prayer, after completing it, and recites within that place *Say: He is Allah, One* eleven times before the sun rises — no sin will follow him that day, though Satan strive hard against him." He also said: "The Messenger of Allah (ص) said to me, O Ali, recite the Verse of the Throne after every prayer, for none guards it faithfully but a prophet, a truthful one, or a martyr."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Whoever glorifies with the tasbih of Fatima (ع) before he folds his legs from the obligatory prayer, after he has given the salutation, is forgiven; and he begins with the takbir."

The tasbih of Fatima is: "Allah is the Greatest, and Glory be to Allah, and Praise be to Allah" — thirty-three times each, sealed with "There is no god but Allah," bringing the total to one hundred. The Messenger of Allah (ص) taught this tasbih to Fatima when she came to him asking for a servant. He said: "O Fatima, this is better for you than a servant, and a servant, and a servant, and than all that is in the world. You say it after every prescribed prayer, and one hundred good deeds are written for you, and the good deed is tenfold its like, so that becomes a thousand."

And whoever says one hundred times: "Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Allah, and I seek the forgiveness of Allah, and there is no god but Allah, and Allah is the Greatest, and as Allah wills, and there is no might nor power except in Allah; Allah is sufficient for me, I have taken refuge in Allah, I have relied upon Allah."

None knows what reward such a person holds with God — whose name be exalted — save God alone. The least of it is ten thousand good deeds, for God multiplies a single good deed tenfold, as He has said.

It is recommended that whoever rises from the prayer, on leaving it, utter the takbir three times and then say: "There is no god but God, alone, with no partner; to Him belongs the kingdom and to Him belongs praise; in His hand is all good, and He is able to do all things. There is no god but God alone; He fulfilled His promise, gave victory to His servant, and defeated the confederates alone; to Him belongs the kingdom and to Him belongs praise. Praise be to God, Lord of the Worlds. There is no god but God, and God is greatest; glory be to God, and praise be to God." This is said ten times.

Ali used to say after every prescribed prayer: "Then by Your light You guided, so to You belongs praise; Your forbearance is great, so You pardoned, and to You belongs praise; You stretched out Your hands and gave, so to You belongs praise. Our Lord, Your face is the most generous of faces, Your station the best of stations, and Your gift the most beneficial and the most pleasant of gifts. You are obeyed, our Lord, and You give thanks; You are disobeyed, our Lord, and You forgive. You answer the supplication of the distressed, heal the sick, and deliver from affliction. Our Lord, You accept repentance and forgive sins for whom You will; none can requite Your favor, and no speaker can number Your due."

Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Because this is a remembrance of God as He deserves to be remembered a thousandfold, whoever says it will have a thousand good deeds."

After the prayer, it is right and fitting to say: "O God, for You I have prayed, to You I have called, and in You I have hoped; so I ask You to place in my prayer and my supplication a blessing by which You expiate my sins, whiten my face, honor my standing, and lift my burden from me. O God, put down my burden, and make what is with You better for me. Praise be to God, who has discharged from me a prayer that was ordained upon me and upon the believers as a timed obligation."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "The least that will suffice you in supplication after the obligatory prayer is to say: O God, I ask You for every good that Your knowledge encompasses, and I seek refuge in You from every evil that Your knowledge encompasses. O God, I ask You for well-being in all my affairs, and I seek refuge in You from the disgrace of this world and the punishment of the Hereafter."

### The desirable forms of supplication

Supplication has no appointed time. Whoever wishes to pray may do so, asking God for his needs and calling upon Him for whatever he desires, so long as he does not call for something wrong or for the severing of kinship. It is fitting for one who would supplicate to set a righteous deed before his supplication, to repent to God of his sins, to clear himself of any claims standing between him and others of God's creation, and to make his deed and his intention sincerely for God. The Messenger of God (ص) is reported to have said: "One who supplicates without action is like one who draws a bow without a string." And Abu Dharr (رضي الله عنه) said: "Righteous conduct is sufficient accompaniment for supplication, as salt is sufficient seasoning for food."

Whoever would supplicate should begin by praising God, his Lord, and extolling Him as He deserves, and by blessing Muhammad, His prophet, and his family. Then let him make his petition, blessing the prophet and his family again in the middle of his supplication and at its end. The Messenger of God (ص) once heard a man supplicating before he had praised God, and said: "This man has been hasty with his Lord." And when the Messenger himself wished to supplicate, he would begin by praising God, then say:

"O God, there is none who can withhold what You give, and none who can give what You withhold; none who can constrain what You expand, and none who can expand what You constrain; none who can bring forward what You defer, and none who can defer what You bring forward. O God, You are the Forbearing, so You are never rash; You are the Bountiful, so You are never niggardly; You are the Mighty, so You are never abased; You are the Impregnable, so You are never assailed; You are the Possessor of Majesty and Honor; and there is no power and no strength save in God, the Most High, the Almighty."

The Master also said: "Do not make me like the rider's cup." The rider fills his cup and sets it aside; when he has finished with his gear and his errand, he looks — if he needs to drink, he drinks; if he needs to make ablution, he makes ablution; otherwise he pours the water out. "Place me at the beginning of the supplication, and in the middle of the supplication, and at the end of the supplication." His meaning was that the blessing upon him and his family should not stand at the end of the supplication alone, but at its beginning, its middle, and its end alike.

When God sent down the verse commanding the believers to bless the Prophet, the people said: "O Messenger of God, you have taught us how to greet you; how, then, are we to bless you?" He said: "Say: O God, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You blessed Ibrahim and the family of Ibrahim; You are Praiseworthy, Glorious."

> فَمَا ٱسْتَكَانُوا۟ لِرَبِّهِمْ وَمَا يَتَضَرَّعُونَ

Ali was asked about the saying of God, ﴾فَمَا اسْتَكَانُوا لِرَبِّهِمْ وَمَا يَتَضَرَّعُونَ﴿, "They did not humble themselves to their Lord, nor did they supplicate" (al-Muminun: 76). He said: "They were not lowly in their supplication to God, nor were they sincere in it."

Supplications are more than any book can number or contain, yet some are recorded here — those whose acceptance may be hoped for, as they have come down from the Ahl al-Bayt through reports and through encouragement to the practice. Among these is what was related from the Messenger of God (ص): "There are ninety-nine names; whoever numbers them enters Paradise." The tradition has been widely narrated, and scholars have differed over what those names are, finding themselves at sea concerning them. Sufyan al-Thawri set them down in his compendium, naming names other than those handed down from the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) — names at variance with what the Imams transmitted. Ali ibn al-Husayn (ع) was asked about them. He described their excellence and the acceptance of the supplication of whoever prays by them, and he magnified their standing in words too extensive to relate here. He then took a covenant from whoever learned them from him: that the learner would teach them only to one he trusted, and would not invoke God by them for any wrong purpose or for the severing of kinship. He said that they are in the Book of God, whose mention is exalted.

The Opening of the Book contains five names: By God, O Lord, O Most Merciful, O Ever Compassionate, O King. Surah al-Baqarah contains twenty-six: O Encompassing, O All-Powerful, O All-Knowing, O Wise, O Ever-Relenting, O Patient, O Vast, O Originator, O Hearing, O Sufficient, O Knowing, O Kind, O Grateful, O God, O One, O Forgiving, O Forbearing, O Withholder, O Expander, O Living, O Self-Subsisting, O Most High, O Great, O Rich, O Guardian, O Praiseworthy. Surah Al Imran contains four: O Ever-Standing, O Bestower, O Swift in Reckoning, O Aware. Surah al-Nisa contains five: O Watchful, O Witness, O Pardoning, O Nourisher, O Trustee. Surah al-Anam contains five: O Fashioner, O Subduer, O Able, O Subtle, O Ruler. Surah al-Araf contains two: O Giver of Life, O Giver of Death. Surah al-Anfal contains two: O Best of Protectors, O Best of Helpers. Surah Hud contains seven: O Preserver, O Near, O Answerer, O Strong, O Glorious, O Loving, O Doer of what He wills. Surah al-Ra'd contains two: O Great, O Most Exalted. Surah Ibrahim contains one: O Ever-Giving. Surah al-Hujurat contains one: O Ever-Creating. Surah al-Hajj contains one: O Raiser of the dead. Surah al-Anbiya contains two: O Truthful, O Inheritor. Surah al-Muminun contains one: O Generous. Surah al-Nur contains one: O Guide. Surah Saba contains one: O Opener. Surah al-Mumin contains four: O Forgiver, O Accepter of repentance, O Severe in punishment, O Possessor of Bounty. Surah al-Dhariyat contains three: O Provider, O Possessor of Strength, O Firm. Surah al-Tur contains one: O Ever-Kind. Surah Iqtarabat contains one: O Omnipotent. Surah al-Rahman contains three: O Everlasting, O Possessor of Majesty and Honor.

Surah al-Hadid contains four: O First, O Last, O Manifest, O Hidden. Surah al-Hashr contains ten: O Holy, O Peace, O Granter of Security, O Overseer, O Mighty, O Compeller, O Supreme, O Creator, O Maker, O Fashioner. Surah al-Buruj contains two: O Beginner, O Restorer. Surah al-Ikhlas contains two: O One, O Eternal Refuge.

These are the names of God — exalted is His mention — as they appear in His Book: ninety-nine names, and with "By God" a hundred. By them, when God is called upon He answers, and when He is asked He gives. Among the believers, those who prayed by these names with sincerity and certainty have known the blessing of answered supplication; their accounts are attested and their excellence handed down.

> إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ سَيَجْعَلُ لَهُمُ ٱلرَّحْمَنُ وُدًّۭا

The Messenger of God (ص) said to Ali: "O Ali, shall I not teach you words which, were your sins upon you like the foam of the sea, God would put them away from you? Say: O God, fix for me affection in the breasts of the believers, and make for me with You a covenant" — or, "make for me with You affection." God then sent down: ﴾إِنَّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ سَيَجْعَلُ لَهُمُ الرَّحْمَنُ وُدًّا﴿ — "Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds, the All-Merciful will appoint for them affection" (Maryam: 96). This verse was among the supplications recorded in his regard.

Among the supplications of Ali was one regarded as the supreme supplication of the Imams, reserved for weighty matters. He would pray:

"O God, I seek from You the grace to thank You well; I ask of You the gift of a tongue unloosed in proclaiming Your praise; and I desire from You to be set right in what earns Your increase. I seek refuge in You from that which calls down the visitation of other than You and brings on the sudden strokes that befall the like of me.

"O God, You began with me by the most abundant of gifts and singled me out for them above much of Your creation, so that, without my asking, there fell to me from Your apportioning the most fortunate share and the fullest lot, when You made me among the people of Your knowledge and those who hold fast to the rope of Your oneness — before the passing of ages, the elapsing of times, and the running of the eras, before the creation of male and female, the multiplying of generations, and the succession of descendants.

"Then You created me in the finest form and the most complete image, and made me hearing, seeing, speaking, understanding, discerning. Then You set my frame straight and completed my faculties, and did not deprive me of any of my limbs, but fortified them against afflictions by Your power and kept them from the stillness of incapacity by Your governance.

"As You have taken it upon Yourself, O my God, to repel maladies from my body by my health and to guard my weakness, so bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy and as they are worthy of You."

Remove from my soul the hindrance of sloth and the discouragement of delay; release it from the grip of weariness and loose from it the fetters of listlessness; rouse it to bear the burdens with which You have charged it, and set it upon the bounds of Your sacred limits.

O God, loose my tongue with the abundance of Your remembrance, the goodness of thanking You, and the repeating of Your favors; make it flourish with the recitation of Your Book at every hour of night and day; make it prefer truth in hope of its benefit and forsake falsehood in fear of its harm; restrain it from sharing in the backbiting of the backbiters and from chewing over the honor of the believers; and silence it from the stabbing of slanderers and the perjury of every contemptible swearer.

O God, spread out my hand in giving — freely and without stint — in spending in Your way; a hand that seeks Your mercy for those who hope in You, not inclining, in Your displeasure, toward hoarding, nor preferring, when Your good pleasure is within reach, riches over open-handedness.

O God, when I have drawn down the treasures of Your abundant storehouses and prayed for the rain of Your gifts in mercy, do not turn my two hands away disappointed of the grant of Your bestowal; rather bind their rising toward You to the descent of Your aid and the swiftness of Your answer, and hold them back from reaching for what the hands of those who tamper with Your sanctities and transgress Your command have long grasped after.

Let my eyes pour down, O God, with flowing tears and streaming weeping out of fear of You; lower them from whatever You have forbidden them to seek; make them humble before the trinkets of this world and its flowers that might otherwise adorn them; and guard them, O God, from the drowsiness of slumber and the sweetness of sleep when the heedless lie down upon their beds.

Bar my hearing, O God, from lending ear to whatever displeases You of the speech of those who err, and make it carry to my heart all that draws me near to You and wins Your good pleasure over me: holding lawful what You have made lawful, holding forbidden what You have made forbidden, keeping good conduct at Your doors, and longing for the voluntary acts by which one hastens toward Your good pleasure. Empty my heart, O God, of every breach in which I was heedless of Your command; turn it back from whatever earns Your displeasure; and pour into it a full measure of Your love, so that that love may drive it toward the nearness of intimate discourse with You and impel it to long for what awaits it with You — of all that You have prepared for Your friends in the abode of Your honor. Call it to account for whatever has slipped away of the wreckage of this world, so weighty in the eyes of its people. Set my feet firm before You, O God, in the standing of devotion for Your intimate discourse, and in knocking at the door of Your answer — a door opened only to one who turns his face toward Your face — and aid me through them to recite Your Book at every hour of night and day, and grant them the strength of Your increase as I journey to You upon them through the pitch-dark of the far-off nights, that You may fulfill Your promise.

Leave in me no limb but that I have made it ready for its task, no aim but that I have accomplished it, no rising resolve but that I have made it firm, no thread I mean to twist upon right guidance but that I have made it fast, and no craving of greed but that I have crushed it. O God, whatever failing has held me back from attaining a good benefit hoped for from none but You, and whatever visiting misfortune is lifted only by humble entreaty drawing near to You, bring me to it by Your bounty and Your generosity; for You do whatever You will and decree whatever You wish, and You are the most merciful of the merciful. So deal with me in all my states as befits You, by Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, the truthful, upon whom be greetings and peace all together, in well-being, O Lord of the Worlds.

He would say in his supplication: O Allah, You are more worthy of what befits You of Your pardon than I am of what befits me of Your punishment. O Allah, honor Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy of them and they are worthy of You. Do not discipline me with Your punishment, do not scheme against me with Your cunning, and do not lead me astray from where I do not know. O Allah, whoever is bettered by Your grace prospers, and whoever errs by his own sin perishes. No benefactor can dispense with Your support and Your aid, and no wrongdoer overcomes You, nor does any affair pass beyond Your power. So do with me what is worthy of You, for You are worthy of every good.

After that, he would pray for whatever he wished.

Among his supplications was this:

O Lord, how many an enemy has drawn against me the sword of his aggression and honed for me the edge of his blade, sharpened the point of his resolve against me, and mixed for me his deadly poisons. His watchful eye never slept over me, and he resolved to visit me with harm and to make me drink its bitterness. Seeing my weakness before such heavy blows, and my helplessness to take revenge upon the one who set out to war against me, and seeing that I stood alone among the many who bore me enmity, You reinforced me with Your victory over him and strengthened me. You scattered his gathering after it had assembled, and raised my standing above his, and turned back upon him the arrows he had aimed at me. You choked him with his own rage and struck him with his own dart, so that he was thrown back upon himself after his arrogance, and humbled and brought low after his overbearing. Servile and captive he lay in the snare of his own rope, that very snare in which he had felt sure he would see me on the day of his triumph. To You be praise, as You are worthy of it, powerful and never overcome, forbearing and never hasty.

This is a supplication that Ali taught to al-Husayn. He described its excellence in such terms that Allah, mighty and majestic, has made the reward binding upon whoever supplicates with it — a merit too long to recount in full. He bound al-Husayn by a covenant not to teach it to anyone save his family, his Shia, and his loyal ones, and al-Husayn pledged himself to that.

The Master said to him: When you wish to ask Allah, mighty and majestic, for any need, or to call upon Him in any matter of this world or the hereafter, then say:

بِسْمِ اللهِ الرَّحْمٰنِ الرَّحِيمِ

Glory be to Allah, praise be to Allah, there is no god but Allah, Allah is the greatest, and there is no power nor strength except by Allah. Glory be to Allah in the depths of the night and at the ends of the day. Glory be to Allah in the morning and in the evening. Glory be to Allah when you enter upon evening and when you enter upon morning. To Him is praise in the heavens and the earth, and in the late afternoon, and when you reach midday. He brings the living out of the dead and brings the dead out of the living, and He revives the earth after its death, and thus shall you be brought forth. Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Might, above what they ascribe to Him. Peace be upon the messengers, and praise be to Allah, the Lord of the worlds. Glory be to the Owner of dominion and sovereignty. Glory be to the Owner of might, greatness, and compelling majesty. Glory be to the Living who does not die. Glory be to the King, the Most Holy. Glory be to the Everlasting, the Abiding. Glory be to the Living, the Self-Sustaining. Glory be to the High, the Most High. Exalted and sanctified is He, all-glorious and all-holy, our Lord and the Lord of the angels and the Spirit. Glory be to our Lord the Most High, exalted and sanctified is He.

O Allah, I have entered upon evening and upon morning within Your favor and Your well-being, so complete Your favor and Your well-being upon me, and grant me to render You Your thanks. O Allah, by Your light I was guided, by Your grace I seek help, and within Your favor I have entered evening and morning. O Allah, I call You to witness, and You suffice as a witness, and I call to witness Your angels, the bearers of Your throne, and all Your creation, that You are Allah, there is no god but You alone, having no partner, and that Muhammad is Your servant and Your messenger, may Allah bless him and his family. O Allah, record for me this testimony with You until I meet it on the Day of Resurrection, while You are pleased with me, for You are powerful over all things.

This prayer continues:

O Allah, to You be praise — a praise before which the sky lowers its shelter, and by which the earth and all upon it glorify You. O Allah, to You be praise — a praise whose beginning ascends and whose end is never exhausted. O Allah, to You be praise: everlasting, abiding, without end. To You be praise upon me, within me, and with me, and with my child, whether I sleep or wake, and when I have died and passed away and You alone remain, O my Master. O Allah, to You be praise with all the praises that are Yours, for all the blessings that are Yours. O Allah, to You be praise upon every vein at rest and every vein that throbs. To You be praise upon every morsel and every drink, every grasp of the hand, and upon every place where a hair grows. O Allah, to You be praise for Your forbearance after Your knowledge, and to You be praise for Your pardon after Your power. To You be praise, the Sender of praise. To You be praise, the Inheritor of praise. To You be praise, the Originator of praise. To You be praise, the Bestower of praise. To You be praise, the Ancient of praise — Raiser of ranks, Sender down of blessings from above the seven heavens, who brings out of the darknesses into the light, who changes evil deeds into good deeds and makes good deeds into ranks. O Allah, to You be praise — Forgiver of sin, Accepter of repentance, Severe in punishment, Lord of bounty. There is no god but He; to Him is the return.

Then he says: There is no god but Allah alone, having no partner. His is the dominion and His is the praise. He gives life and gives death, and He is Living and does not die. In His hand is all good, and He is powerful over all things — ten times.

Then he says: I seek forgiveness of Allah, other than whom there is no god, the Living, the Self-Sustaining, for all my sins, and I repent to Him — ten times.

Then he says: O Allah, O Most Gracious, O Most Merciful — ten times.

Then he says: O Compassionate, O Bestower — ten times.

Then he says: O Originator of the heavens and the earth, O Owner of majesty and honor — ten times.

Then he says: In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful — ten times.

Then he says: Amin, Amin — ten times.

Then he states his need and prays upon the Prophet and upon his family.

Ali ibn al-Husayn would supplicate in the heart of the night, after every eye had grown still. His supplication ran: My God, the stars of Your heavens have set, the eyes of Your creation have slept, and the voices of Your servants have fallen silent. The kings of Banu Umayyah have shut their doors upon themselves, set their guards to circle them, and veiled themselves from any who would ask them for a need or seek from them a benefit. But You, my God, are Living, Self-Sustaining. Neither drowsiness nor sleep takes hold of You, and nothing turns You away from anything else. The doors of Your heavens stand open to whoever calls upon You, and Your treasuries are not withheld from whoever asks of You — they are freely given. My God, You are the Generous who turns away no believing petitioner who asks of You, and veils Yourself from none among them who seeks You. No — by Your might — their needs are not intercepted short of You, nor are they fulfilled by any other than You.

"O Allah, You see me standing here, and You know my inmost secret. You look upon what is in my heart and upon what would set me right in my hereafter and my world. So come to me in this with Your bounty and Your generosity, my God. You see me awaiting death, and it is the appointed meeting and the standing before You. It has spoiled for me my food and my drink, choked my throat, driven sleep from my pillow and my rest, and kept me from repose. My God, how shall he sleep who fears the sudden coming of the angel of death in the watches of the night and the watches of the day? Rather, how shall the wise man sleep when the angel of death does not sleep in his vigil over him, seeking to seize his soul in the morning, in the depths of night, and in whatever hour it may be?"

At these words he would weep, and would sob until his family and his loyal ones took fright at his weeping. They would come to him and find that he had pressed his cheek to the dust, saying: "My Lord, I ask You for rest and repose at the hour of death, and for the passage to the abode of settled rest."

Among the supplications of Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad is this:

"O Allah, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, and have mercy on my lowliness before You, my humble entreaty to You, my estrangement from people, and my intimacy with You. O my God, do not disgrace me, for You know me well, and do not chastise me, for You are able over me. O Allah, I seek refuge in You from abasement in life, from an evil sight at death, from an ill return in the grave, and from remorse on the Day of Resurrection. O Allah, I ask You for a life of contentment, a death of ease, and a return that is honorable — neither disgracing nor shaming. O Allah, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins, and Your mercy is more to be hoped for by me than my own deeds, so forgive me and have mercy on me. O Allah, to You be praise if I obey You, and Yours is the proof against me if I disobey You. No good deed of mine or of any other is ever lost with You, and there is no excuse for me or for any other in wrongdoing. O Allah, help me in my religion through my worldly life, and in my hereafter through my piety. O Allah, guard me in what I am absent from, and do not entrust me to myself in what I am present for. O You whom forgiveness does not diminish and whom sins do not harm — bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, forgive me what does not harm You, and grant me what does not lessen You."

"O Allah, I ask You for ampleness in provision, for ease, forgiveness, mercy, health, piety, pardon, well-being, sufficiency, contentment, moderation, righteousness, gratitude, and success in all my affairs — for the hereafter and for this world, by the ease of all things and not by their hardship, O Generous One. My Lord, extend that same favor to my child, my family, my kin, and my brothers among the believing men and women and the Muslim men and women, the living of them and the dead, whom You have loved and drawn near, and to those whom I have begotten and those who begot me. O Allah, I ask You for patience in obeying You and patience away from disobeying You, for contentment with Your decree, for truthfulness in every place, for the realities of faith, and for gratitude for Your blessings that cannot be numbered. O Allah, bless — You and Your angels — Muhammad Your prophet and all the family of Muhammad."

---

What speech and action are permissible within the prayer, and what are not, is the subject of what follows.

What cuts off the prayer, according to what has been transmitted from the Ahl al-Bayt, falls into four categories:

- Spoken words not addressed to God
- Breaking of ablution
- Turning wholly away from the Qiblah
- Gross physical action

Supplication, glorification, the takbir, the recitation of the Quran, and whatever a servant addresses to his Lord within the prayer are another matter entirely. Though they are speech, they are not the speech that cuts off the prayer. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said, concerning whatever a man addresses to Allah in prayer, that it is speech which does not cut off the prayer.

On the question of greetings: whoever is greeted with peace while he is in prayer and returns that greeting inwardly or by a slight gesture does not thereby break his prayer. A gesture, or a word said within oneself, is not counted among the things that cut off the prayer. Even so, leaving all of that aside and turning wholly to the prayer is better. If the one greeted waits until he has finished his prayer and returns the greeting after giving the closing salaam — should he wish to do so — that is better still.

One who, during prayer, witnesses something requiring correction, feels alarm, or fears missing something is permitted to say "Glory be to Allah" — either intending it as recitation, since the phrase belongs to the Quran, or intending it as glorification, since glorification is permitted within the prayer. When the matter carries no great harm, however, turning back to the prayer and letting it pass is the better course. Gesturing toward someone the worshipper wishes to address falls under the same ruling: if he points without turning his face from the Qiblah, there is no harm in it, though leaving it and returning wholly to the prayer is better, wherever he finds good in doing so.

Laughter within the prayer cuts it off; a smile does not, though it is disliked. Blowing within the prayer is forbidden — as when a worshipper blows upon his place of prostration because he dislikes what he sees there. If what lies there is something that may not be prostrated upon, he may remove it with his hand without harm. If something is in his place of prayer, he should move it aside; if it is on the ground, he should push it beneath his feet; and if he takes it away with a garment, that is better still.

The worshipper must not spit toward his face or to his right. He should spit to his left, without turning his face from the Qiblah.

The cracking of the fingers within the prayer is likewise forbidden. This occurs when a worshipper bends his fingers deliberately until they crack. It is a form of idle play and heedlessness, and it cuts the prayer off, since the one who does it has no need of it. If, however, the fingers crack as a result of some movement he did not intend, or from something he could not avoid, there is no harm in that.

A worshipper who is seized by an itch he cannot endure, and which distracts him from his prayer, is permitted to scratch. He is likewise permitted to kill a snake, a scorpion, or any fearsome and harmful creature he encounters during the prayer, and to kill a louse, a flea, or a gnat found on his body or garment while he prays. Deliberately toying with the hand upon the beard or anywhere else during the prayer is forbidden, though if such movement occurs without intent, no harm attaches to it. Reciting from a written copy of the Quran during the prayer is permitted. Stretching and yawning in the prayer are forbidden, though whoever is overtaken by either without intending it does not thereby interrupt his prayer. A sneeze, too, does not cut the prayer off — though the worshipper should soften it where he is able, cover his mouth with his hand, praise Allah, and invoke blessings upon Muhammad and his family.

Wiping dust or any other substance from the forehead during prostration, while still in the prayer, is forbidden. Once the worshipper has turned from the prayer, however, he may wipe it away — and he should not leave it. Setting the hand upon the hip during the prayer — the posture known as tawarruk — is likewise forbidden. A man is also forbidden to pray with his face veiled, except in cases of necessity or ailment.

As for the garments worn in prayer, the Messenger of God (ص) said: "Let every one of you fear God in his garment during his prayers." For this reason, a person should pray in the finest clothing available, for prayer is the worthiest occasion on which one adorns oneself. It is narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they permitted praying in a single garment, provided it covers the worshipper so that his awrah is not visible. If the garment is so sheer that it reveals him, they did not consider it sufficient on its own; something must be worn with it to conceal the awrah. For a man, the awrah is what lies between the navel and the knees. For a woman, her entire body is awrah that must be covered in prayer, with the exception of her face. A free woman, when she prays, veils herself; a slave woman may pray without a veil, but she covers her head and does not leave her hair exposed. When a man finds nothing else, it is permissible for him to pray in a wrapper that covers his awrah even if the rest of his body remains uncovered. This is not something he does by choice while other clothing is at hand; he wears what he finds.
