When wearing a robe, a gown, or a cloak, the worshipper drapes it across his shoulders, placing its middle length over them, casting the left end over to his right and the right end over that to his left.

The scholars forbade in prayer the wrapping known as ishtimal al-samma — that is, gathering the edge of a garment that lies on the shoulder, drawing it with the left hand toward oneself, throwing what had rested on the right side over to the left, and winding the body inside it as the people of the wilderness do, keeping the hands within. Prayer performed in that manner is not permitted.

They did permit praying in the covering burnus, and they forbade sadl in prayer, calling it the practice of the Jews. Sadl is to lay a robe by its middle length across the shoulders and let both sides hang loose at the hands without folding any part back over the shoulders.

They permitted a worshipper to pray girded with a sword, and they commended this for:

- the Friday prayer
- the two Eid prayers
- Jihad and Ribat
- any situation in which the worshipper fears for himself, whether in travel or otherwise

They saw no harm in praying in leather socks, and they permitted praying in sandals provided these do not distract the worshipper and he is able to manage in them. They forbade praying in the garments of the polytheists that those people had worn, until such garments are purified.

They commended that a woman pray wearing her ornaments when she possesses them, and that she not strip herself of them — even to the extent of tying about her neck a cord or some small token by which she is distinguished from men. They disliked that women divest themselves of ornament and of henna, alter the appearance of their palms, or take on any resemblance to men in appearance.

Whatever is permissible to wear is permissible to prostrate upon, whether it comes from the growth of the earth or from other materials such as wool, hair, fur, or the like.

The worshipper must stand firm in prayer and must not prostrate upon any foodstuff. The scholars disliked his prostrating on any part of his own clothing — neither on his sleeve nor on the coil of his turban — and held that he must uncover his forehead so that it, or as much of it as he is able, reaches the surface of prostration, together with his nose.

Concerning the Friday prayer, the Messenger of God (ص) said that four have what preceded them forgiven: the sick man when he recovers, the polytheist when he embraces Islam, the pilgrim when he completes his Hajj, and the one who returns from Jumuah in faith and in hope of reward. He said: "Pray abundantly upon me on Friday, for it is a day on which deeds are multiplied." And he said: "When Friday night comes, God Almighty sends down angels bearing pens of gold and silver and scrolls, and when the dawn breaks they record nothing but the prayers upon me and upon my Ahl al-Bayt until the sun sets. And angels stand at the doors of the mosques recording those who enter, each in his rank, first and then next, from the breaking of dawn until the Imam ascends the pulpit; when the Imam ascends the pulpit they fold their scrolls and enter to listen to the remembrance." He said: "There are three things which, if my nation knew the merit they hold, they would cast lots for them: the call to prayer, going early to Jumuah, and the first row in prayer." And he said: "Whoever goes early to the mosque on Friday and recites Surah Al Imran to its completion, seventy angels ascend with it and seventy angels receive it, and he is forgiven what lies between the two Fridays. And if he recites Surah al-Kahf, seventy angels ascend with it and seventy angels receive it, and he is gathered on the Day of Resurrection with a light spanning the distance between Mecca and Medina."

Ali said, concerning the words of God Almighty, "حَافِظُوا عَلَى الصَّلَوَاتِ وَالصَّلَوَاتِ الْوُسْطَى" ("Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer"), that the middle prayer is the Friday prayer, which on the remaining days is the noon prayer; and he would command that the moment of the sun's decline be carefully observed. He said: "Beware of forsaking attendance at the mosques, for one of you may begin by coming to the mosque only on Friday, then fall back and come one Friday and miss another, then fall back further until he no longer comes at all, and so God sets a seal upon his heart."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Hussein (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) said: "Deeds are multiplied on Friday, so I love to increase my prayers and charity on that day."

Ali said: "Friday night is a resplendent night and its day is radiant. Whoever dies on Friday night, God writes for him a release from the torment of the grave; and whoever dies on Friday, God frees him from the Fire. Hell is kindled every day except Friday, for on that day it is not kindled."

There is no harm in praying throughout the whole of Friday.

Ali said that the Friday prayer is an obligation, and gathering for it under a just Imam is an obligation. A man who forsakes it three times without excuse has forsaken three obligatory duties, and none forsakes three without excuse but a hypocrite.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that when Friday night comes, God commands an angel who calls out from the night's beginning to its end — just as He calls out every other night in the final third: "Is there one who asks, that I may give him? Is there one who repents, that I may turn to him? Is there one who seeks forgiveness, that I may forgive him? O seeker of good, draw near; O seeker of evil, hold back." And when Friday morning comes, God apportions provisions among His servants until the sun rises.

Narrations from the Ahl al-Bayt commend several practices:

- Performing ghusl on Friday
- Avoiding medicine on Friday and Thursday, lest it weaken a person and prevent him from attending Jumuah
- Wearing perfume and the finest clothing available when setting out to witness the Friday prayer

The Messenger of God (ص) said that on Friday there is an hour in which no believing servant asks God for any good but that He grants it. Ali, Abu Jafar, and Abu Abdullah (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) said that this hour runs from the sun's decline until an hour has passed.

Jumuah is obligatory on all Muslims except the traveler, the woman, the child, the sick, the owned slave, and anyone else with a valid excuse. If any of these attend it nonetheless, it counts in place of the noon prayer.

Whoever lives within two farsakhs of the congregational mosque — or closer — is bound to attend. A farsakh equals three miles, and a mile equals three thousand cubits. Where two congregational mosques stand three miles apart, Jumuah is obligatory at each of them, even within a single town.

The minimum number required to establish Jumuah is five men, one of whom must be the Imam. If fewer than five are present, they do not gather for the Friday prayer but pray the noon prayer instead — for the Friday prayer led by the Imam carries the reward of seventy prayers. This ruling applies to those living in a settled town.

> فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ ٱلصَّلَوٰةُ فَٱنتَشِرُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱبْتَغُوا۟ مِن فَضْلِ ٱللَّهِ

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا نُودِيَ لِلصَّلَاةِ مِن يَوْمِ الْجُمُعَةِ فَاسْعَوْا إِلَىٰ ذِكْرِ اللَّهِ

On the verse "O you who believe, when the call to prayer is made on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of God" [al-Jumuah: 9], the scholars held that this is a divine command, yet the hastening it enjoins is neither a trot nor a run. The merit lies in setting out early, before the call is given. Once the call sounds, one may not remain behind; the worshipper walks to the prayer at a normal pace, arriving for whatever portion he arrives for, without hurrying or running.

As for the verse فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ الصَّلَاةُ فَانتَشِرُوا فِي الْأَرْضِ وَابْتَغُوا مِن فَضْلِ اللَّهِ — "And when the prayer is concluded, disperse through the land and seek the bounty of God" [al-Jumuah: 10] — this is a command of permission. Just as God commanded hastening at the call, He permitted departure after the prayer for whoever wishes to leave. Whoever remains in the mosque — praying, attending to remembrance, or simply sitting — receives the merit and reward of that. Whoever departs once the prayer is concluded bears no blame, for God has expressly permitted it.

If a worshipper enters the mosque on Friday before the prayer, he may pray whatever voluntary prayers he wishes beforehand, and whatever voluntary prayers he wishes afterward. The scholars disliked that anyone arriving to find the mosque already full should climb over the necks of those already seated. Ali said: "That I should miss the Friday prayer altogether is dearer to me than that I should wait and then, once the Imam has taken his seat, come stepping over the necks of the people." Whoever arrives when the mosque is full should sit wherever he finds room, without stepping over anyone. If, however, he finds the congregation spread apart in such a way that he can pass between them to an open space at the front, there is no harm in walking forward to it.

Speech once the Imam has ascended the pulpit is forbidden. The congregation is enjoined to face him and to listen to the sermon with full attention. When the Imam has finished his address, there is no harm in speaking during the interval between that moment and the raising of the prayer.

When the Imam enters the mosque on Friday, he ascends the pulpit, settles, turns to face the congregation, greets them, and sits. The muezzins rise and give the call to prayer before him; when they have finished the adhan, they sit. He rises, praises and extols God, prays upon the Prophet and his family, and exhorts the people to the degree he is able and has prepared; then he sits briefly, rises again, praises and extols God once more, prays upon the Prophet and his family, and supplicates for the Imam and the believers to the degree he is able. When he has finished, the muezzins rise and call the iqama. The Imam descends and leads the Friday prayer of two rakahs. In the first rakah, after the opening chapter of the Book, he recites Surah al-Jumuah; in the second, after the opening chapter, Surah al-Munafiqun. He recites aloud, performs the qunut after completing the recitation in the second rakah, performs the tashahhud, gives the salam, and departs. The sermon is to be delivered only while the Imam is turbaned and girded with a sword, where that is possible and available to him.

Whoever joins the Friday prayer with the Imam in time to catch the last rakah — before the Imam raises his head from bowing — has caught that rakah and adds a second to it. If he does not reach it until after the Imam has raised his head, he has not caught the Jumuah prayer and must pray four rakahs. If the congregation misses Jumuah entirely, they do not make it up; they pray the noon prayer instead. Should the Imam be absent and no one be found to lead them in the congregational mosque, one of them leads the rest in the noon prayer of four rakahs.

> وَأَذَنٌۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِۦٓ إِلَى ٱلنَّاسِ يَوْمَ ٱلْحَجِّ ٱلْأَكْبَرِ

Regarding the prayers of the two Eids, God Almighty said: "شَهْرُ رَمَضَانَ الَّذِي أُنزِلَ فِيهِ الْقُرْآنُ" ("The month of Ramadan in which the Quran was sent down"), through to His saying: "وَلِتُكْمِلُوا الْعِدَّةَ وَلِتُكَبِّرُوا اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا هَدَاكُمْ وَلَعَلَّكُمْ تَشْكُرُونَ" ("that you may complete the number and magnify God for having guided you, and that you may give thanks") [al-Baqarah: 185]. He also said: "وَأَذَانٌ مِنَ اللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِ إِلَى النَّاسِ يَوْمَ الْحَجِّ الْأَكْبَرِ" ("And a proclamation from God and His Messenger to the people on the day of the Greater Pilgrimage") [al-Tawbah: 3].

The transmitted accounts from the Imams of the household of the Messenger of God ﷺ hold that the day of the Greater Pilgrimage is the day of sacrifice, and that within it fall:

- the Visitation
- the Tawaf al-Ifadah
- the slaughter
- the shaving
- the casting of the pebbles

The Imams further stated that the takbeer follows the completion of the number of the fast — on the night of Fitr and the day of Fitr. The takbeer is likewise made on Adha, and that will be addressed in its proper place, God willing.

Ali said: "It pleases me that a person free himself four nights in the year for prayer and the remembrance of God: the night of Fitr, the night of Adha, the night of the middle of Sha'ban, and the first night of Rajab."

Ali also said: "I heard the Messenger of God (ص) address the people on the day of Adha, saying: This is the day of thajj and ajj. As for thajj, it is the blood you shed; whoever's intention is sincere, the first drop of it is an expiation for him of every sin. And as for ajj, it is supplication, so raise your voices to God. By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, none departs from this standing place but is forgiven, save the one who persists in one of the major sins and does not resolve within himself to abandon it."

The Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have commended several practices for the two Eids:

- Performing ghusl on the day of Fitr and the day of Adha before going out to the prayer ground.
- Breaking the fast on the day of Fitr — with whatever is available — before going out, and refraining from eating on the day of sacrifice until after returning from the prayer.
- Wearing the finest clothing one possesses when going out to either Eid, and using whatever perfume is at hand.
- The Imam being turbaned, whether in winter or summer.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali, when he intended to go out to either of the two Eids or to Jumuah, would say: "O God, whoever has prepared and made ready and equipped and provided himself to arrive before a creature, hoping for its bounty and its gift — then to You, O God, is my preparation, my making ready, my equipping, and my provisioning, hoping for Your bounty, Your gift, and Your favors. I have gone out to You, presenting no righteous deed I have advanced and no intercession of any creature in whom I have placed hope, save the intercession of Muhammad and his family. Rather I have gone out to You confessing my sins and my wrongdoing against myself. O Magnificent, O Magnificent, O Magnificent, forgive the great sin, for none forgives the great sin but You. O Exalted, O Magnificent, there is no god but You."

It is also related that weapons, equipment, and mounts are to be displayed on the day of Eid, so that the community may take pride in them and the enemy be awed by them. The pulpit is brought out and erected on open ground; and if that ground lies near the burial-ground, that is better. The Imam then goes out.

The Commander of the Believers (ع) used to go out to the two Eids in Kufa proclaiming the takbeer, and around him and before him a thousand of the city's elders and men of mature years proclaimed it with him. The people likewise go out in their finest garments, with dignity, magnifying Allah, declaring His oneness, and praising Him as they go and as they return. On reaching the prayer ground, they sit without offering any voluntary prayer either before the Eid prayer or after it. They sit in awe and reverence, remembering Allah, not pressing upon one another; each man sits where he has arrived. The rows are to be spaced so that every worshipper has room to prostrate and to bow, for whoever cannot manage that has no valid prayer.

When the imam arrives, he begins with the prayer before the sermon, for such is the Sunnah of the Eid. Upon this practice stood the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and those who came after him, until Uthman changed it. He had prayed with the people at an Eid, and when the prayer ended most of them dispersed and did not stay to hear the sermon. Thereafter he placed the prayer after the sermon, so that they would not leave him as they leave on Friday. Yet it is not fitting that the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ be altered, exchanged, or turned away from what he established. Allah, mighty and exalted, has said: ﴿وَمَا آتَاكُمُ الرَّسُولُ فَخُذُوهُ وَمَا نَهَاكُمْ عَنْهُ فَانتَهُوا﴾ [al-Hashr: 7]. And He said: ﴿فَلْيَحْذَرِ الَّذِينَ يُخَالِفُونَ عَنْ أَمْرِهِ أَن تُصِيبَهُمْ فِتْنَةٌ أَوْ يُصِيبَهُمْ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌ﴾ [al-Nur: 63].

> لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا

The imam opens the prayer without adhan and without iqama, for neither belongs to the two Eids. He pronounces the takbir al-ihram, then recites the Opening of the Book and Surah al-Shams wa duhaha aloud. He then pronounces five takbeers; between each two he offers a light qunut, which is: "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, grant me well-being, and pardon me in this world and the hereafter; You are powerful over all things." He pronounces the sixth takbir, rising with it, and prostrates two prostrations. He stands for the second rakah and recites the Opening of the Book and Surah Hal ataka hadith al-Ghashiyah. He then pronounces four takbeers, offering a light qunut between each two, then the fifth takbir, rising with it, and prostrates two prostrations. He then gives the tashahhud and the salam.

He departs and ascends the pulpit. The people fall silent, facing him, seated in their places, and he delivers two sermons as described for the Friday prayer — with this difference: in the sermon of Eid al-Fitr he mentions the Sunnah of the Fitr charity and enjoins it, and in the sermon of Eid al-Adha he mentions the Sunnah of the sacrifice and urges it upon whoever is able and possesses the means. He then descends and departs, and the people depart with him.

It is recommended that they return by a route other than the one by which they came, when this is possible for them. Whoever misses the Eid prayer with the imam prays it alone at home as four rakahs. Women are permitted to go out for both Eids.

Upon the people of the open country there is no obligation of gathering or of going out for the two Eids. The Eid is held only in settled towns, wherever the Friday prayer is gathered, and is established by the same number that establishes the Friday prayer: five men, one of whom is the imam. If they are fewer than that, no Eid is held for them.

There is no Eid upon the traveler. If, however, the people of the wilderness attend, and any of the travelers choose to join the Eid, that is good and carries merit for them. It is fitting that a person of the wilderness who is within two leagues of the town, or nearer, should witness the Eid.

When Eid falls on a Friday, the Eid prayer is performed at its own time and the Friday prayer at its own time. The people of the wilderness, once the Eid prayer is done, may return to their dwellings — if these are distant — and that is their preference.

If the people rise on the day of Eid unaware that it is Eid, and it becomes clear to them before midday, they go out, pray the Eid, and break their fast if it is Eid al-Fitr. If they do not learn of it until after midday, they break their fast and then go out the following day at the usual hour of the Eid prayer and make it up exactly as they would have prayed it on the day itself.

The time for going out to both Eids is when the sun has risen somewhat. On the day of Eid al-Fitr, going out is delayed a little later than on the day of Eid al-Adha.

The takbeer of the days of Tashreeq runs from the Fajr prayer on the day of Arafah through to the Asr prayer on the last of the days of Tashreeq; after that Asr it is pronounced and then cut off. It is obligatory upon men and women alike, whether travelling or at home, and is said after every prescribed prayer. No takbeer follows a voluntary prayer.

The takbeer of the days of Tashreeq is:

"Allahu akbar, Allahu akbar, la ilaha illa Allah, Allahu akbar, Allahu akbar, wa lillahi al-hamd. Allahu akbar ala ma hadana, wa Allahu akbar ala ma razaqana min bahaim al-anam."

The same takbeer is said while going out to the prayer ground on the day of sacrifice and on the day of Eid al-Fitr alike, except that on Eid al-Fitr the phrase concerning what He has provided of the grazing beasts is omitted.

The days of Tashreeq are the three days following the day of sacrifice. They take their name from the practice of spreading strips of meat out in the sun to dry. The day of Arafah is the day before the sacrifice. The day of sacrifice is the day of Eid itself, which follows the day of Arafah. After it comes the day of settlement, so named because the pilgrims settle at Mina on that day. Then follows the first day of departure, and after it the last day of departure — the final day of the days of Tashreeq — on which the takbeer is pronounced after the Asr prayer and then cut off.

When the congregation prays during the days of Tashreeq, the imam gives the salam and pronounces the takbeer; those behind him then follow with the takbeer, raising their voices as he raises his. Whoever the imam has preceded in part of the prayer rises, once the imam has given the salam, to make up what he missed, then pronounces the takbeer after his own salam.

On the matter of forgetfulness in prayer: it is related from the Imams of the House of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ that they taught the following rulings.

- Whoever forgets the takbir al-ihram — the opening takbeer — and prays, then learns afterward that he never pronounced it at the beginning, must repeat the prayer in full.
- Whoever doubts whether he has bowed a rakah bows, then performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.
- Whoever doubts and cannot determine whether he has prayed one rakah or two: if one rakah is established with certainty, he builds upon it and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness; if nothing is established, he begins the prayer afresh, treating the rakah he is currently in as the first, then performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.
- Whoever doubts and cannot determine whether he has prayed two rakahs or three builds upon certainty — that is, two — and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.
- Whoever doubts and cannot determine whether he has prayed three rakahs or four gives the salam, then prays two rakahs seated and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If he had already prayed four, those two rakahs seated count as a voluntary prayer for him; if he had prayed only three, the two rakahs from a seated position stand in place of one rakah standing.

This ruling rests on what has come from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, who said: "The prayer of one seated, without excuse, is half the prayer of one standing."

If he doubts and cannot determine whether he has prayed two rakahs or four, he gives the tashahhud and the salam, then rises and prays two more rakahs, reciting the Opening of the Book in each, gives the tashahhud and the salam again, and then performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Should it turn out he had prayed only two, his prayer is complete; should it turn out he had prayed four, those final two rakahs count as a voluntary prayer for him — though he ought to fix his heart on them as fulfilling the obligation.

Whoever forgets the bowing must repeat the prayer. Whoever forgets a prostration prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness when he remembers, preceded by the tashahhud. Whoever forgets the tashahhud prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness and then gives the tashahhud and the salam after them. If he departs having given the tashahhud but neglects to give the salam, the salam within the tashahhud itself suffices him — that is, his words "peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah." Whoever forgets the recitation but has completed the bowing and the prostration owes no repetition; he performs only the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If, however, he omits the recitation deliberately, he must repeat the prayer.

If he forgets to sit for the first tashahhud and rises toward the third rakah without sitting, then remembers before he bows, he sits and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness after he has finished the prayer. In all the cases mentioned above, the two prostrations of forgetfulness come after the salam.

If he remembers before rising and before speaking, he performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness, then gives a light tashahhud, gives the salam, and departs.

If he has forgotten and given the salam after two rakahs, then realizes this before he has left his prayer, he completes the prayer, gives the tashahhud, and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

Likewise, if he has left the prayer and spoken, he need only supply what remained upon him and then perform the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

The Messenger of Allah ﷺ himself once forgot and gave the salam after two rakahs of a shortened prayer, then departed. Dhu al-Yadayn said to him: "Has the prayer been shortened, or have you forgotten, O Messenger of Allah?" He said, "How is that?" He said, "You departed after two rakahs." The Messenger of Allah ﷺ then turned to the congregation and asked, "Is what Dhu al-Yadayn said true?" They said, "Yes, O Messenger of Allah." He prayed the remaining two rakahs, gave the salam, then performed the two prostrations of forgetfulness, gave the salam again, and departed.

Whoever forgets and adds to his prayer falls into one of two cases. If he had already given the tashahhud at the end of his prayer before rising to add, his prayer stands complete and what he added counts as a voluntary prayer; he then performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If he had not yet sat for the tashahhud and the addition was a bowing, he repeats the prayer from the beginning; but if the addition was only a prostration, he completes the prayer and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

Whoever forgets and cannot determine whether he added to his prayer or fell short — his mind inclining to neither possibility — gives the tashahhud and the salam, then prays two rakahs in which he recites the Opening of the Book, gives the tashahhud and the salam again, and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

Whoever doubts whether he performed the bowing after he has already prostrated continues with his prayer. Likewise, if he doubts whether he performed the prostration after he has already risen, he continues and owes nothing further until certainty comes to him. The same applies to any doubt concerning his prayer that arises after he has finished it: nothing is owed until he is certain.

Whoever neglects his prayer and abandons it deliberately prays it when he remembers. Whoever is frequently afflicted with forgetfulness proceeds on the basis of what he knows with certainty, builds upon that certainty, and performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever imagines, while in the middle of his prayer, that he has broken his ablution continues with the prayer unless he becomes certain that he has. Whoever forgets something while praying behind the imam owes nothing.

As for what cuts off the prayer: if something arises mid-prayer that a worshipper fears for himself, his property, or another — some sudden matter that cannot wait for the prayer to be completed — there is no harm in cutting the prayer off, then beginning it again. Breaking one's ablution within the prayer also cuts it off; the worshipper leaves, renews the ablution, and resumes. If a worshipper vomits or has a nosebleed during the prayer and can rinse away the vomit or wash off the blood without turning his face from the qibla, he does so and builds upon what he has already prayed; but if he turns his face from the qibla, he begins the prayer afresh. Whoever speaks during the prayer has cut it off and must repeat it. Whoever, while praying, remembers that he is in a state of major impurity or is otherwise without purity cuts the prayer off, purifies himself, and begins afresh. If he remembers this only after the prayer is finished, he purifies himself and repeats the prayer — and if he was leading the congregation as imam, all who prayed behind him must repeat their prayers as well.

A worshipper does not cut off the prayer on account of something passing before him; rather, he wards it off as best he can. Ali said: the Messenger of Allah ﷺ prayed one day, and there passed before him a woman, then a donkey, then a dog, while the companions were watching. When he had finished, he said, "I have seen what you have seen; nothing cuts off the prayer of the believer. But ward off what you are able to ward off."

As for one who has been preceded in part of the prayer: when a man arrives while the imam is leading the congregation and joins their prayer, he pronounces the takbeer of consecration. If he reaches the imam before the bowing, or before the imam has raised his head from the bowing, he bows with him and counts that rakah. If he does not reach him until after the imam has raised his head from the bowing, he joins the prayer at that point and prostrates with the imam's prostration, but does not count that rakah; he counts what follows it and takes that as the beginning of his own prayer.

If the imam has preceded him by a full rakah, the latecomer prays the second rakah with him. When the imam sits for the first tashahhud, the latecomer does not settle fully but sits poised to rise, and does not recite the tashahhud. When the imam rises for the third rakah, that rakah is the latecomer's second. The latecomer prostrates with the imam, and when the imam rises for the fourth rakah, the latecomer briefly returns to the ground, recites a short tashahhud, then rises.

When the imam sits again, the latecomer sits as before — poised, not settled — and again withholds the tashahhud. When the imam gives the salam, the latecomer does not give it; instead he rises, makes up the remaining rakah, then sits fully settled, recites the tashahhud and the salam, and departs.

If the imam has preceded him by two rakahs, the latecomer takes the rakah he catches as the first of his own prayer. When the imam gives the salam, the latecomer does not give it; he rises instead and prays two rakahs to complete his prayer, then gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs. The tashahhud he performs at the imam's final sitting counts, for him, as the first tashahhud.

Likewise, if he catches only a single rakah of the prayer, he builds upon it, taking it as the first of his prayer, and observes at the imam's sittings and risings the same pattern already described for one who has missed a rakah.

In the Maghrib prayer, if the imam has preceded him by one rakah, the latecomer enters with the imam in the second rakah, taking it as the first of his own prayer. He sits when the imam sits for the first tashahhud — poised, as described, without giving the tashahhud. When the imam rises for the second rakah, the latecomer takes it as his own second. When the imam sits for the final tashahhud, the latecomer sits with him and recites, for himself, the first tashahhud. When the imam gives the salam, the latecomer rises, prays one rakah, sits, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs.

If he does not catch even that one rakah with the imam, he takes what he has as the first of his prayer. When the imam sits to give the tashahhud, the latecomer sits in the same poised manner already described, without giving the tashahhud. When the imam gives the salam, the latecomer rises and prays one rakah, reciting in it the Opening of the Book and a surah. He then sits for the first tashahhud, rises again, and completes his prayer.

And if the imam has preceded him by a rakah in the Fajr prayer, and the imam sits for the tashahhud, the latecomer sits with him as we described, poised, without giving the tashahhud; and when the imam gives the salam, the latecomer rises and prays a rakah, then sits, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs. If the latecomer forgets and gives the salam with the imam's salam, then remembers, he makes up what he missed and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. This is the teaching of the Imams of the House of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ.

If the imam has preceded a latecomer by one rakah in the Fajr prayer, and the imam sits for the testimony, the latecomer sits as well in the manner already described, poised and ready, but does not recite the testimony himself. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer rises and prays a single rakah, then sits, recites the testimony, gives the salutation, and departs. Should the latecomer forget that the imam had already given the salutation and give it with him, then remember, he makes up what he missed and performs the two prostrations of omission. This is the teaching of the imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ.

The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "The pen is lifted from three: from the child until he reaches puberty, from the sleeper until he wakes, and from the insane until he recovers." This establishes that prayer carries no binding obligation upon boys until they reach puberty. Yet it is transmitted from Ali and the imams of his lineage that they directed boys to be brought to prayer once they are able to understand it, and to fasting once they are able to bear it — both before puberty, and neither as a strict duty. The purpose is training: that a boy may grow accustomed to prayer and settle into it, so that when he reaches puberty he already knows it and has been keeping it, and is not suddenly plunged into it in a way that breeds weariness, restlessness, and indifference. Habits laid down early become a second nature.

A related teaching from them holds that whoever takes up a good deed — whether prayer, fasting, or any other gate of righteousness — and persists in it should continue without interruption until a full year has passed. The intention behind this was to draw people toward good gradually, making the path light for them. Had they said "persist forever," the weight of that command would have fallen heavily on those who heard it. But when a man is told to keep to something for a year, the task feels manageable; and once the year has passed, that practice becomes his nature and his habit, something he can no longer simply cut away, because he has grown familiar with it, found ease in it, and taken genuine comfort in it — so that abandoning it becomes almost impossible. It is the same spirit that moved them to bring boys to prayer and fasting before puberty. Their ruling was that children are to be commanded to pray at seven years of age, and corrected for neglecting it at nine. At ten, they are to be separated in their beds. That separation at this particular age is a precaution: the closeness of children to one another during sleep coincides with their approaching puberty and the first stirring of desire, and proximity in bed risks leading to something feared. This is the discipline the friends of Allah exercise toward His servants — those whom Allah has entrusted to their care and whose affairs, both of religion and of the world, He has committed to their keeping.

> فَسِيحُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ أَرْبَعَةَ أَشْهُرٍۢ

On the traveler's prayer, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "Allah, blessed and exalted, has bestowed on my nation a gift He has bestowed on no other nation before it" — an act of honor from Allah, mighty and majestic. It was asked: "O Messenger of Allah, what is this gift?" He said: "The breaking of the fast and the shortening of the prayers in travel. Whoever does not do so has returned to Allah His gift." Ali said: "Whoever shortens the prayer in travel has accepted the relief of Allah, mighty and majestic, and his prayer is complete." It was also reported that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ forbade praying the prayer in full during travel. Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "I am innocent of anyone who prays four rakahs in travel." Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali held that whoever prays in full while traveling, knowing that the sunnah is to shorten, must repeat the prayer; whoever does not know this need not repeat it.

Shortening in travel means praying every prayer as two rakahs, except Maghrib, which is not shortened — whether in travel or at home. The traveler prays the sunnah and supererogatory prayers as well. If he is journeying by day and the road presses him on, he prays the obligatory prayer as two rakahs and defers the remaining sunnah until he alights at night, making it up together with the night prayer. This was held praiseworthy, and its neglect was not approved. In no obligatory prayer — whether in travel or at home — does the traveler pray in full, nor does he pray fewer rakahs than the number that applies to his situation.

The distance at which shortening the prayer and breaking the fast become obligatory is two barids — twenty-four thousand paces. A barid equals four farsakhs; a farsakh equals three miles; a mile equals three thousand cubits, which amounts to one thousand moderate paces measured by a single leg, from where the right foot is lifted to where it is set down. Even if a traveler is bound for a place only a single barid away — with no family or dwelling there, and intending to return in fewer than ten days — he still shortens and breaks his fast for the duration of that journey. The traveler's license to break his fast and shorten his prayers runs from the moment he leaves his house and sets out, until he returns and enters his house again, provided he presses on in his travel.

When a traveler joins a congregational prayer led by residents, he gives the salutation after two rakahs and departs. When a traveler leads residents in prayer, he concludes at two rakahs and then instructs them to complete the prayer on their own. Both situations are disliked, as already noted, because the traveler's ruling in prayer is not the resident's ruling. Whoever carries an unpaid prayer from his days of residence and recalls it while traveling must make it up in full, exactly as it was originally owed. Whoever carries an unpaid prayer from his travels and recalls it while resident makes it up shortened, exactly as it was when it was owed.

While traveling, a person may pray voluntary prayers on his mount, facing whichever direction the animal happens to be heading. This rests on the word of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَأَيْنَمَا تُوَلُّوا فَثَمَّ وَجْهُ اللَّهِ﴾ "Wherever you turn, there is the face of Allah" [al-Baqarah: 115]. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ himself prayed voluntary prayers on his mount during the return from the Farewell Pilgrimage, with the House behind his back. The dispensation, however, belongs to supererogatory prayer alone; an obligatory prayer must be performed on the ground, facing the Qibla. Praying on a beaten track — a highway along which people pass — is forbidden, as is praying in the space between two roads.

At sea, the traveler shortens the prayer and prays aboard the ship. If the ship changes course, he turns his face with it to maintain the Qibla. Should he be unable to stand, he prays seated. Three further cases follow the same principle of praying by gesture, with the prostration held lower than the bowing:

- One who is drowning
- One who is naked and finds nothing with which to cover himself
- One wading through water

The person who is naked additionally prays seated in that condition.

Shortening prayer is not permitted for one who travels merely for leisure, nor for a governor making his rounds among his people, nor for a tax-collector moving through his district, nor for a hired driver, nor for seafarers whose very calling is perpetual travel, nor for one who stops during a journey at a household of his own intending to stay there. Whoever halts at a place during travel intending to remain for ten days prays in full; if he intends to stay for less, he shortens. If his stay is prolonged without that intention — each day expecting to depart — he shortens for up to a month, and after the month has passed he prays in full until he leaves.

As for the prayer of the sick, the teaching received from the Ahl al-Bayt is as follows: if the sick person cannot pray standing, he prays lying on his back with the Qibla before his face in the direction of his feet, or lying on his right side with his face turned toward the Qibla, gesturing through his movements so that his prostration is held lower than his bowing. One who prays seated and cannot bow or prostrate observes the same principle. One afflicted with a nosebleed that will not stop, or with a wound that keeps discharging and can neither be bound nor stanched, performs ablution and prays in that state — for Allah is most ready to excuse His servant when He has placed him under trial.

If the sick person cannot pray beyond the obligatory, he may, once recovered, make up the voluntary and supererogatory prayers he missed — and there is merit and reward in doing so — though their make-up carries no binding obligation as the make-up of the obligatory does.

If he can pray seated with his legs folded, he folds them as he would in the sitting position during the standing. If he cannot, he prays in whatever posture he is able, and that suffices him. It likewise suffices him to recite the Opening of the Book alone, and if even that is beyond him, to say a single glorification in his bowing and his prostration when nothing more is within his power.

If he is able to prostrate, he prostrates. If not, he nods his head, holding the prostration lower than the bowing. If he cannot nod his head, he signals with whatever remains available to him — his head or his eye — as his condition allows, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.

If he is able to pray standing, he prays standing only. Should standing aggravate his illness, however, he may pray in the manner described above, doing what he can. Whoever falls into a swoon makes up the prayers he missed upon recovering, whether they were few or many.

The prayer of fear rests on the word of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَن تَقْصُرُوا مِنَ الصَّلَاةِ إِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَن يَفْتِنَكُمُ الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا إِنَّ الْكَافِرِينَ كَانُوا لَكُمْ عَدُوًّا مُّبِينًا وَإِذَا كُنتَ فِيهِمْ فَأَقَمْتَ لَهُمُ الصَّلَاةَ فَلْتَقُمْ طَائِفَةٌ مِّنْهُم مَّعَكَ﴾ "There is no blame upon you if you shorten the prayer, if you fear that those who disbelieve may harm you.

Indeed, the disbelievers are to you a clear enemy. And when you are among them and lead them in prayer, let a party of them stand with you" [al-Nisa: 101–102], down to His words: "a humiliating punishment."

In this way Allah, mighty and majestic, granted the relief of shortening the prayer in the state of fear, making no exception for travel as distinct from residence. Abu Abd-Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about the prayer of fear and the prayer of travel — whether both are shortened — and he said: "Yes, and the prayer of fear is more deserving of shortening than the prayer of travel, for in the prayer of travel there is no fear."

The imams of the Ahl al-Bayt transmitted that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ prayed the prayer of fear during the campaign of Dhat al-Riqa. He divided his companions into two parties, stationing one facing the enemy and placing the other behind him. He said the opening takbir and they said it with him; he recited and they listened; he bowed and they bowed; he prostrated and they prostrated. Then the Messenger of Allah ﷺ remained standing, and that party completed a further rakah on their own, gave the salutation to one another, and withdrew to join their companions, taking their place facing the enemy.

The second party then came forward and stood behind the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. He said the takbir and they said it; he recited and they listened; he bowed and they bowed; he prostrated and they prostrated; he sat and they sat and bore witness. When the Messenger of Allah ﷺ gave the salutation, they rose and made up a further rakah for themselves, bore witness, gave the salutation to one another, and withdrew.

Such, they said, is how the prayer of fear is performed whenever circumstances permit — in all the prayers except Maghrib, which is not shortened, as has already been noted. The first party prays one rakah with the imam and then completes two rakahs on its own, following the manner of the Messenger of Allah's ﷺ prayer described above. The other party prays two rakahs with him, so that each party hears the recitation during the first two rakahs, in which he recites aloud.

If this arrangement is impossible and the fear grows severe, they said, the worshippers pray mounted on their beasts or standing on their feet, nodding with their heads and making the prostration lower than the bowing — neither dismounting nor prostrating on the ground when that is beyond them. If they cannot face the Qibla, they face it at the first takbir and then turn to meet the enemy. This is the word of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ فَرِجَالًا أَوْ رُكْبَانًا﴾ — "If you are in fear, then on foot or riding" [al-Baqarah: 239].

If the fighting closes in entirely — the clash of swords, the thrust of spears — and even a gesture is beyond them, they pray with the takbir alone, pronouncing a takbir for each rakah with the intention of prayer. It was in precisely this way that the Commander of the Believers, Ali, and his companions prayed on the night and the day of al-Harir.

For the eclipse prayer, Ali reported that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "The moon was eclipsed while Jibril (عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ) was with me, and I said to him: What is this, O Jibril? He said: Truly it is more obedient to Allah than you, and it has not disobeyed Allah since He created it; but this is a sign and a lesson. I said: What is to be done at such a time? He said: Prayer and the recitation of the Quran." When the sun or the moon was eclipsed, he would say to the people: "Hasten to your mosques."

The imams of the Ahl al-Bayt teach that the prayer for the solar eclipse and the prayer for the lunar eclipse follow the same form, the solar prayer being somewhat longer. When an eclipse occurs, the people are summoned without the adhan, and in a city they gather at the Grand Mosque. The imam steps forward and they line up behind him. He pronounces the takbir aloud, they follow, and he opens the recitation with the Opening of the Book, then the longest surah he can manage, reciting aloud throughout. He then says the takbir and bows, and the congregation bows with him. He holds the bowing for as long as the recitation lasted, and they hold it with him, glorifying God and saying what is said in the bowing. He raises his head with a takbir, and they raise theirs. He then recites the Opening of the Book again, followed by a long surah comparable to the first. After this recitation, before bowing, he performs the qunut, then bows in the same manner and rises with a takbir. This pattern continues through five bowings in all, with the qunut performed after each pair. When he raises his head from the fifth bowing, he says: "Allah hears the one who praises Him" — a phrase he pronounces only there and at the tenth bowing, after which he prostrates. In all the remaining bowings he says "Allahu akbar" as he raises his head, and then prostrates.

He draws out the prostration as he drew out the bowing, glorifying God and saying what is said in prostration. Then he raises his head and sits between the two prostrations, saying what is said there in its fullest form, before making the second prostration in the same way. He then rises and prays a further five bowings like the first set, then prostrates and sits for a long testimony in which he calls upon Allah, turns to Him in longing, and asks of Him what he can of this world and the Hereafter — then gives the salutation.

If the eclipse has cleared, he departs. If not, he remains seated in his place facing the Qibla and bids the people sit likewise, and they continue reciting the Quran, supplicating, and remembering Allah until the eclipse clears or passes away.

The eclipse prayer is not performed until the eclipse has actually been seen and confirmed. One may not rely on a report that the sun has eclipsed when no eclipse is visible, nor on a claim made at night that the moon has eclipsed by day, nor by day that the sun has eclipsed by night. The solar eclipse prayer is prayed only during the day, and the lunar eclipse prayer only at night — and in both cases, only after the eclipse has been witnessed.

The qunut in the eclipse prayer falls in every second bowing before the bowing itself: in the second, the fourth, the sixth, the eighth, and the tenth. There is no harm in reciting a full surah — or more than one — at each standing within a single rak'ah, distributing the recitation across the bowings. When doing so, the Opening of the Book is recited only at the outset, alongside the first surah; each subsequent standing begins at the opening of a new surah. The same applies when he rises from prostration into the second standing.

The qunut in this prayer is performed in every second rak'ah, before the second, fourth, sixth, eighth, and tenth bowings. It is also permitted to recite it in every rak'ah — and more than once — before bowing. When doing so, the Opening of the Book is recited only at the outset of each surah, marking it as the first opening of that surah; the same applies when rising from prostration in the second rak'ah of the eclipse prayer.

These two rak'ahs — with their four prostrations and five bowings in each rak'ah — were the form in which the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, led the people in the eclipse prayer. He prayed it at any time, by night or by day, whether traveling or residing in a city. If the eclipse coincided with the time of an obligatory prayer, he would begin with the obligatory prayer. If, however, he had already entered the eclipse prayer and feared that the time of the obligatory prayer would lapse, he would interrupt the eclipse prayer, perform the obligatory prayer, and then, upon completing it, resume and build upon what he had already finished of the eclipse prayer. In the same manner he prayed at every sign that occurred — wind, darkness, tremors, earthquakes, or any other portent — just as the eclipse prayer is prayed. Whoever misses the eclipse prayer, or the prayer of the signs, does not make it up. It is recommended that the eclipse prayer be performed in an open, cleared space so that the clearing of the eclipse may be witnessed. If the imam departs, those who can see the eclipse should be informed of it; and if he does not depart, the people are instructed to remain seated in their places, as has been mentioned.

> وَإِذِ ٱسْتَسْقَىٰ مُوسَىٰ لِقَوْمِهِۦ فَقُلْنَا ٱضْرِب

The prayer of al-Istisqa — the seeking of rain — is grounded in the word of Allah Almighty: "And when Moses sought water for his people, فقلنا اضرب بعصاك الحجر" (Al-Baqarah: 60). It is narrated that when the people of موسى were in need of water, rain later fell upon Madinah, and the Messenger of Allah went out and prayed for it. The rain came so heavily that the people grew fearful for themselves from its intensity. What has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt is that the prayer of al-Istisqa is performed like the two Eid prayers. The imam goes out, and the people go out to the prayer ground in humility before their Lord, in fear and self-abasement. Unlike the Eid, they do not carry weapons. The imam leads two rakahs without the call to prayer and without the iqamah, pronouncing the takbeer as he does for the two festival prayers and reciting as he recites for the two Eids. He then ascends the pulpit, and upon reaching the top he sits briefly before rising. He then turns his cloak, placing what was on his right side onto his left and what was on his left onto his right, exactly as the Messenger of Allah did.

He begins his sermon with the takbeer, the tahleel, and the seeking of forgiveness, offering as much of each as he is able. He then praises Allah, sends blessings upon the Prophet and his family, and urges the people to seek forgiveness, to repent, and to do good deeds, making them aware that rain is withheld on account of their sins. He repents to Allah Almighty on their behalf, asks Him, and implores Him for water and mercy to the fullest extent he is able. It is recommended that this prayer be performed on a Monday.

On the prayers of the Shaf, the Witr, and the two rakahs of Fajr: Allah Almighty said, "By the even and the odd" (Al-Fajr: 3), swearing by all of them. In the speech of the Arabs, the shaf is the paired or even, and the witr is the odd. In prayer, the witr consists of three rakahs — a sunnah of the night prayer performed after the final Isha — and no prayer is prayed after it until dawn breaks. The name witr applies to all three rakahs together; it may also refer to the single final rakah alone, with the two rakahs preceding it constituting the shaf. This latter understanding is what is preferred according to what is established from the words of the Ahl al-Bayt. The worshipper gives the salam after the two rakahs, then rises and prays a single rakah as the witr.

Strong encouragement has come for the witr. The Messenger of Allah commanded it, saying that Allah is witr and loves the witr, and that the people of the Quran should therefore pray it. Ali was severe on the matter and permitted no one to abandon it. Jafar ibn Muhammad held that the witr ought not to be abandoned, yet it does not rank among the prescribed obligatory prayers — it is of the sunnah. Whoever misses it is recommended to make it up, though this is not an obligation upon him in the way of prescribed duties, since it does not belong to that category. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali granted a dispensation allowing the witr to be prayed while traveling upon a mount, as the supererogatory prayers are prayed. The preferred time for the witr is the end of the night; whoever prays it at the beginning of the night has nonetheless fulfilled it.

The recommended recitation for the two rak'ahs before the witr is as follows: in the first, the Opening of the Book followed by "Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High, who created" (Al-A'la, اَلْأَعْلَى); in the second, the Opening of the Book together with "Say: O disbelievers" (Al-Kafirun); and in the third, the Opening of the Book followed by "Say: He is Allah, the One" (Al-Ikhlas, اَلْإِخْلَاص). In the witr itself, the qunut is performed after bowing, with the hands raised and with whatever supplication comes readily to the worshipper. In the Fajr prayer, by contrast, the qunut is performed before bowing and after the recitation.

The two rak'ahs of Fajr are prayed before the Fajr prayer itself: the call to Fajr is given, and once dawn has broken the worshipper prays two light rak'ahs, reciting in the first the Opening of the Book and "Say: O disbelievers," and in the second the Opening of the Book and "Say: He is Allah, the One." He then makes the tashahhud, remains seated in his place of prayer, and continues in supplication until the Fajr prayer begins.

On the matter of supplication, the scholars held that no fixed wording is so binding that nothing else will suffice — whether inside the prayer or outside it. Whoever wishes to supplicate may call upon Allah Almighty with whatever words he wishes.

The traditions recorded from the Ahl al-Bayt contain many forms of supplication and qunut, all confirming that no fixed wording is strictly required — as has already been established. The minimum form of qunut is three glorifications of Allah. From them have come supplications for the qunut of the witr and the qunut of the Fajr in long, short, and medium forms. Among the medium forms — the one upon which the narrations agree — is the instruction to recite in the qunut of the witr:

"O Allah, You see and are not seen, and You are in the highest of vantages. To You are lifted the eyes and moved the feet; toward You necks are stretched and hands spread open; by tongues You are called upon, and to You deeds are submitted. O You to whom hands are stretched out, O You to whom hearts have turned, O You before whom eyes are cast down, O You to whom necks are bowed: we complain to You of the hardship of the age, the massing of enemies, the fewness of our number, and the division of our hearts. We give thanks to You for Your blessings through our guardian and our imam, the son of the daughter of our Prophet, named the imam of his age, our guide to You and the one who points us to You. We ask You to send blessings upon him and upon his forefathers, and to support him with a victory by which You give might to Your religion and by which You aid Your friends. O Allah, gather hearts upon Your obedience and his obedience and upon holding to his imamate, and support him against his renegade enemies, O God of creation, Lord of the worlds. O Allah, make certainty firm in my heart, and increase me in light, guidance, and forgiveness, and guide me to Your straight path. Ameen, Ameen. I ask You, my Lord, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to guard me from the torment of the Fire."

Whoever wishes to lengthen the supplication may add to it as he wishes. And in the qunut of the Fajr:

"O Allah, we praise You, and seek Your help, and seek Your forgiveness, and extol You with good, and give thanks to You and do not deny You; we humble ourselves to You and submit to You, and we disown whoever is wicked toward You and denies You. O Allah, You alone we worship, and to You we pray and prostrate, and to You we strive and hasten; we hope for Your mercy and fear Your severe punishment, for Your punishment surely overtakes the disbelievers. O Allah, punish the disbelievers, the hypocrites, the liars, and those who deny Your friends, the imams from the pure family of Your Prophet, and send down upon them Your scourge, Your might, Your anger, and Your torment. O Allah, punish the disbelievers among the People of the Book, and the idolaters, men and women. Forgive me, and the believing men and women and the Muslim men and women, and set right what is between them, and unite their word upon Your obedience and the obedience of Your friends, and make firm in their hearts faith and wisdom, and steady them upon the religion of Your Prophet, and support them against Your enemy and their enemy. O Allah, guide me among those You have guided, take me into Your care among those You have taken, heal me among those You have healed, bless me in what You have given, and protect me from the evil of what You have decreed, for You decree and none decrees against You. He whom You befriend is not humiliated, and he whom You oppose is not honored. Blessed are You and exalted. There is no god but You.

"I seek Your forgiveness and repent to You, and I ask You, my Lord, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to guard me from the torment of the Fire."

For the qunut of the Friday prayer and the qunut of the eclipse prayer, the form runs:

"There is no god but Allah, the Wise, the Generous. There is no god but Allah, the High, the Great. Glory be to Allah, Lord of the seven heavens and Lord of the seven earths and all that is within them and between them, and Lord of the Mighty Throne. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and make my heart firm upon Your religion and the religion of Your Prophet. Let not my heart swerve after You have guided me, and grant me mercy from Yourself, for You are the Bestower. O Allah, make me one of those You have created for Your Garden and chosen for Your religion, and turn to me in acceptance, for You are capable of all things, and You are the Accepter of repentance, the Merciful. And send blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy of it and as they are worthy of it from You, may."

The obligatory prayers total seventeen rakahs in each day and night. Dhuhr is the first of them — called first because it was the first prayer the Messenger of Allah performed, at the hour the obligation of prayer was revealed to him. It is four rakahs: in the first two he recites the Opening of the Book and a surah, then makes the tashahhud; in the last two he recites the Opening of the Book alone, then makes the tashahhud and the tasleem. The Asr is likewise four rakahs, following the same pattern as Dhuhr. The Maghrib is three rakahs: he recites aloud in the first two and makes the tashahhud after them, then rises for a third rakah in which he recites the Opening of the Book silently, sits, makes the tashahhud, and departs. The Isha is four rakahs, like Dhuhr, except that he recites aloud in its first two rakahs. The Fajr is two rakahs, both recited aloud. That is seventeen rakahs.

The sunnah prayer mirrors the obligatory prayer in number, with all its recitation performed silently — though one may recite a little aloud in the night prayer if he wishes. The salam is given after every two rakahs, and in each rakah he recites the Opening of the Book and a surah. The rakahs are distributed as follows:

- Six rakahs before Dhuhr, after the sun has passed its zenith, and four after it
- Four rakahs before Asr, after which no prayer is performed until the sun sets
- Six rakahs after Maghrib, before the final Isha
- Four rakahs before Isha and four after it
- The witr: three rakahs, with the salam given after two, then a third rakah prayed alone, followed by two rakahs performed seated
- Two rakahs before the Fajr prayer

That gives thirty-five rakahs in total; yet because the two rakahs performed seated count as one rakah of standing, the true total is thirty-four — equal to the obligatory prayer. The prayer is called the sunnah prayer because the Messenger of Allah used to pray it and held himself to it. The imams from his progeny likewise bound themselves to it, commanded their companions and followers to observe it, encouraged them toward it, and conveyed to them its merit and its reward.

> وَمِنَ ٱلَّيْلِ فَتَهَجَّدْ بِهِۦ نَافِلَةًۭ لَّكَ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَبْعَثَكَ رَبُّكَ مَقَامًۭا مَّحْمُودًۭا

The supererogatory prayer has no fixed limit. It is voluntary; whoever wishes may pray as many rakahs as he wishes, at any permitted hour, by night or by day. It is narrated of Ali ibn al-Husayn that, when he was able, he would pray a thousand rakahs in a single day and night. The most excellent of this voluntary prayer is what is performed at night, after rising from sleep. Allah Almighty said to His Prophet Muhammad: "And in a part of the night keep vigil with it, as an additional prayer for you; perhaps your Lord will raise you to a praised station" (Al-Isra: 79). In Arabic usage, a man is said to have "hajada" when he sleeps, and "tahajjada" when he rises from sleep with some intention in mind — he wakes from his sleep for it and keeps vigil with it as an additional prayer, meaning with the Quran. Allah also said: "O you who are wrapped in garments, rise the night except a little, half of it, or lessen from it a little" (Al-Muzzammil: 1–4), to the end of the passage.

> وَٱسْتَغْفِرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ۖ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۢ

The Messenger of Allah used to stand in prayer through the night until his feet swelled from the length of his vigil. He forbade a man to sleep the whole night through, lest he become like a carcass thrown down. It is recommended to prolong the standing in the night prayer; it is not to be abandoned, but a fixed portion of it should be set aside for oneself.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "I hold in contempt the servant who has read the Quran and then wakes in the night yet does not rise — until, when morning draws near, he hurries through his prayer."

Whoever has not read the whole of the Quran may recite whatever comes readily to him. Allah Almighty said: "So recite what is easy for you of the Quran" (Al-Muzzammil: 20).

As for how one stands, there is latitude:

- Standing the whole night with a single surah, repeating it throughout
- Standing with part of a surah
- Standing with two surahs, or with more than that

There is likewise no harm, in the supererogatory prayer, in any of the following:

- Reciting part of a surah in a rakah together with the Opening of the Book
- Joining two surahs within a single rakah
- Joining many surahs

Some among them would stand the whole night, or most of it, with a single verse repeated each time they returned to it — a verse of promise and warning — pondering it, repeating it, seeking to understand it and to grow humble before it.

They preferred to open the night prayer with two light rakahs, then to pray after them whatever one wished.

> مَّآ أَفَآءَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ رَسُولِهِۦ مِنْ أَهْلِ ٱلْقُرَىٰ فَلِلَّهِ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ

The most excellent form of supererogatory prayer, charity, and all works of righteousness is what is performed in concealment and secrecy; the most excellent way to fulfill the obligatory acts — prayer, zakat, and the rest — is openly. Supererogatory prayers are not to be performed in congregation, whether in Ramadan or at any other time; the people are to pray them only individually. The most excellent place for them is the home, for their merit lies precisely in their hiddenness and privacy.

The Messenger of Allah once prayed a voluntary night prayer in the mosque during Ramadan, and the people ranged themselves behind him and prayed along with him. He forbade them from doing so, withdrew to his chamber, and prayed within it. The first to establish the standing in congregation during Ramadan as a regular practice was Umar ibn al-Khattab. Yet had there been merit in that practice, the Messenger of Allah would never have forbidden it, and he would have been the first to reach it ahead of Umar and everyone else. The common people have transmitted this very account from the Messenger of Allah and then followed Umar in it — in opposition to the Prophet and in defiance of his prohibition. Allah Almighty says:

> "Let those who go against his command beware, lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment strike them." *(Al-Nur: 63)*

And He says:

> "Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it, and whatever he forbids you, abstain from it, and fear Allah." *(Al-Hashr: 7)*

The most that has been said in defense of this practice is that the Messenger of Allah disliked it and forbade it only for those who prayed with him on that occasion, out of concern that it might become an obligation upon the people.

No license was claimed on his behalf in this matter, for had any license existed, he would have declared it as a prophet, may Allah bless him and his family. When he forbade a thing, it was because Allah Almighty had made the ruling plain and had made that clear declaration an obligation upon him. The command of Allah Almighty therefore binds one to refrain from what he forbade, unless a clear allowance for the claimed permission reaches one in turn. The claim is that it was permitted — yet the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, neither did it in the days of his own life, nor in the days of Abu Bakr, nor in the early days of Umar. It appeared only in Umar's later period. By their own reckoning, this makes it an innovation, and some of them acknowledge precisely that, while calling it a good innovation. But this contradicts the words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family: "Every innovation is misguidance, and every misguidance leads to the Fire. Follow, and do not innovate." How can an innovation be good when Allah Almighty forbade it upon the tongue of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and his family?

In the same way, they invented the Duha prayer, which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, had forbidden. A man of the Ansar asked Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali about it, and he answered: "Were your own people, the Ansar, not the first to invent it?" The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, had forbidden it. They would leave their homes, enter the mosque in the forenoon, and pray — and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, forbade them from doing so. Then Yazid ibn Muawiyah revived it over the severed head of al-Husayn in the forenoon: he rose, prayed two rakahs, and made it a fixed practice for himself — not out of any remorse his people held against him for what he had committed, but claiming, on his own account, that it was an act of thanksgiving for what he had done. It will only sink him deeper into the blaze of Hell.

Ali ibn al-Husayn held that a person should make up whatever voluntary prayers he had missed, whether he had been kept from them by other demands or had slept through them. This is not an obligation, but he — may Allah's blessings be upon him — loved not to let anything lapse that Allah Almighty had made part of his devotion.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Whoever intends to rise in the night, and takes to his bed, let him say: O Allah, do not leave me safe from Your cunning, and do not make me forget Your remembrance, and do not place me among the heedless. I will rise, Allah willing, at such and such an hour. Then Allah Almighty appoints for him an angel, an angel of that hour, who rouses him."

And he said: "Whoever intends something of the night vigil, and sleep overcomes his eyes until morning, his sleep is a charity to him from Allah, and Allah owes him the reward of his intention and completes for him the vigil of his night."

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Allah has an angel who, when half the night has passed, declares: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let those who strive arise. So whoever Allah wills arises. Then he falls silent as long as Allah wills, then he says: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let those who remember arise. So whoever Allah wills arises. Then when dawn breaks he says: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let the heedless arise."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said that when a third of the night has passed, a caller cries out: "O seeker of good, draw near. O seeker of evil, hold back. Is there one who repents, that he may be turned to in mercy? Is there one who seeks forgiveness, that he may be forgiven? Is there one who asks, that his request may be granted? O Allah, grant every one who spends a replacement, and grant every one who withholds ruin."

This call goes out every night from the passing of a third of the night until morning, and on the night of Friday from the first of the night to its last. He said: when a third of the night remains, the gates of heaven are opened, and the angel calls out in this way until morning.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "The people of the highest ranks are set level in the Garden upon dappled horses whose bridles are of gold and whose saddles are of pearl and ruby. They pass by those who are below them, who say: Our Lord, by what have You brought Your servants to this honor? And it is said to them: They used to rise in the night while you slept, and they used to fast in the day while you ate, and they used to fight while you shrank in cowardice, and they used to spend in the way of Allah while you were miserly."

On the subject of prostration in the Quran, Allah Almighty said: ﴿ وَإِذَا قُرِئَ عَلَيْهِمُ الْقُرْآنُ لَا يَسْجُدُونَ ﴾ [al-Inshiqaq: 21], meaning at the places of prostration within it. It is narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that there are fifteen prostrations in the Quran. Whoever recites them, or hears them recited, should prostrate. Some of these prostrations are obligatory, such that omitting them at those points is not permitted; these will be identified as they appear.

The first prostration of the Quran falls at the end of Surah al-Araf. Then, in Surah al-Ra'd, at "and their shadows in the mornings and evenings." In Surah al-Nahl, at "and they do what they are commanded." In Surah al-Isra, at "and it increases them in reverence." In Surah Maryam, at "they fell down prostrate and weeping." In Surah al-Hajj, two prostrations occur: one at "Allah does what He wills," and another at "and do good, that you may succeed." In Surah al-Furqan, at "and it increases their aversion." In Surah al-Naml, at "the Lord of the mighty Throne." In Surah al-Sajdah, at "and they are not arrogant" — this is one of the obligatory prostrations. In Surah Sad, at "and he bowed low and turned in repentance." In Surah Fussilat, at "if it is He whom you worship" — also obligatory. At the end of Surah al-Najm, likewise obligatory. In Surah al-Inshiqaq, at "and when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate." And at the end of Surah al-Alaq, at "Read in the name of your Lord who created" — this too is obligatory.

Anyone in prayer who hears a reciter deliver a verse of prostration — or has one recited before him — and does not prostrate for it should incline his head in a nod, or he may form the intention of prostration if the verse is one of the obligatory ones. The scholars disliked reciting, within an obligatory prayer, a surah that contains an obligatory prostration, because if the verse is recited, prostration becomes required, even when the verse falls at the surah's close. In that case, he prostrates, then rises and recites the Opening of the Book, then bows and prostrates. For prostrations outside the obligatory category, the worshipper has a choice: he may prostrate or he may leave it.

Whoever recites a verse of prostration while riding prostrates in whatever direction he faces, nodding with his head. The prostration of the Quran carries no takbir, no tashahhud, and no taslim. It is owed by anyone who hears the verse — whether he was listening attentively or reciting it himself — and this holds even if he is not in a state of ablution. While prostrate, he glorifies Allah and asks of Allah Almighty, calling upon Him with whatever he wishes.

## 5. Facing Death: The Rites of the Departed

It is narrated from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that Allah Almighty says: "If I afflict My servant with an illness and he is patient three times and does not complain — and Allah knows best his condition — I will replace his flesh with flesh better than his flesh, and his blood with blood better than his blood. If I take him, I take him into My mercy; and if I heal him, I heal him with no sin upon him."

He (ص) also said that fever is a purification from a Forgiving Lord. And he (ص) said that the groaning of the sick person, if he is patient, is recorded for him as good deeds; but if he panics, only panic is recorded for him, and he receives no reward for it.

The Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said that the sick person remains in the custody of Allah so long as he does not complain to those who visit him, and his misdeeds are wiped away. He (ع) also said that when Allah Almighty afflicts a servant, He removes from him sins in proportion to his illness, and from the fever he endures comes a reward beyond counting. He said further that there is no visiting the sick until after three days, and that women are not visited in illness. And he said that whoever visits a sick person seeking the face of Allah and fulfilling His appointment will be in the garden of Paradise for as long as he sits with the sick person; and when he departs, Allah sends on that day seventy thousand angels who pray for him until nightfall — or, if the visit was at night, until morning. Visiting should therefore be hastened to the early part of the day.

Allah Almighty said: ﴿فِطْرَتَ اللَّهِ الَّتِي فَطَرَ النَّاسَ عَلَيْهَا﴾ [الروم: ٣٠] — the natural disposition of Allah upon which He created mankind, meaning His fashioning of them so. He said that it belongs to the fitrah that the dying person, when death draws near, be turned toward the Qiblah. Narrations from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) record that they commanded this: the dying person is to be laid upon his back and positioned so that his face and the soles of his feet face the Qiblah, if that is possible. If it is not possible, he is placed upon his side with his face toward the Qiblah.

The Imams also commanded that the testimony of faith be recited aloud at the dying person's head — "There is no god but Allah, and that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger" — and that the Verse of the Throne and the two verses following it be recited there as well: ﴿إِنَّ رَبَّكُمُ اللَّهُ الَّذِي خَلَقَ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضَ فِي سِتَّةِ أَيَّامٍ﴾ [الأعراف: ٥٤] to the end of the verse, and the last third of Surah al-Baqarah: ﴿لِلَّهِ مَا فِي السَّمَاوَاتِ وَمَا فِي الْأَرْضِ وَإِن تُبْدُوا مَا فِي أَنفُسِكُمْ أَوْ تُخْفُوهُ﴾ [البقرة: ٢٨٤] to its end.

A supplication is then offered for him: if his appointed term has come, let his affair be made easy, and let his soul be drawn forth into contentment and good pleasure from You; give him glad tidings, forgive him, and have mercy upon him.

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: when one of the believers dies and is prevented from speaking, the Messenger of Allah (ص) comes to him, together with Ali, Fatimah, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn. The Messenger of Allah (ص) says to him: what you once feared, you have now been made safe from; and what you once hoped for stands before you. A door to the Garden is then opened for him, and he sees his dwelling within it. He is told: if you wish to return to the world, you will be returned to it, and its gold and silver are yours. He replies: I have no need of the world, nor of anything in it. And he recites the word of Allah Almighty: ﴿لَهُمُ الْبُشْرَىٰ فِي الْحَيَاةِ الدُّنْيَا وَفِي الْآخِرَةِ﴾ [يونس: ٦٤].

The Messenger of Allah (ص) was asked about the believer — how death may be made hard upon him and how it may be made easy for him — and likewise about the disbeliever. He said that the believing servant has a station in the Garden that no measure of affliction can bring him to, so death comes before he has reached that degree, and death is made hard upon him so that he may reach it. It may also be that Allah, Blessed and Exalted, commands the Angel of Death to draw the believer's soul out from its gentlest side, so that those present see only that death has been difficult for him, when in truth it is a lightening from Allah Almighty upon His servant. Or He may command him concerning the disbeliever, so that the soul is wrenched free in a single pull — like the wrenching of a roasting-spit from wet wool — so that those present see only that it was made easy for him, when in truth a great punishment has already befallen him.

The Messenger said: death is a blessing for the believer. And he (ص) said: the believer is one who finds rest, and one from whom others find rest. The one who finds rest has been relieved of the grief and hardship of this world and has come to the mercy of Allah. As for the one from whom others find rest — that is the disbeliever, from whom the servants of Allah are relieved.

He said: increase the remembrance of the destroyer of pleasures. He was asked: O Messenger of Allah, what is the destroyer of pleasures? He said: death. And he said: are not the believers those who most remember death and most prepare themselves for it?

The Commander of the Faithful, Ali, delivered a sermon and said: "I have never seen a certainty of faith more resembling doubt than the state of this man.
