Every day he bids farewell to the dead and walks in their funeral procession to the graves, yet returns to the delusion of the world and does not cease from its desires and pleasures. Were there for the wretched son of Adam no sin to bring him low and no reckoning to be settled against him, the dead alone would summon him to caution — for death scatters what a man has gathered, disperses his assembly, and orphans his children. He ought, then, to guard himself against the state he is in with the utmost vigilance. Yet people have grown heedless of death with the heedlessness of those upon whom it will never descend, and they have leaned upon the world and its desires with the leaning of those who expect no reckoning and fear no punishment."

---

**Mentioning Grief at the Time of Death, and Condolence**

When Ibrahim, the son of the Messenger of Allah (ص), died, the Prophet ordered Ali to wash him, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) shrouded him. He then said, "O Ali, carry him." Ali carried him to al-Baqi, where the Messenger of Allah (ص) prayed over him. He said again, "O Ali, carry him." Ali carried him to the grave. Then the Prophet said, "O Ali, go down into the grave." Ali descended, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) lowered the child to him. When he saw Ibrahim laid in his place, he wept, and the people wept with him, until the voices of the men rose above the voices of the women. The Messenger of Allah (ص) then forbade them most sternly from wailing, and said, "The eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but do not say what angers the Lord. Indeed we are stricken by losing you, and indeed we sorrow over you, O Ibrahim."

He (ص) said: I do not forbid you to weep, but I forbid you from lamentation and wailing. This weeping is a mercy that Allah Almighty places in the heart of whom He wills among His creation, and Allah shows mercy to whom He wills, and Allah shows mercy only to the merciful among His servants. And he (ص) said: whoever is stricken by a calamity, let him remember his calamity in me, for it is greater than every calamity. And he said: patience is with the first shock, meaning at the moment of bereavement. And he said: whoever loses three of his children and bears their loss for the sake of Allah, they will be a shield for him from the Fire. It was said: and two, O Messenger of Allah? He said: and two.

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) was taken, his household heard one speaking whose words they heard but whose person they did not see. He said: peace be upon you, O people of the House, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings.

﴿كُلُّ نَفْسٍ ذَائِقَةُ الْمَوْتِ وَإِنَّمَا تُوَفَّوْنَ أُجُورَكُمْ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ فَمَن زُحْزِحَ عَنِ النَّارِ وَأُدْخِلَ الْجَنَّةَ فَقَدْ فَازَ وَمَا الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا إِلَّا مَتَاعُ الْغُرُورِ﴾ [آل عمران: ١٨٥].

In Allah there is consolation for every calamity and a replacement for everything that perishes. Hope, therefore, rests in Him alone, and worship belongs to Him alone. The one truly afflicted is the one who is deprived of reward. Peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) used to require of women, when receiving their oath of allegiance, that they would not wail. He (ص) said: three practices of the age of ignorance will not cease among the people until the Day of Resurrection — seeking rain by the stars, impugning lineages, and wailing over the dead. Jafar ibn Muhammad enjoined at his death: do not strike your cheeks for me, and do not tear your garments for me. And the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, wrote to his judge over al-Ahwaz: beware of wailing over the dead in any land in which you hold authority.

---

**Mentioning the Washing of the Deceased**

The Messenger of Allah (ص) charged Ali to undertake his washing and said to him: Jibrail will be with you to turn me, and let al-Fadl ibn Abbas pass by so that he may lower his gaze and hand you the water, for none may look upon my nakedness but you.

Ali washed him, and afterward he said: I was being helped with him, and I felt a hand turning him alongside mine that I could not see. When I began to wash him, I wished to remove the shirt from him, but a voice called to me: do not remove the shirt from him. So he was washed in his shirt. Then I wished to lay him face down, but a voice called to me: do not lay him face down. So I turned him upon his side.

The boy was washed three times:

1. A washing with water and cleansing herb — specifically soapwort, with which the deceased is customarily washed, or whatever serves in its place, such as lote-tree leaf, marshmallow, and the like.
2. A washing with water and camphor.
3. A washing with pure water.

This is the manner in which the deceased is to be washed, according to the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt: three washings — with water and lote-tree leaf, with water and camphor, and finally with pure water alone. Throughout, the body is turned upon its side. It is not laid face down, nor is it propped upright in the manner common people practice when they seat the deceased.

The washing of the deceased follows the same form as the ritual washing from major impurity. The washer begins with the hands, then attends to the private parts, wrapping a cloth around his own hand for that purpose. He presses the belly gently while pouring water over the private parts, so that whatever remains inside is expelled. He washes the face three times and the hands up to the elbows, wipes the head and the feet, but introduces no water into the mouth or nose. When wiping the head, he wipes the ears as well. He then pours water over the whole body, passing his hand across it, and turns the deceased so that water reaches beneath him.

The deceased is washed unclothed, with a wrapper covering from the navel to the knees; washing him in a garment is also permissible. The washer cleans away any smear or filth, scrubs the soles of the feet with a shard or stone if dirt is upon them, and removes whatever grime has gathered beneath the nails. The head and beard are washed with lote-tree leaf, and care is taken to remove every trace of impurity and defilement. If the water must be heated for the washing, it is heated. The whole washing is performed three times in the manner described.

The washer must conceal the nakedness of the deceased, handle him gently, and keep secret whatever he observes. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: any Muslim man who washes his Muslim brother — without exposing his nakedness, without recoiling at what he finds, and without speaking ill of him — then walks in his funeral procession, prays over him, and remains until he is laid in his grave, emerges from that day fragrant, cleansed of his sins.

The Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (ص) commanded that all Muslims be washed — the disobedient and the upright alike. They permitted a person in a state of major ritual impurity, and a menstruating woman, to wash the dead. A man washes men, and a woman washes women; a wife may wash her husband and a husband may wash his wife when necessity demands, washing from behind a garment. If a woman dies among men, or a man among women, and no mahram (مَحْرَم) is present or can be found to wash the body, it is buried without washing.

The martyr is buried in the garments in which he fell and is not washed; his weapons, leather, and fur are removed from him. This applies when he dies on the field of battle. If he is carried away, reaches his people, and dies among them, he is washed and shrouded in the ordinary manner.

The drowned, the burned, and any severed limb of a person — where the loss of that limb would itself be fatal — are washed and buried. Whatever falls from the deceased is washed and placed with him. If limbs, or parts of them, become separated from the body, each is washed individually, set in its proper place, and the body is then shrouded and buried as a whole.

It is recommended to hasten the burial of the deceased. The exceptions are the drowned, the one struck senseless, and anyone whose death remains in doubt — in such cases, burial waits until death is established with certainty.

If a man dies in a state of major ritual impurity, or a woman dies while menstruating or in a state of postpartum bleeding, washing the body suffices for them, just as it suffices for any who die in a state of purity.

**Mentioning the Embalming and the Shrouds**

The Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have taught that when the washer has finished washing the deceased, he should dry the body with a cloth, then take a length of cloth sufficient to wrap around the loins and spread cotton upon it. He sets the embalming camphor over the cotton. With gentleness and modesty, he removes the cloth that had been covering the deceased's nakedness, binds the prepared cloth around the loins, and places the cotton he has spread over the private parts and the seat, binding it securely. He then dresses the deceased in a shirt, if a shirt forms part of the shroud or is among the garments set aside for him. If no shirt is available, the deceased is left as he is upon the washing-place.

The shrouds are then spread out and their edges set in order. The washer lifts the deceased and lays him within them. Embalming is applied to the places of prostration: the forehead and nose, the palms of both hands, and the fronts of the knees and feet. The folds of the shroud are drawn over him, and he is wound with a turban — its middle set upon his head and brought back beneath his chin, its tail allowed to fall upon his chest over the shrouds. Embalming is then applied to his head, his ears and eyes, his nose, his beard, and his chest. Sweet-scented substances are placed within the embalming; however, wars and saffron are not used in the embalming of men, though both are used for women. The Commander of the Faithful, Ali, placed in the embalming of the Messenger of Allah (ص) the musk that the Messenger had charged him to use, and whatever of that musk remained, the Messenger of Allah (ص) instructed Ali to place in his own embalming at the time of his death. Camphor is also placed in the embalming.

The shrouds and the washing place are perfumed with incense, though the censer is not carried behind the bier. One in a state of ritual consecration is kept away from all scent, just as scent is kept away from such a person while living. It is recommended that the number of winding-sheets be odd — three, five, or seven — and no fixed upper limit is set. A single garment suffices as a shroud, as it suffices for prayer. The scholars held it disliked for a man to be shrouded in pure silk, yet permitted shrouding in dyed garments. The cost of the shroud is drawn from the principal of the estate the deceased leaves behind.

Once the deceased has been shrouded, he is placed upon a bier if one can be found and carried on the shoulders of men wherever this is possible. This is what the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt transmitted. If no bier is available, he may be carried on an animal or by whatever means are at hand, and his shroud may be covered over with a garment. Over a woman's bier a coffin is set in the form of a dome and draped with a garment; the body is tied down if there is any fear it might slip.

The procession moves at a brisk and steady pace — neither hurried into disorder nor drawn out slowly as the Jews did. Whoever wishes to take a turn bearing the bier begins by lifting its front right side onto his right shoulder, then moves around it if he wishes, and the others take their turns in turn. Adorning the coffin is disliked. Walking ahead of the funeral procession is likewise disliked; walking behind it is what is commanded.

When the procession reaches the graveyard, the bier is set down crosswise, with the deceased lying on his back, his right side toward the qibla and his left toward the one who will pray. The prayer leader stands over the bier and pronounces five takbirs, raising his hands with each one, then gives the salutation to his right and to his left, as in the ritual prayer.

- In the first takbir, he praises Allah and extols Him as He is worthy.
- In the second, he prays upon the Prophet and his family.
- In the third, he supplicates for the deceased, striving earnestly in that supplication if he knew him.

If he knew the deceased, he says: "O Allah, this is Your servant and the son of Your servant. He has departed from this world and come to You, and we know of him only good, and I have come as an intercessor for him." He then prays for him with whatever supplication he is able.

If he did not know the deceased, he says: "O Allah, You know best his secret and his intention. If he was a doer of good, then increase him in his good and have mercy upon him; and if he was a sinner, then forgive him and pardon him." He labors in supplication and continues: "Our Lord, You have encompassed all things in mercy and knowledge, so forgive those who repent and follow Your way," to the end of the verse, "and that is the great triumph."

If he wishes, he may also say: "O Allah, You know this servant of ours better than we do, so gather him with those he loved, and give his charge to the one who had authority over him," and the like of this.

If the deceased is a child, the supplication runs: "O Allah, make him a forerunner for his parents, a reward and a treasure laid up in advance, and let them and us profit through him." One supplicates in such a case with whatever one is able. In the fourth takbir, one supplicates for the believing men and women; in the fifth, one prays upon the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) and his family, then withdraws. Everything is kept quiet except the takbir, which is announced aloud. If the prayer is offered over the deceased at night, the voice is raised, as it is raised in the night prayers.

A second narration comes from Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع). He said: the one praying over the deceased praises Allah and extols Him in the first takbir, prays upon the Prophet and his family in the second, supplicates for the Imam in the third and for the believers in the fourth, supplicates for the deceased in the fifth if he is someone known, and then prays upon the Prophet and his family and gives the salutation. He said: in like manner, the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) once pronounced only four takbirs over one he prayed over from among the hypocrites — one who did not deserve supplication. They departed after the fourth takbir, made no supplication for him, and left his corpse cast aside.

The funeral prayer may be offered at any hour of the day or night, except at the precise moment of sunrise and sunset, though praying it by day is preferable. Funerals should not be prayed over at night unless necessity demands it, for it has been transmitted from the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) that he said: "Bury your dead by day, for the angels of the day are more merciful than the angels of the night."

When the ruler is present, or one whom the ruler has appointed to lead the congregational funeral prayer, he has a stronger right to come forward and lead than the deceased's guardian. Where no ruler is present, the right passes to the nearest of the guardians, then to the next nearest.

The funeral prayer is offered over the following:

- the martyr
- those guilty of grave sins and transgressions
- one put to death by a prescribed legal penalty or in retaliation
- a child born of adultery
- a severed limb, provided it is known that its owner cannot survive once it is parted from the body

If it is not known that the owner cannot survive, no prayer is offered over the limb separately from the rest of the body.

When several funerals are brought together, the leader may pray over them all at once, ranging them in a single row — standing in the middle if all are men or all are women. Where only one body is present, he stands at the chest of a man and at the head of a woman. Praying over them one by one is equally permitted.

When funerals of men and women are gathered together, the women are placed nearest the qibla and the men beyond them. The same arrangement applies when there is a single man and a single woman: the woman nearest the qibla, the man beyond her.

Whoever joins the leader after the takbir has begun enters the prayer with him, begins with whatever supplication belongs at that point, and follows the leader's takbirs. When the leader has finished, the latecomer makes up what he missed, then gives the salutation and withdraws — precisely as he would when arriving late to the ritual prayer.

Whoever does not reach the leader before the salutation has missed the funeral prayer. He may still supplicate for the deceased if he wishes, but the prayer itself is not offered.

As for the burial itself, the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have commanded that graves be dug for the dead and forbidden that they be dug deeper than three cubits. A niche — the lahd — is then cut into the side of the grave, or a trench — the darih — is dug down its middle, whichever is preferred. The lahd is a recess hollowed out on the side facing the qibla, where the body will lie; the ground behind it, toward the rear of the grave, is left standing so that the one who cuts the niche has somewhere to stand as he works. The darih is a hollow running down the center of the grave, with a ledge left on either side of it. Either form is permitted and acceptable, and the Imams allowed the niche to be lined when there is need for it.

When the deceased is brought to his grave and laid down at its edge on the side facing the qibla, his guardians descend into the grave — or those whom they appoint to lower him. The one closest to a man takes his place at the front; the one closest to a woman takes her place at the rear. They descend into the grave, then come up again from the side of the deceased's feet and receive him, taking hold of him with their own hands. If the deceased is a woman, a garment is spread over the grave to screen her as she is lowered, until she is hidden from view.

The body is laid upon its right side, set evenly — neither tilted onto its face nor onto its back. The knots tied upon the shroud are loosened, and those present supplicate for the deceased with whatever supplication comes to them.

Unbaked bricks are then set up over him, or planks, or palm branches if that is what is available; and if the earth is heaped over him directly, that too is good. The earth is pressed down over him from above, and those who descended to prepare the niche climb out. The earth taken from the grave is returned into it, and whatever remains is left mounded upon it. Water is sprinkled over it from above, and then the people withdraw.

When two or more of the dead must share a single grave, they are buried together, with the most excellent placed nearest the qibla. When men and women are buried together, the men are positioned in front of the women, and a little earth is laid between each pair to divide them. The scholars regarded moving the dead from one place to another as disliked, and were severe in the matter.

Their direction was that the grave be made square and level, not raised into a peaked mound. They permitted it to be marked with a sign so that it may be recognized, and permitted it to be plastered, whitened, and covered with a roof. They also permitted the grave to be visited from time to time across the years, with mercy sought for the deceased and forgiveness asked on his behalf. Whoever enters the burial ground was commanded to greet the people of the graves and to supplicate for them all. Stepping across the graves, laughing beside them, praying between them, and relieving oneself among them were all forbidden.

If a polytheist woman dies carrying in her womb a child fathered by a Muslim, and that child has not yet been delivered, her own people take charge of her burial and lay her in their graveyards. If a woman dies with a living child still stirring in her womb, there is no harm in a midwife reaching in with her hand and delivering the child without opening the mother's body, provided she is able to do so. If she cannot, the belly is opened and the child is brought out. Should no midwife be available, the closest male relative may perform the delivery in her place. This, however, is done only when the child's life is certain — that it has reached six months or more, the age at which a delivered infant is known to survive. If that condition is not met, the child is left, and the mother is not to be buried while it still stirs within her. Likewise, if the child dies in the womb and the mother's life is endangered by it, the midwife reaches in, severs the child, and removes it — for a belly has been opened before now for causes less urgent than a child, and the mother has emerged alive. To open the body of a dead child is more fitting than to cause the death of a living mother.

It was held proper that food be sent to the family of the deceased for as long as they remain in their grief, this being done by their neighbors and those nearest to them. Grief so occupies the bereaved that they are kept from preparing food for themselves. The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) commanded his household to prepare food for the family of Jafar ibn Abi Talib when news of his death reached them. Al-Numan said: "There has come upon them that which occupies them and turns them away from seeing to this for themselves."

## 6. The Due of Wealth: Zakat

The chapters of zakat open with the command to give it, the encouragement toward it, and the severity that attends its withholding. Allah the Almighty said: "Successful indeed is he who purifies himself, and remembers the name of his Lord, and prays" [al-Ala]. And He said: "Blessed be the believers who are humble in their prayers, and who turn away from ill speech, and who are active in giving zakat" [al-Muminun]. And He said: "Establish the prayer and pay the zakat." And He said: "When the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you find them, and take them, and confine them, and wait for them at every lookout. But if they repent, establish prayer, and pay zakat, then let them go their way" [al-Tawbah: 5]. Allah does not accept the Islam of a polytheist who withholds zakat. And He said: "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay zakat" [al-Fussilat: 6–7].

The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) was asked about this verse — "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay zakat" — and he said: Allah does not reproach the polytheists for it. Have you not heard His words, "Woe to those who pray, who are heedless of their prayers, who make a show of themselves and withhold the maun"? For the maun is zakat. Then he said: By the One in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, none betrays Allah in anything of the zakat of his wealth except a polytheist.

And he (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said: When Allah wills good for a servant, He sends an angel from among the keepers of Paradise, who wipes his breast so that his soul grows generous with zakat. And he said: Whoever gives the zakat of his wealth, let him give it with a willing soul, for he gives only a portion of what Allah has given him, and Allah stores away the rest for him; and whoever gives the zakat of his wealth, its evil has departed from him. And he said: No wealth is ever destroyed on land or sea except through the withholding of zakat from it; so fortify your wealth with zakat, treat your sick with charity, and turn away affliction with supplication. And he said: No one gives the zakat of his wealth and finds his wealth thereby diminished, nor withholds it and finds it thereby increased, nor does any wealth decrease through charity; so give, and do not shrink from it. And he said: Allah the Almighty has tracts of ground called the parched wastes, to which is driven whoever withholds Allah's due from his wealth, so that he is left in want among them.

And he (ع) said: Whoever has camels or cattle or sheep and does not pay their zakat will be laid out flat on the Day of Resurrection upon a barren, level plain, and they will trample him with their hooves and gore him with their horns and bite him with their mouths; whenever the last of them has passed over him, the first of them returns to him, until the reckoning of all creation is finished.

From the Commander of the Believers, Ali (ع), it is related that he said: The worshipper has three signs: prayer, zakat, and fasting. And the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said in his bequest to al-Hasan (ع): I counsel you, O Hasan, and all my children and the people of my house, and whoever among the believers this writing of mine reaches, to fear Allah your Lord. And Allah, Allah, regarding zakat, for it extinguishes the anger of Allah from you.

He also said: Allah the Almighty has made obligatory upon the wealthy, from their wealth, the measure that suffices their poor. If the poor go hungry or naked, it is because of what the wealthy withhold, and Allah the Almighty will hold them to account for that on the Day of Resurrection and punish them severely.

He said further: Whoever amasses wealth and does not pay its due, his wealth becomes a serpent that will devour him on the Day of Resurrection.

And he said: The withholder of zakat is like the devourer of riba, and no one who withholds zakat is a Muslim.

The Master said, concerning the words of Allah the Almighty — "They will be encircled by what they were stingy with on the Day of Resurrection" [Al Imran: 180] — that the wealth on which zakat was withheld is fashioned into the likeness of a bald serpent, which winds about the owner's neck and bites at him until all people have finished their reckoning, saying: "I am your wealth, whose zakat you withheld."

He said further, concerning the words of Allah the Almighty — "And those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in the way of Allah, give them tidings of a painful punishment, on the day when it will be heated in the fire of Hell, and with it will be branded their foreheads, their sides, and their backs. This is what you hoarded for yourselves, so taste what you used to hoard" [al-Tawbah: 34–35] — that the hoard spoken of here is wealth on which zakat has not been paid, while wealth whose zakat has been paid is no hoard. As the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said: on the Day of Resurrection, the wealth of one who withheld its zakat will be brought forth, beaten into plates, heated in the fire of Hell, and with those plates his forehead, his sides, and his back will be branded; whenever they cool, they are heated again and the branding resumed, until the reckoning of all creation is finished.

He said: Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth has discharged what was owed upon him in it.

The story that follows the branding passage concerns a man who came to Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) and told him of another man who had complained of being seized with terror in his sleep, as though a woman were pressing a demand upon him and frightening him. Abu Abd Allah (ع) said that this was a man who did not pay the zakat on his wealth. The questioner relayed this to the man, who replied: "By Allah, I do pay it." That answer was brought back to Abu Abd Allah, and he said that if that were so, then the man was not placing it in its proper recipients' hands. The man acknowledged: "As for that, yes."

**The payment of zakat to its rightful recipients, and the prohibition of placing it elsewhere.**

> خُذْ مِنْ أَمْوَلِهِمْ صَدَقَةًۭ تُطَهِّرُهُمْ وَتُزَكِّيهِم بِهَا

Allah the Almighty said to Muhammad, His Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ): "Take from their wealth a charity that will purify them and cleanse them by it" [al-Tawbah: 103]. He then informed him of the places in which it is to be set, saying: "Charity is only for the poor and the destitute, and those who administer it, and those whose hearts are to be reconciled, and for freeing captives, and for those in debt, and in the cause of Allah, and for the wayfarer, an obligation from Allah, and Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise" [al-Tawbah: 60].

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: The poor man is one who is in need but does not beg; the destitute is in even greater hardship and does beg. Those who administer the charity are its collectors, charged with receiving it from the people. Those whose hearts are to be reconciled are men of enmity who nonetheless hold power, standing, and loyalty among their people; such a man is given from the zakat so that he may be won over, and may in turn bring those who follow him toward obedience — as the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ) used to give to tribal chiefs in order to reconcile them, among them Uyayna ibn Husn and al-Aqra ibn Habis. Their like exists in every age, and whenever they do, the imams give to them from the zakat. "In the freeing of captives" refers to manumission; "those in debt" are those burdened with debts; "in the cause of Allah" is in the struggle; and "the wayfarer" is the stranger cut off from his means.

These, then, are the eight categories upon whom Allah the Almighty has made the giving of zakat obligatory. It is not proper that one category receive the whole of it to the exclusion of the others — on this there is no dispute among Muslims. If a man were to name several men by name and set aside a sum for them, and one of those men then claimed the whole of it for himself without passing his companions their share, that would not be lawful. Far less lawful, then, is the conduct of one who has heard the words of Allah the Almighty — "Charity is only for the poor and the destitute, and those who administer it" — through to the naming of all eight categories, and who yet gives it to a single category among them. He is bound to hand it over to those whom Allah has commanded to receive it.

Allah has made it an obligation upon the one entrusted with zakat to place it in its proper positions. He has charged that person with oversight of the funds, with knowledge of the condition of each class of recipients, and with knowing the measure that should be given at every time and season in accordance with whatever befalls the Muslims and Islam. This is something that none can know or fulfill except the Imams, whom Allah the Almighty has charged with the affairs of His servants and set in authority over His earth and His lands.

No one disputes that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) personally received the charities from those present with him, and dispatched agents to collect them from those who were distant, who then brought what they had gathered to him so that he could distribute it as Allah had commanded. Allah made this plain in His Book by naming those who administer charity among its recipients, assigning them a share for their labor — just as He said to His Messenger (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ): "Take from their wealth a charity" [al-Tawbah: 103]. When Allah took the Prophet to Himself, no one disputed that this obligation remained, undiminished by his passing (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ). On the contrary, there was agreement that whoever stands in his place takes charge of receiving the charity as he had received it (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) and directs it to the same appointed channels. The community agreed with Abu Bakr in fighting those who withheld the zakat, declared them people of apostasy, and held their blood lawful. Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, and the Commander of the Believers Ali each took charge of receiving it during their respective times, and no one among the community differed with them or contested it.

When the caliphate passed to the Banu Umayya and the people saw that the rulers were keeping the zakat for themselves, some withheld payment and turned in alarm to the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) who still lived among them, seeking guidance. Every one of those Companions commanded that the zakat be paid to the rulers, and said: upon them is the burden of what they have taken on, and upon you is the burden of what you have taken on; your duty is to hand it over, and theirs is to place it where it belongs. This ruling is related from Aisha, Abu Hurayra, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, Abu Said al-Khudri, and Abd Allah ibn Umar. No contrary word is related from any Companion, nor is any disagreement among them recorded.

When Ibn Umar was pressed with persistent questioning on the matter, he replied: "Woe to you. Tell me: if I were to seize thieves and cut off the hands of some while leaving the rest unpunished, would you be in the right?" They said no. He continued: "And if you handed them to the ruler and he did that, would it be upon you?" They said no. He said: "So too do these matters run. Pay the zakat to them, even if with it they devour the flesh of serpents."

This is the position of the generality and what they transmit from their predecessors — yet today they have abandoned it entirely, without proof or cause offered in its place. In doing so, they have joined the very company they once counted among the people of apostasy for withholding zakat from their rulers, just as those others withheld it from Abu Bakr. The ground for that earlier withholding exists, though this is not the place to set it out, and its full exposition would run long.

The Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) hold that zakat is owed to none but the Imams of guidance from the household of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) — those whom Allah the Almighty has commanded His servants to obey in His Book, and to whom He has entrusted the affairs of His servants. Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth willingly to anyone other than them has not discharged the obligation; he must pay it to the Imam of his age from among them. If, however, the imams of injustice overpower him and take it from him by force, that suffices.

The Commander of the Believers Ali said to one of his agents over al-Jazira: whoever pays the zakat of his wealth to Muawiya and his agents — those whom the cavalry and men of the Imam have kept at bay — that payment is not to be credited to him; it must be taken from him again, for it neither reached its proper recipient nor served as his protection. But where such rulers overpowered a man and took it from him by force, his word is to be accepted and he is not to be pressed further.

The Master said: The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade a man to conceal his zakat from his imam, and declared that doing so is hypocrisy. Allah the Almighty made clear to His servants, through the tongue of His Messenger Muhammad (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), to whom they are to hand their charities. He appointed the imams as trustees over it, since He forbade zakat to them and to no one else. The community is agreed that it is not lawful for the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) nor for the people of his house to receive it.

It is related that al-Hasan ibn Ali, while still a small boy, took a date from the charity dates and put it into his mouth. The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) saw him, hurried to him, drew the date from his mouth with its moisture still on it, and threw it back among the charity dates. Then he said: "We, the people of the house — charity is not lawful for us."

By this, Allah informed the people that the imams are the trusted custodians over zakat, charged with receiving it and placing it where it rightly belongs. That charge belongs to them alone, and to no one else among the people.

In the same way, He assigned to them the fifth (اَلْخُمْس) that He had assigned to the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), as a mark of their trustworthiness and of their standing in the Messenger of Allah's (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) place within his community after him. This belongs to the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) among them, and not to the rest — for the rest of the household are bound, as the wider community is bound, to deliver their charity to the Imam, just as the household of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) were bound to deliver it to him during his lifetime. The right passes from one Imam to the next: an honor transmitted without interruption, a virtue kept whole.

As for one who has denied their right and failed to recognize their trustworthiness, the least that the words of the Imams and his own predecessors bind him to — once Allah makes their affair manifest and gives them dominion over the one who denied them — is that he pay them the zakat of his wealth across every period of his life in which he withheld it. The proofs sufficient to establish this have already been set out, though in brief, for the one whom Allah grants its understanding. Allah guides to His right course.

**The zakat of gold, silver, and precious substances.**

The Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt are reported to have taught that zakat is not due on gold until it reaches twenty mithqals, and a full year must have elapsed while it remained in the possession of its owner; nor is it due on silver until it reaches two hundred dirhams, again with a full year having passed in the owner's possession. When that year has turned, the due on twenty mithqals is half a mithqal, and the due on two hundred dirhams is five dirhams — and this amount has now become a nisab in his hand. Whatever exceeds it is subject to a quarter of a tenth: if the excess came into his possession before the head of the year, it is due at the head of the year, and he pays accordingly. If, at the head of the year, the amount falls short of this measure, no zakat is owed on it; whatever does exceed the measure is assessed by that same reckoning. The two metals are not combined with one another, and no zakat is due on gold or silver until each separately reaches the measure set out above.

If a man holds one hundred dirhams and ten dinars whose value amounts to one hundred dirhams or more, no zakat is owed on that holding until the dinars reach twenty dinars and the dirhams reach two hundred dirhams and a full year has elapsed over them — unless he spends some of it away before the year is out in order to escape the zakat. If he does that, zakat is nonetheless upon him. The case is like that of a man who held two hundred dirhams and, seeing that zakat was about to fall due, spent some of it away to bring the total below the nisab.

Whoever converts some of his silver into dinars to escape the zakat owed on it, or spends some of his dinars for the same purpose, has done what is not permitted.

As for mineral wealth such as pearls and amber, when these are drawn from their source, the fifth is due upon the one who extracts them, and no zakat falls upon what he retains. Once he sells such a thing and the buyer takes possession of it, it becomes like the rest of the buyer's wealth: the buyer assesses its value and pays zakat upon it at the completion of the year, provided it is held for trade. There is no zakat upon jewelry or upon mineral goods taken for personal use — unless a person takes them as a stratagem to flee from zakat. If that is his intent, the intent invalidates the stratagem, and zakat is due.

The same rule governs whatever is drawn from the mines, whether gold, silver, brass, iron, lead, mineral stone, or the like: the fifth is due upon it, and once it passes into circulation it is treated like the rest of one's wealth.

Whoever holds debased coinage pays its zakat in proportion to the pure metal it contains, giving debased coin for debased. If the coin is copper, iron, lead, or some other base metal merely coated over, its value is referred back to what it truly is at its origin, joined to the rest of his holdings, and zakat is paid upon the whole.

Clothing, riding animals, household vessels, and furnishings carry no zakat obligation, provided they are not held for trade and were not acquired as a device for escaping zakat. If they are held for trade, or if they were purchased with wealth on which zakat was already due and then set aside precisely to avoid that obligation, zakat becomes due upon them.

Whoever holds trade goods assesses their value at the head of the year and pays zakat on them, on condition that the goods are present with him or in a place he is not prevented from reaching. The same applies to a debt owed to him: if nothing bars him from it, the year's completion makes zakat due upon it, and it is treated as though it were already in his hands. If, however, he can recover the debt only through litigation — and the debtor refuses him — he pays no zakat on it until the money actually reaches him.

There is no zakat upon the wealth of a child until he reaches maturity, takes possession of his property, and a full year passes over it in his keeping. Yet if that wealth passes into the hands of an adult who trades with it, zakat becomes due, and the obligation falls upon the one who trades; any loss is his to bear, while any profit belongs to the child. A woman's dowry follows the rule of a debt, as has already been explained.

Whoever dispatches the zakat of his wealth to deliver it, and it is lost before it reaches the place where it belongs, must make it good again — he remains its guarantor until he places it where it is due.

It is taken from the principal of his estate, whether or not he directed it by bequest, so long as the obligation is known and established.

On the zakat of camels, cattle, and sheep, the Imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ have transmitted that working camels — those used to carry loads or to draw water for irrigation — owe no zakat, regardless of their number. Grazing camels, however, those kept at pasture for their increase and their milk, are subject to the following schedule:

- Fewer than five: nothing is due.
- Five, once a full year has passed over them: one sheep is due; nothing more is owed on what exceeds five until the next threshold is reached.
- Ten: two sheep.
- Fifteen: three sheep.
- Twenty: four sheep.
- Twenty-five: a *bint makhad* is due — a she-camel that has completed her first year and entered her second, her mother having conceived again after her and now being in labor, not yet delivered. If no *bint makhad* is available, an *ibn labun* suffices: a male that has completed two years and entered his third, whose mother conceived after him and has since given birth, so that she is now giving milk. This holds up to thirty-five.
- Thirty-six: a *bint labun* is due, up to forty-five.
- Forty-six: a *hiqqah* is due — a she-camel that has completed three years, entered her fourth, and become fit to carry a foal and to be covered. This holds up to sixty.
- Sixty-one: a *jadhaa* is due — a she-camel that has completed four years and entered her fifth. This holds up to seventy-five.
- Seventy-six: two daughters of *labun* are due, up to ninety.

If one more camel is added, two *hiqqas* are due, up to a hundred and twenty. Beyond that, for every forty a *bint labun* is due, and for every fifty a *hiqqah*. If the assessor does not find among the herd the exact age that is owed, he takes the age below it and adds two sheep or twenty dirhams, or he takes the age above it and returns two sheep or twenty dirhams.

There is no zakat on working cattle — those used for plowing or drawing water — nor on grazing cattle kept for increase and milk, until the herd reaches thirty. When it reaches thirty and a full year has passed under their owner's possession, a *tabi* or *tabia* is due: the yearling whose horns have grown even. Nothing is owed on what exceeds thirty until the number reaches forty, at which point a *musinnah* is due. The schedule then runs as follows:

- Forty to sixty: one *musinnah*
- Sixty: two *tabi*'s
- Seventy: one *musinnah* and one *tabi*
- Eighty: two *musinnah*s
- Ninety: three *tabi*'s
- A hundred: one *musinnah* and two *tabi*'s

This pattern continues: for every thirty, a *tabi*; for every forty, a *musinnah*. Nothing is owed on any number that falls between these counts.

No zakat falls on domestic sheep — those kept and fed within the household rather than sent out to graze. Zakat is due on a grazing flock once it reaches forty head and a full year has passed in the owner's keeping. At forty, one sheep is owed. Nothing further is owed on any increase until the flock reaches a hundred and twenty; if it exceeds that by even one, two sheep are due. That rate holds until two hundred; if the flock exceeds two hundred by even one, three sheep are due. Beyond that, as the flock grows, one sheep is due for every hundred.

As for what the workers of charity are to do in the districts they oversee, and what they are to collect, and how they are to handle the wealth of those whose holdings are mingled — the following has been narrated from the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, who appointed Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym al-Azdi over the zakat of Bakr ibn Wail and wrote him a covenant. In it he said:

This is what Ibn Abi Talib charged upon Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym when he sent him to gather the charity. He commanded him to fear Allah, his Lord, in all his affairs — and in the hidden acts of his conduct, where no witness stands over him but Allah and no watchful guardian exists besides Him. He commanded him not to let his outward obedience to Allah differ from what he keeps concealed, for whoever does that has fallen into treachery, has made light of the right of Allah, and has exposed himself to His displeasure.

He commanded him to conduct himself with justice among those placed in his charge, to deal fairly with them in what is apportioned, to meet them with an open face and a gentle manner, and to secure for the wronged among them redress from the one who wronged them. He commanded him to hold the balance even among them — in his seat, his countenance, his bearing, and his leave to approach — so that no man among them might perceive that another was favored over himself.

He commanded him not to revile them, nor to scowl upon them, nor to turn away from them out of any sense of superiority derived from the authority he held over them. They are brothers in religion, helpers against the enemy, and aids in the drawing out of what is owed.

The Master said: "You have in this charity, O Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym, a portion and an appointed right, and you have partners in it: the poor, the needy, and the weak of household. If they render you your right, then render them theirs; and if you do not, you will be among the people most beset by adversaries and misery on the Day of Judgment. Woe to the man whose adversary is the poor, the destitute, the ruined, and the wayfarer."

It is narrated that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade that people be made to swear oaths over their charity. They are to be trusted — trusted regarding whatever of their livestock is hidden and out of sight — while what is openly visible is taken from them. He further said that the one who transgresses in the collection of charity is like the one who withholds it, and he forbade all harsh treatment of people in the matter of zakat.

The Commander of the Believers, Ali, said: the zakat of people who live in open country is collected where they are; they are not to be driven from place to place. In a year of drought, they are granted a delay until the land turns green again.

It is also narrated that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade two manipulations in the matter of charity: joining what is separate, and separating what is joined.

Separating what is joined works as follows: a man owns a hundred and twenty sheep, on which only one sheep is due until the number grows further. The assessor is forbidden to break that flock into groups of forty and extract three sheep from them. Equally, if a man owns forty sheep, he may not be required to divide them into two lots of twenty in order to escape any liability.

Joining what is separate works as follows: three men each own forty sheep independently, with no partnership between them, and three sheep in total would be due if each were assessed alone. The assessor may not combine their flocks and take a single sheep as though they were one holding.

Equally, the assessor may not combine the flocks of two men when neither separately owes zakat, so that by joining them a liability is created. Partners in livestock are not charged zakat until each of them individually holds enough to make zakat due. When that condition is met and their shares are equal, the assessment is taken from them accordingly; if one holds more than the other, they settle the difference between themselves by mutual adjustment.

The principle may be illustrated as follows. Two men share a flock on which nothing is due until it reaches eighty animals. Once it reaches eighty, two sheep are due — one upon each partner — provided the flock is divided equally between them. But if one owns a third and the other two-thirds, only a single sheep is due, falling upon the owner of the two-thirds share, and this remains the case until the flock reaches a hundred and twenty. When one more animal is added, two sheep become due, and the two partners settle the difference between themselves. The assessor took two sheep from the whole flock, while one partner owned forty animals and the other owned eighty. He therefore took from the owner of two-thirds one sheep and a third, although that partner's true liability was only one sheep; and from the owner of forty he took a third of a sheep, although a full sheep was owed by that partner. The result comes to rest at one sheep upon each, with the shortfall in each partner's share made good against what the assessor had taken from the flock as a whole.

The same principle governs all forms of wealth on which zakat falls — coin, camels, cattle, sheep, grain, or any other category — when that wealth is held jointly. Each partner owes only what he would have owed had he held his share alone, because partnership neither dissolves ownership nor transfers any portion of it from one hand to another. Every partner holds exactly what is his; whatever was due from him before the partnership remains due from him after it. And when men of wealth pool their holdings without a true partnership existing between them, each is assessed only on what belongs to him, and no account is taken of the commingling itself.

When a person holds a quorum of wealth on which zakat has become due and a full year passes over it, he pays what is owed. Should an addition then come to that wealth, he joins it to the principal at the head of the second year and pays zakat on the whole together. From coin, he gives a quarter of a tenth. For livestock, any increase is reckoned within the count of the herd. If a mother gives birth while the assessor is present among the flock, he does not count the newborn, nor take it, nor take its equivalent in charity. From camels and cattle he takes only the ages that have been described; from sheep he takes only the full-grown. He does not take the aged, the defective, the male, the choicest, or the poorest of the flock — he takes from the middling. Should the assessor and the owners of the wealth fall into dispute, the owners set apart one third of the flock, and the assessor chooses from the remaining two thirds whatever is due and takes it.

On the zakat of grains and fruits, God — mighty and majestic — has said: ﴿وَهُوَ الَّذِى أَنشَأَ جَنَّاتٍ مَّعْرُوشَاتٍ وَغَيْرَ مَعْرُوشَاتٍ وَالنَّخْلَ وَالزَّرْعَ مُخْتَلِفًا أُكُلُهُ وَالزَّيْتُونَ وَالرُّمَّانَ مُتَشَابِهًا وَغَيْرَ مُتَشَابِهٍ كُلُوا مِن ثَمَرِهِ إِذَا أَثْمَرَ وَآتُوا حَقَّهُ يَوْمَ حَصَادِهِ﴾ [Al-Anam: 141].

Jafar ibn Muhammad, commenting on the words of God — mighty and majestic — ﴿وَآتُوا حَقَّهُ يَوْمَ حَصَادِهِ﴾ [Al-Anam: 141], said that this verse points to something over and above the obligatory charity: a portion given at the time of harvest to whoever asks, consisting of a handful — that is, a fistful — along with whatever is given in charity beside the imposed obligation.

It is also narrated from him, through his forefathers, that the Messenger of God (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) made it unambiguously clear that a tenth is due on everything watered by the sky or by rivers, and half a tenth on everything watered by wells.

From the household it has been transmitted that a tenth is due on all grains once they have set and ripened. At that point they are appraised at first appearance, and their owners are given a fair estimate. The owners are then left free to manage their crop and are held liable for a tenth of what was estimated. The one charged with making the estimate is instructed not to press it to its absolute limit, but to leave some margin aside.

They said: the Messenger of God (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) sent Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari to the people of Khaybar, having appointed him as his agent among them on the basis of a half-share, when the fruit began to ripen. He assessed the crop, and they disputed his figure. Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari said to them:

"I have made my estimate and have not laid upon you more than is right. If you wish, render to us half of what we have estimated; and if you wish, hand it over to us and take half of it."

The Jews said: "This is the truth by which the heavens and the earth stand."

As for honey: if it is held for trade, zakat is due upon it. What is purchased for consumption, or drawn from the combs and cut from the hives, carries no zakat.

On the zakat of the breaking of the fast, Allah, mighty and majestic, has said: ﴿قَدْ أَفْلَحَ مَن تَزَكَّى وَذَكَرَ اسْمَ رَبِّهِ فَصَلَّى﴾ [Al-Ala]. Jafar ibn Muhammad, explaining the words "He has prospered who purifies himself," held that they refer to one who gives the zakat of the breaking of the fast; and that "and mentions the name of his Lord and prays" refers to the festival prayer. He taught that the zakat of the breaking of the fast is a duty upon the rich and the poor alike.

The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said that the charity of the breaking of the fast is due upon every soul — for himself and for all his dependents, young and old, free and enslaved — a sa of food from each one of them.

It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad that a man pays the zakat of the breaking of the fast on behalf of his Jewish and Christian slave. It is further narrated from him that if the slaves of a man's wife are among his dependents, he pays on their behalf; if they are not among his dependents, the wife must pay on their behalf.

He used to pay the zakat of the breaking of the fast on behalf of his father after his father's death, and this is a voluntary good, not an obligation. He also said that one who cannot find food gives in its place dirhams to the value current at that time, and that the way of the sunnah is to pay the zakat of the breaking of the fast before the festival prayer.

The zakat on honey follows the same principle that applies to merchandise: if honey is held for trade, zakat is due on it. What a man buys for his own use, draws from his own hives, or cuts from his own combs carries no zakat.

As for the zakat al-fitr, Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has said: ﴿قَدْ أَفْلَحَ مَن تَزَكَّىٰ وَذَكَرَ اسْمَ رَبِّهِ فَصَلَّىٰ﴾. Jafar ibn Muhammad explained this verse: the words "He has indeed succeeded who purifies himself" refer to the one who pays the zakat al-fitr, and "and remembers the name of his Lord and prays" refers to the Eid prayer.

He held that the zakat al-fitr is obligatory upon the rich and the poor alike. The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said that the sadaqat al-fitr is due upon every person — on behalf of himself and on behalf of all his dependents, young and old, free and slave — one sa of food for each.

It is related from Jafar ibn Muhammad that a man pays the zakat al-fitr on behalf of his slave whether that slave is a Jew or a Christian. It is also related from him that if a man's wife has slaves counted among his dependents, he pays on their behalf; if they are not among his dependents, the obligation falls upon the wife.

He used to pay the zakat al-fitr on behalf of his father after his father's death. This is a voluntary act of goodness, not an obligation.

He said that whoever cannot find food should give dirhams in its place, reckoned at the value current at the time. The way of the sunnah is to pay the zakat al-fitr before the Eid prayer.

## 7. The Fast: Ramadan and the Discipline of Abstention

Fasting in the month of Ramadan is an obligation in its own right. It is fitting, therefore, to set out what the fast is, what is sought within it, how it begins, what breaks it, and how it is observed during travel and illness.

**On fasting and what is desired in it**

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days," continuing to His words, "so that you may complete the number and glorify Allah for having guided you, that perhaps you will be grateful." [al-Baqarah]

By this, Allah Almighty made fasting the month of Ramadan a yearly obligation upon every person of legal responsibility. The chapters on prayer contain what has already been said regarding what boys are commanded in respect of prayer and fasting, and the circumstances under which that command applies.

It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) that when the month of Ramadan drew near, he would supplicate in these words: "O Allah, the blessed month of Ramadan has come. If You bring us to it, help us through it and accept it from us." And when the month entered, he would say in his supplication: "O Allah, this is the month of Ramadan in which the Quran was sent down as guidance for the people, and as clear signs of guidance and the Criterion. O Allah, bless us in the month of Ramadan, help us through it, and accept it from us, for You are capable of all things."

It is narrated from his father (ع) that he said: "Do not say Ramadan alone, but say as Allah Almighty said, the month of Ramadan." And from his forefathers, that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Shaban is my month, and Ramadan is the month of Allah, and it is the springtime of the poor." This points to its excellence. All months belong to Allah Almighty, yet the month of Ramadan is attributed to Him specifically as an honor, and the saying "Shaban is my month" likewise reflects an honoring of that month.

The Prophet (ص) used to fast. He ascended the pulpit and said: "O people, Jibril came to me and said, O Muhammad, whoever reaches the month of Ramadan and is not forgiven in it, then dies, may Allah keep him far away. Say Amin." So I said, "Amin."

On the last day of Shaban he (ص) addressed the people and said: "O people, a great and blessed month has cast its shade over you, a month in which one night is better than a thousand months. Whoever draws near to Allah in it through any quality of goodness is as one who performs an obligatory duty in another month, and whoever performs an obligatory duty in it is as one who performs seventy obligatory duties in another month. It is the month of patience, and the reward of patience is Paradise. It is the month of consolation, a month in which the sustenance of the believer is increased. Whoever provides a fasting person with what breaks his fast earns forgiveness of his sins and the freeing of his neck from the Fire, and he receives the like of that fasting person's reward, without anything being diminished from the fasting person's reward."

Some of them said, "O Messenger of Allah, not all of us find what to give a fasting person to break his fast with." He (ص) replied that Allah Almighty grants this reward even to one who breaks a fasting person's fast with a draft of milk, a date, or a drink of water. Whoever satisfies a fasting person, Allah will give him to drink from the Basin — a drink after which he will never thirst until he enters Paradise.

The month has three parts: its first is mercy, its middle is forgiveness, and its last is freedom from the Fire. Whoever lightens the burden of the one his right hand possesses in it, Allah forgives him and frees him from the Fire.

The Master then said: "So increase in it four qualities — two by which you will please your Lord, and two you cannot afford to be without.

- As for the two by which you please your Lord: the testimony that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner, and that you seek His forgiveness.
- As for the two you cannot do without: that you ask Allah for Paradise, and that you seek refuge in Him from the Fire."

> إِنَّمَا ٱلصَّدَقَتُ لِلْفُقَرَآءِ وَٱلْمَسَكِينِ وَٱلْعَمِلِينَ عَلَيْهَا وَٱلْمُؤَلَّفَةِ قُلُوبُهُمْ وَفِى ٱلرِّقَابِ وَٱلْغَرِمِينَ وَفِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ وَٱبْنِ ٱلسَّبِيلِ ۖ فَرِيضَةًۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌ حَكِيمٌۭ

The Prophet (ص) said: "For everything there is a zakat, and the zakat of bodies is fasting." On the words of Allah Almighty, "And seek help through patience and prayer," he said: "Patience is fasting" — and Allah gives the patient their reward without reckoning. He (ص) said to Ali (ع): "O Ali, fast in the heat, complete the ablution fully in the cold — that is, in winter — and teach yourself patience." He also said that those who will enjoy the most rest at the Standing are those who ate the least in this world, and that the hungriest on the Day of Resurrection are those who were the most filled in this world. He (ص) said that Allah Almighty and all His angels send blessings upon those who fast, and he said: "I have not commanded My angels to supplicate for anyone without their supplication being answered."

He (ص) said: "The sleep of the fasting person is worship, and his breath is glorification." He also said that Allah Almighty declares: "Fasting is for Me, and I Myself reward for it. The fasting person has two joys: a joy when he breaks his fast, and a joy when he meets his Lord. By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, the odor of the mouth of the fasting believer is more fragrant to Allah than the scent of musk." When the Prophet (ص) ate at someone's table, he would say: "May the fasting break their fast with you, may the righteous eat your food, and may the angels send blessings upon you." This points to the excellence of providing for the breaking of the fast.

**On the beginning of the fast and entering it**

> أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ لَيْلَةَ ٱلصِّيَامِ ٱلرَّفَثُ إِلَىٰ نِسَآئِكُمْ

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "The pre-dawn meal is a blessing, so take it, even if one of you drinks only a mouthful of water, for Allah has angels who send blessings upon those who take the pre-dawn meal."

The Commander of the Believers, Ali, said: When Allah Almighty revealed, "Eat and drink until the white thread becomes distinct to you from the black thread," people took two threads — one white, one black — and continued eating and drinking through the night until the white thread became distinct from the black. Then Allah Almighty revealed the word "of dawn."

He said: the dawn at which food is forbidden to the fasting person is the dawn whose light spreads across the horizons of the east. The first dawn forbids nothing; it is the one that rises slender and climbs steeply into the sky.

The sunnah is to hasten the breaking of the fast after sunset, and to delay the pre-dawn meal. The Messenger of Allah (ص) forbade that the obligatory fast be observed without a firm intention to fast.

The Commander of the Believers (ع) said: "Allah Almighty made the crescent moons markers for fasting, appointed times for the Hajj, and fixed terms for debts. That is among the blessings of Allah Almighty upon His creation."

Whoever fasts on a day without having sighted the crescent, and then learns that the day did belong to the month of Ramadan, must make it up — for what he performed was a voluntary fast, not an obligatory one. There is no harm, however, in fasting voluntarily on a day about which there is doubt as to whether it belongs to Ramadan, provided the intention is that it belongs to Shaban. If he later learns it was indeed of Ramadan, he makes it up, again because he fasted it voluntarily. An obligatory fast may not be observed on a day of doubt, for Allah Almighty is not to be worshipped in any act of worship on a basis of doubt.

**On what breaks the fast and what is required of one who breaks it**

> أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ لَيْلَةَ ٱلصِّيَامِ ٱلرَّفَثُ إِلَىٰ نِسَآئِكُمْ

Allah Almighty said: "It has been made lawful for you, on the night of the fast, to go in unto your wives," continuing to His words, "then complete the fast until the night." [al-Baqarah] The fasting person is therefore forbidden food, drink, and intercourse during the daytime of the fast — a matter of consensus among Muslims.

It has come from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that he made expiation obligatory upon whoever deliberately breaks the fast or has intercourse during the daytime of Ramadan. The expiation is as follows:

1. Freeing a slave.
2. If that is not possible, fasting two consecutive months.
3. If that too is beyond him, feeding sixty poor persons.

In all cases, he must also make up the day on which he broke his fast. If he can find neither a slave to free nor anything to give in charity, and is unable to fast, then nothing further is required of him, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity except with what He has given it.

The transmitted teaching from the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) concerning a man who fondles his wife during the daytime of Ramadan, or kisses her, or touches her, and emits — is that he owes both making-up the fast and expiation. If he does not emit, nothing is required of him, though a man who fears this outcome for himself ought not to expose himself to the occasion; he should keep his fast pure, guard it from what would corrupt it, and not place it before what would break it. As for a man who goes in to his wife during the daytime of Ramadan while she is asleep, or does so under compulsion, or she is insane, or she is a child — he owes the making-up and the expiation, provided he is a responsible adult. No obligation falls upon the woman when she is in any of the states mentioned.

Whoever has intercourse during a night of Ramadan must not deliberately go to sleep before performing the ritual bath. If he does sleep and rises in a state of major impurity, he owes the making-up of that day. The same applies to one who has a nocturnal emission, wakes, and then sleeps again. If, however, sleep overtakes him against his will, or he became impure and did not wake until dawn, he performs the ritual bath, completes his fast, and nothing is required of him beyond that.

Whoever eats, drinks, or has intercourse forgetfully during the daytime of Ramadan must complete his fast, and no making-up or expiation is owed. The moment he remembers, however, he must stop — spitting out whatever food or drink remains in his mouth and rinsing it. If, after that recollection, he deliberately swallows any of it, or continues the intercourse, he owes both the making-up and the expiation.

One who deliberately vomits during the daytime of Ramadan must make up a day in its place, on account of his laxity toward his fast. Should any of that vomit return to his throat, he owes both the making-up and the expiation. If, however, the vomiting overcame him and he could not restrain it, nothing is required of him.

Whoever rose in the last part of the night and ate or drank while still inside his house — neither going out nor looking to see whether dawn had risen — and dawn had in fact already risen when he ate or drank, must complete his fast and make up a day. But if he had gone out, searched for the dawn, and found no sign of it, then returned and ate or drank while dawn had in fact risen — without intending it, without laxity, and without neglecting to look — nothing is upon him.

Whoever is in doubt about the rising of the dawn and belongs to those who have knowledge of it may eat and drink until he is certain it has risen. Whoever is in doubt but has no such knowledge must refrain until the matter becomes certain, or until someone who does have knowledge of it and has observed it confirms it for him.

Whoever looks at the sun, judges that it has set, and therefore eats or drinks during Ramadan, and then the sun appears to him again, must leave off food and drink until it sets — and nothing is upon him.

When a fasting person applies kohl and its taste reaches his throat, he owes a make-up day — provided he did not know that its taste would reach his throat. If he knew this and applied it deliberately, he owes both the make-up and the expiation. If he applies a kohl whose taste does not reach his throat, nothing is upon him.

The tooth-stick is permitted for the fasting person, unless he uses a moist twig. If he does so and the moisture reaches his throat, his fast is broken. Whoever does this deliberately, or goes beyond it, owes the make-up and the expiation; whoever did not intend it owes the make-up alone. If he uses a dry twig, nothing is upon him.

The fasting person is likewise permitted to:

- Chew gum or food, and taste with his tongue, so long as nothing of it reaches his throat
- Rinse his mouth with water

As for cupping: if vomiting results from it, he makes up a day. If he knows beforehand that cupping will bring this upon him, he ought not to expose himself to it unless compelled — in which case it is permitted.

The fasting person is also permitted to smell perfume, oils, and pleasant scents, though he should not draw any of it in deeply, lest it reach his throat. Likewise, he should not pour into his ear anything that would reach his throat.

---

**On fasting during travel**

A traveler who fasts during Ramadan is obliged to make up those days once he returns home.

The verse that governs this reads: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days, and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days." [al-Baqarah]

God thus prescribed the fast of Ramadan upon every resident who bears legal responsibility, and prescribed upon the traveler a compensating number of days in its place. Whatever days of the month a man travels through, he owes their fast when he is home again.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: The Messenger of Allah (ص) set out on the campaign of Mecca during Ramadan, broke his fast, and commanded those with him to do the same. Word reached him that some of his companions had continued fasting. He said: "Have you not in the Messenger of Allah a beautiful example?" He then halted on the road at a point where any of them could see him clearly, took a cup of water, and drank it in full view of the company. When they saw this, they broke their fast. A group, however, persisted — and the Messenger of Allah (ص) named them the disobedient.

Breaking the fast during the daytime of Ramadan while traveling is therefore permitted, by the Book of Allah Almighty and the Sunnah of His Messenger (ص). Whoever refrains from food and drink while traveling is not counted as fasting and must make up the number of days he abstained, as Allah Almighty has obligated upon him. If a traveler does refrain from food and drink — without intending it as a fast and without counting it as such — and then makes up those days once he is resident, no further obligation rests upon him for having abstained while traveling. This practice is reported from many of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

The chapters on prayer set out which kind of travel obliges both the breaking of the fast and the shortening of prayer; wherever shortening the prayer is obligatory, breaking the fast is likewise obligatory. Deliberately traveling in the month of Ramadan without need is disliked.

The timing of departure and arrival also governs what is owed:

- If a traveler leaves home before the sun's decline from its zenith, he must make up that day.
- If he leaves after the decline, he neither breaks his fast that day nor makes it up.
- If he arrives home before the decline and has tasted nothing, he completes his fast for that day and owes no make-up.
- If he arrives after the decline, he must make up that day.

**On breaking the fast for illnesses that arise**

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days, and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days." [al-Baqarah] I have set out in the chapter before this what follows from the apparent sense of these words of Allah Almighty for one who travels in the month of Ramadan, and the like of it is obligatory for one who is sick in it. Abu Abdillah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the limit of illness at which the fasting person may break his fast, and he said that this is entrusted to the sick person, who is trusted concerning it: if he can bear the fast without harm to himself, let him fast.

**The Fast: Ramadan and the Discipline of Abstention**

**The Dispensation Granted to the Ill**

Allah Almighty said: *"Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may fear God, for a fixed number of days… and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days."* [Al-Baqarah] As was clarified in the chapter preceding this one, it is not to be supposed that Allah intends by this only those who travel during the month of Ramadan, for the same obligation rests equally upon those who are ill during it.

Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the degree of sickness that entitles a fasting person to break the fast. He said that the matter is entrusted to the sick person himself: if fasting harms him, there is no objection to his breaking it, and if it does not harm him, he should fast. Whatever causes harm through fasting is sufficient grounds for breaking it.

The Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, ruled concerning the sick person who cannot fast during Ramadan that he must feed one poor person for every day missed — half a sa of food. He specified that this applies to the sick person who may despair of ever recovering, such as one afflicted with leprosy or a settled, chronic ailment. Likewise, an elderly man who is unable to fast must feed the poor, since he too may despair of ever regaining the ability to make up the missed days. One who expects to recover and regain that ability, however, must make up the missed days once he is able.

The pregnant woman and the nursing woman are treated in the same manner as the sick. If either is unable to fast, she breaks her fast — even should her condition persist until another Ramadan arrives. If she is then able to fast, she fasts and also feeds the poor for the days she broke before that Ramadan. If she is still unable, she breaks that Ramadan's fast as well, discharging the first set of missed days through feeding the poor and the second through fasting once she recovers.

All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt. They held it praiseworthy that whoever dies with obligatory fasting still owed should have those days made up on his behalf. They likewise held that a woman who menstruates during Ramadan and breaks her fast is obligated to make up what she missed. Whoever owes make-up days for Ramadan may observe them as he wishes — consecutively or separately — for Allah has said only, *"then a number of other days."* They disliked making up the days of Ramadan during Dhu al-Hijjah, since that is a month of devotion, and they preferred that missed days be made up as soon as possible after Ramadan itself. Nothing is incumbent upon one who delays the make-up, so long as it is completed before the next Ramadan arrives; but if the next Ramadan comes before the make-up has been performed, then feeding the poor is required in addition, as has been mentioned.

---

**On Breaking the Fast**

Allah Almighty said: *"Then complete the fast until the night."* [Al-Baqarah: 187] The night begins with the setting of the disc — that is, the disappearance of the sun below the horizon at sunset, with no barrier obstructing it. If a barrier does obstruct the view, then the sign of the disc's setting is the darkening of the eastern horizon and the advance of night from that direction, with the redness departing from it.

The traditions transmitted from the Ahl al-Bayt record that they loved to hasten the breaking of the fast and disliked any delay in it. They instructed that a man break his fast after the Maghrib prayer as promptly as he is able, provided food is at hand, and that he not remain fasting without need. Their guidance was this: if food is present and the sun has set, he begins with the food before the prayer; if no food is present, he prays first and then breaks his fast. It is not fitting for him, when food has been set before him, to leave it and rise to pray. He eats first, then rises and prays while the time still remains.

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) broke his fast, he would say: *"O Allah, for You we have fasted, and upon Your provision we have broken our fast, so accept from us. The thirst has gone, the veins are filled, and the reward remains, if Allah wills."*

The traditions further state that if the crescent of Ramadan is sighted during the daytime — whether at the beginning of the day or at its end — the people do not break their fast until night, for that day counts as a day of Ramadan. This ruling applies to a sighting at the end of the month as well. Likewise, if the crescent of the new month is seen during the daytime but had not been sighted the night before, that day is not fasted as part of Ramadan, since a day belongs to a month only when its crescent has been sighted on the night preceding it.

**On Laylat al-Qadr**

> إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ

Allah Almighty said: *"Indeed, We sent it down on the Night of Power,"* to the end of the surah. [Al-Qadr] And He, Blessed and Exalted, said: *"Ha Mim. By the clear Book. Indeed, We sent it down on a blessed night. Indeed, We were warning. In it every wise matter is distinguished, a command from Us. Indeed, We were sending."* [Al-Dukhan]

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع), commenting on the words of Allah Almighty, *"The angels and the Spirit descend in it by the permission of their Lord,"* taught that the angels and the scribes descend to the lowest heaven by the permission of their Lord, and there they write down what is to unfold during the year — the affairs of men and what shall befall the servants. This matter has with Allah a station in which His will operates: He advances what He wills, delays what He wills, and confirms what He wills, and with Him is the Mother of the Book. Jafar ibn Muhammad, commenting on the words *"in it every wise matter is distinguished,"* taught that on that night the affairs of the entire year — all that is to occur within it — are written.

Jafar ibn Muhammad also gave a sign by which the Night of Power may be known: a wind blows on that night, and if the season is cold it grows warm, and if it is hot it grows cool.
