The Imams granted a concession to anyone wearied during the tawaf or the sa'i: he may rest and then resume, building upon what he has already completed. If a prescribed prayer is called while a person is in the midst of tawaf, he stops at the point he has reached, prays, and then resumes from there. He should not perform the obligatory tawaf hastily and without cause, nor should he begin it when he judges that a prayer time is about to enter — nor while the imam is praying and he has not yet finished his tawaf and sa'i. Rather, he delays the tawaf until the obligatory prayer has been performed, and then he circumambulates.

The Imams also permitted the interruption of a voluntary tawaf for some righteous occasion that presents itself. It is narrated of al-Husayn ibn Ali that he was circumambulating the House voluntarily when a man approached him with a need. He broke off his tawaf and went with the man. When questioned about this, he said, "How could I not go with him, when the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) said, 'Whoever walks with his believing brother in his need — even if the need is not fulfilled — has an Umrah recorded for him for the accompanying; and if the need is fulfilled, a Hajj and an Umrah are recorded for him'? I went with this man in his need, and it was fulfilled, so I gained a Hajj and an Umrah — and then I returned to my tawaf."

Whoever is overtaken by drowsiness during the obligatory tawaf or its sa'i and cannot complete it builds upon what he has already done, provided he has passed the halfway point; if he has not, he begins again. If he sets it aside until he has rested and then completes it on his own, no fault attaches to him. When doubt arises during the tawaf, he builds upon what is certain. If a person adds to his rounds and performs eight, he joins six further rounds to that eighth so that he has completed two full weeks — the first being his obligatory tawaf, the second voluntary. He then prays four rak'ahs, two for each week, and performs the sa'i between al-Safa and al-Marwah for seven rounds. He may then perform another seven voluntarily, or leave them. Deliberately joining two weeks together without separating them by two rak'ahs is not permitted, except in the case of forgetfulness already described.

When the pilgrim has completed a week of tawaf around the House, he draws back to the Maqam of Ibrahim, placing it between himself and the House, and prays two rak'ahs. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has said: "And take from the Maqam of Ibrahim a place of prayer" [Al-Baqarah: 125]. These two rak'ahs are to be prayed only from behind the Maqam, with it kept between the worshipper and the House.

Whoever performs the obligatory tawaf after the Asr prayer prays its two rak'ahs at that time. If the tawaf was voluntary, he delays the two rak'ahs until the disliked hour has passed. Likewise, if he performs a voluntary tawaf after the Fajr prayer, he does not pray its two rak'ahs until voluntary prayer becomes permissible again, as noted in the chapters on prayer. If, however, the tawaf was obligatory, he prays its two rak'ahs the moment he finishes, whatever the hour. It is recommended that in these two rak'ahs, after the opening of the Book, he recite Surat al-Kafirun in the first rak'ah and Surat al-Ikhlas in the second. All of this is the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِمْ).

They said: after completing the tawaf and praying its two rak'ahs, he returns to the Black Stone and touches it in reverence, then goes to Zamzam. He drinks of its water and pours some over himself if he is able, then goes out through the gate of al-Safa. There he praises Allah, declares His oneness and His greatness, and supplicates with whatever comes readily to him, while facing the House in the direction of the Black Corner.

It is related from Jafar ibn Muhammad (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ), traced back to the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ), that during the Farewell Pilgrimage, standing upon al-Safa, he said: "There is no god but Allah alone, who has no partner. To Him belongs the dominion and to Him belongs all praise. He gives life and causes death, while He Himself is ever-living and does not die. In His hand is all good, and He is capable of all things. There is no god but Allah alone. He has fulfilled His promise, aided His servant, and defeated the confederates alone." He said this three times. After it, Abu Abdillah would say: "O Allah, I ask You for pardon and well-being in this world and the hereafter. I ask You, O Lord, to send blessings upon Muhammad and his pure household, to strengthen me in acting upon what You have taught me of Your right, and to spread over me some of Your provision. I ask You, O Lord, for good in this world and good in the hereafter, and I ask You to shield me from the torment of the Fire."

The Master said: When the supplication upon al-Safa is complete, one descends and walks at a measured, tranquil pace. Upon reaching the bottom of the valley — at the point marked by the first beacon, the flag that signals the start of the striving — the pace quickens until the belly of the valley has been crossed and the second beacon, the flag marking the end of the striving, is reached. It is for this that the rite is called the sa'i between al-Safa and al-Marwah. The first to perform the sa'i was Ibrahim. When he descended from al-Safa and reached the belly of the valley, Iblis — may Allah curse him — blocked his path. Ibrahim hastened as if fleeing from him until he gained the far edge of the valley on the other side, whereupon the accursed one turned away, and Ibrahim walked on.

The Master said: Among what is recommended to say during the sa'i is: "In the name of Allah; Allah is the greatest; and may Allah send blessings upon Muhammad and his family. O Allah, forgive, have mercy, and overlook what You know, for You are the Most Mighty, the Most Generous." When one leaves the place of quickened pace and returns to an ordinary walking pace, the hands are raised toward the heavens and one says: "O Lord of grace, bounty, favor, blessings, and generosity, my deed is weak, so multiply it for me and accept it from me, and send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy of and as they are worthy of from You."

The Master said that there is no quickened pace for women; they walk throughout. The quickened pace is for the men alone. The pilgrim then ascends al-Marwah and says upon it what he said upon al-Safa, returns to al-Safa and stands upon it likewise, then returns again to al-Marwah — continuing in this way until he has completed seven rounds, beginning with al-Safa and ending with al-Marwah, striving in each round and supplicating as described. If he begins with al-Marwah before al-Safa, that round is discarded and he begins the sa'i afresh, starting from al-Safa and ending at al-Marwah. When the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) ascended al-Safa, he recited the verse, "Indeed al-Safa and al-Marwah are among the rites of Allah" [Al-Baqarah: 158], and then said, "Begin with that which Allah began with."

This is how whoever circumambulates the House should perform the sa'i — joining it to the tawaf, as the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) did. If the two are separated for a valid reason, there is no harm in that: as when the pilgrim is wearied and rests, is pressed for time, or is presented with something he cannot decline. Without excuse, however, he should not delay it.

> وَأَنزَلْنَآ إِلَيْكَ ٱلذِّكْرَ لِتُبَيِّنَ لِلنَّاسِ مَا نُزِّلَ إِلَيْهِمْ

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ) was asked whether the sai between al-Safa and al-Marwah is obligatory, and he said, "Yes." He was asked whether the shortening of prayer while traveling is likewise obligatory, and he said, "Yes." It was said to him: "But Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said, And when you journey through the land, there is no blame upon you if you shorten the prayer, if you fear that those who disbelieve may afflict you [al-Nisa: 101], and He said of Hajj and Umrah that there is no blame upon one who walks between them." He answered that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, made mention of this in His Book, and the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) made it clear by his word and his deed and his command. He is the one whom Allah charged with the clarification, for He said, "And We have revealed to you the Reminder, that you may make clear to the people what has been sent down to them" [al-Nahl: 44], and He made it obligatory upon the people to accept from him, for He said, "And whatever the Messenger gives you, take it, and whatever he forbids you, abstain from it" [al-Hashr: 7].

The verse "Indeed al-Safa and al-Marwah are among the rites of Allah, so whoever makes Hajj to the house or performs Umrah, there is no blame upon him for walking between them" [al-Baqarah: 158] carries proof that the sa'i between them is obligatory. Had its omission been left to free choice, the wording would have been "there is no blame upon him if he does not walk between them." Instead, the phrase "there is no blame upon him for walking between them" arose only because some had regarded it as blameworthy. Allah made clear thereby that the sa'i is among His ordained rites and that no sin attaches to one who performs it. The Ansar had been accustomed to entering the state of consecration for the idol Manat, which stood at the level of Qudayd, and for that reason they were reluctant to walk between al-Safa and al-Marwah. Allah informed them that there was no blame upon them in doing so, and that the two sites are among His rites. In the same way, when the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) commanded the shortening of prayer and the breaking of the fast during travel, some Muslims found both practices weighty and burdensome, and Allah informed them likewise that there was no blame upon them in either.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ) said: Whoever deliberately abandons the sa'i must perform the Hajj again the following year. Whoever forgets it appoints someone to make it up on his behalf, just as one who forgets the two rak'ahs of tawaf appoints someone to make them up.

Concerning the one who joins Umrah to Hajj in the manner of tamattu':

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ) spoke of the one who performs tamattu', joining Umrah to Hajj — meaning whoever brings the two together in a single journey, enters ihram for both at once, and consecrates himself for them together, as was mentioned in the chapter on ihram. He said: when the one performing tamattu' has circumambulated the House and made the sa'i between al-Safa and al-Marwah, intending by them the tawaf and sa'i of the Umrah, he has fulfilled it. He should then release himself from ihram, taking something from his hair and his nails, and keeping the rest for the Hajj.

The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ) commanded likewise in the Farewell Pilgrimage: those with him who had not driven a sacrificial offering released themselves from ihram once they had performed the tawaf and sa'i, and became fully unbound. But he himself (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ), having driven the offering, did not release himself, and he joined the Umrah to the Hajj — in keeping with the word of Allah, "And do not shave your heads until the offering reaches its place of sacrifice" [al-Baqarah: 196], meaning until the offering is slaughtered. It was at that time that Allah sent down the command of tamattu', and the Prophet said: "Had I foreseen from the start of my affair what I now perceive at its end, I would not have driven the offering, and I would have made it a tamattu'." He urged the people toward it, for they had not known this; they had known Umrah only as a rite performed on its own.

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

The Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ), arrived that day from Yemen, having driven an offering. He found Fatimah (عَلَيْهَا السَّلَامُ), who was among those performing Hajj with the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ), wearing dyed garments and already released from her ihram — something that was held against her, though the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ) had himself commanded her to do so. Ali went to the Prophet and informed him of what he had found. The Prophet said, "Yes."

Then he asked, "And you, O Ali — for what did you enter ihram?"

Ali said, "I said: O Allah, I enter ihram for that which Your Messenger has entered ihram."

The Prophet said, "Then do not release yourself until you have sacrificed the offering. Perform the tawaf and the sai for your Umrah, and remain as you are in your ihram until you are released from the Hajj."

Ali then shared with him (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) in his offering. What Ali had brought from Yemen was combined with what the Messenger of Allah had driven, and he was made a partner in the whole of it.

Abu Abdillah Jafar ibn Muhammad (صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ) was asked how the one performing tamattu should handle the shaving of the head. He said that the head is not shaved with a razor; rather, the hair is shortened or trimmed at its edges, with some kept in reserve for the Hajj. He was then asked what happens if the pilgrim does shave. He replied that if the hair has not grown back by the day of sacrifice, a blood offering is owed — a sheep to be slaughtered and its meat given in charity. He added that the razor is passed over the head on the day of sacrifice regardless of whether the hair has grown back, and that the same applies to the bald man, who has no hair: he passes the razor over his head in place of the act of shaving.

The pilgrim performing tamattu must give first attention to the proper ordering of his release. If he forgets to shorten his hair and only remembers after he has already entered ihram for Hajj, he should seek Allah's forgiveness, and nothing further is required of him. Once he has been released from his tamattu, he may travel freely in the interval between that release and his entry into ihram for Hajj. He should not, however, perform any voluntary tawaf after the tawaf of tamattu until he has dissolved his ihram and shortened his hair.

Once he has completed his Umrah, he should neither put on the ihram garments again nor present himself in a manner that resembles those who are still in ihram. The people of Makkah, likewise, should conduct themselves in keeping with the sanctity of the Hajj days; those among them who do not perform Hajj remain in their ordinary state. If the one in ihram comes to his wife before he has shortened his hair, he owes a camel. If he kisses her, he owes a sheep. Once he has shortened his hair, he may come to her freely, and nothing is required of him, for what was forbidden during ihram becomes lawful upon his release from it. If the one performing tamattu arrives in Makkah on the day of Tarwiyah, he performs the tawaf of his Umrah and the sai, walks between the two hills, and then enters ihram for Hajj on that same day.

As for the woman: she is permitted, if she fears that menstruation will prevent her from performing the tawaf and sai of Hajj in time, to perform them before she sets out for Hajj. If she has joined Umrah to Hajj in tamattu and then menstruates before she is able to perform the tawaf of Umrah, she delays it until she becomes pure, and then performs it before the tawaf of Hajj.

The Master said that tamattu is not permitted for the people of Makkah. God Almighty allowed it only for those who travel from distant lands — and that is the meaning of His words: ﴿ذَلِكَ لِمَنْ لَمْ يَكُنْ أَهْلُهُ حَاضِرِي الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ﴾ "That is for one whose family is not present at the Sacred Mosque." [al-Baqarah: 196]. The people of Makkah may perform Umrah at any time they wish. He also said that whoever enters the Sacred Precinct, performs Umrah in one of the months of Hajj, and then remains there until he performs Hajj, has performed tamattu.

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

The one who performs tamattu, joining Umrah to Hajj, owes what God Almighty named: ﴿فَمَا اسْتَيْسَرَ مِنَ الْهَدْيِ﴾ "then whatever offering can be readily obtained." [al-Baqarah: 196]. This means a sheep, or something greater. He slaughters it during the days of Mina and distributes its meat in charity, eating none of it himself. If he cannot find a sheep, he takes a share in a camel — and the required portion of a camel is explained in the section on the hady. If he can find neither, he fasts, as God Almighty commanded:

- Three days during the Hajj: one day before Tarwiyah, the day of Tarwiyah, and the day of Arafah.
- Seven days once he has returned to his family.

If he fasts those seven days on the road home, that suffices. If he fasted neither the three days during Hajj nor on the way back — whether out of ignorance or forgetfulness — he lets those days go and fasts ten days upon returning to his family. Fasting them consecutively is better, but if he separates them, that too suffices.

### Going out to Mina and the standing at Arafah

Jafar ibn Muhammad said to one of those he was teaching the rites — a pilgrim performing tamattu: "On the day of Tarwiyah, Allah willing, bathe, put on your garments, and enter the Sacred Mosque barefoot, with serenity and dignity. Perform, if you wish, seven rounds of voluntary tawaf upon your arrival, and pray the two rakahs of your tawaf. When the sun has passed its zenith, pray the obligatory prayer, then enter ihram for Hajj and do just as you did at Dhu al-Hulayfah. Then go out to Mina."

He said: the people should leave Makkah for Mina on the day of Tarwiyah, from the beginning of the day until its end. It was named the day of Tarwiyah because the people would water their animals on it, for there was no water in Mina at that time. It is the eighth day of Dhu al-Hijjah. When the people went out from Makkah toward Mina and Arafah, intending the Hajj, whoever wished among them rode and whoever wished walked; and for one who could find no mount, there is reward in walking.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that, for one who is able, riding is better than walking, because the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) made the journey mounted. Jabir ibn Abdullah (رض) said: "I looked out that day upon the company before the Messenger of Allah — people on foot and people mounted — as far as the eye could reach, and on his right and his left and behind him the like of that, rider and walker together."

When they reached Kura al-Ghamim, those on foot drew themselves into a line, seeking the blessing of his supplication. As he passed among them, they said, "O Messenger of Allah, the journey has grown long upon us and the walking has become hard for us." He said, "May Allah reward you," and then added, "If you were to help yourselves with a light, quick pace, your bodies would grow lighter and you would cover the ground the sooner." They did so, and it eased them.

The rider, then, arrives at the standing at Arafah in greater strength — stronger for supplication than the man who has walked the distance — and for that reason riding is recommended for one who finds a mount and is able.

When the season arrives, the imam should not pray the noon prayer on the Day of Tarwiyah except at Mina. He passes the night there, and the people pass the night with him. Then, on the morning of the Day of Arafah, after the sun has risen, he sets out from Mina toward Arafah, and the people set out with him.

It is recommended to bathe on that day and to break one's fast, for anyone who fears that fasting would weaken him for the standing and supplication at Arafah. When they reach Arafah, they descend there; descending and standing near the mountain is recommended. Descending and standing at the place of al-Idrak, however, is forbidden. Jafar ibn Muhammad held that those who descend beneath it and stand there have no valid Hajj. All the rest of Arafah is a valid standing-place.

When the sun passes its zenith, the assembly gathers. The imam descends to the floor of the valley and addresses the people. The muezzin then calls the adhan and gives the iqamah, and the imam leads them in the noon prayer. The muezzins give the iqamah a second time, and he leads them in the afternoon prayer, the congregation praying behind him — the two prayers combined under a single adhan and two iqamahs. So did the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family). He then stands, and the people stand with him. They remain standing, faces turned toward the sun, calling upon Allah Almighty, yearning toward Him, asking Him for their needs of this world and the next, until the sun sets. Whoever wishes recites the Quran; whoever wishes glorifies Allah, declares His oneness, exalts Him, and seeks His forgiveness.

It is narrated of Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Husayn that when he stood at Arafah he would begin Surah al-Baqarah, and after every ten verses he would glorify Allah, declare His oneness, exalt Him, seek forgiveness, and supplicate. He would then begin the next ten and do likewise, until he had completed the surah.

It is narrated of Jafar ibn Muhammad that he said: when you stand at the standing-place of Arafah, praise Allah and extol Him, and pray upon the Prophet Muhammad and his family, then say:

"O Allah, I ask You by Your might, by Your strength, by Your generosity, by Your bounty, by Your grace, and by all that comes from You, O most Hearing of those who hear, most Seeing of those who see, O Swift in reckoning, O Ruler of rulers, O Most Merciful of the merciful, that You send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, and that You do with me what is worthy of You." Then raise your hand and your face toward the sky and say: "O Allah, my need is such that if You grant it nothing You withhold will harm me, and if You withhold it nothing You grant will profit me. I ask You for the deliverance of my neck from the Fire. O Allah, I am Your servant, my forelock is in Your hand, and my term is within Your knowledge. I ask You to grant me success in what pleases You with me, and to make me one whose deed You are pleased with and whose striving You accept." Then say: "Praise be to Allah for His forbearance after His knowledge; praise be to Allah for His pardon after His power; praise be to Allah for His mercy that outstrips His wrath; and praise be to Allah with every praise that is His, for all His favors," until you conclude, "praise be to Allah in the measure that our Lord loves and is pleased with; praise be to Allah for His favors, which can neither be numbered nor ever repaid.

"O Allah, I ask You by Your beautiful names, by Your strength, Your power, and Your might, by all that Your knowledge encompasses, by the right of Your Messenger, and by Your Greatest Name — the most Great — by which whoever calls upon You, it is binding upon You to answer him and not to turn him back, that You grant him his request: that You forgive me all my sins and all that You know of me."

Then ask Allah for the needs of this world and the next, and say often: "O Allah, I ask You for the deliverance of my neck from the Fire." Repeat this supplication and others, whatever is made easy and opened, multiplying exaltation, declaration of His oneness, and seeking of forgiveness, until the sun sets. When it sets, say:

"O Allah, do not make this the last of my covenants with this standing-place, and open it to me forever as long as You keep me alive. Turn me back from it triumphant and successful, my supplication answered, my sin forgiven, returning with the best return of any of Your delegation who came to You and any of the pilgrims of Your Sacred House. Make me today among the most honored of Your delegation before You, and grant me the best of what You have granted any of them of good and blessing, of mercy and pleasure and well-being. Bless me in that to which I return — of family and wealth, of little and much — and bless them in me. O Allah, expand for us Your lawful and wholesome provision, and do not plot against me nor draw me on by degrees, and turn away from me the evil of the corrupt among jinn and mankind, and send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy and as they are worthy of You."

Whoever falls ill and loses consciousness is to be stood at the standing-place of Arafah. The standing at Arafah, it is held, is not valid except in a state of purity. It is narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) that he said: "The gravest in sin of the people of Arafah is he who departs from it believing that he has not been forgiven."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali explained: he means the one who despairs of Allah's mercy.

One day, having stood at Arafah, Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "Allah is a Lord of generosity. By Allah, not one who pours forth from this standing-place this evening — neither the righteous nor the corrupt — departs without a share from Allah. As for the righteous, he is answered for his Hereafter and his world; and as for the corrupt, he is given his portion and his lot of what he asked for of this world."

Al-Husayn ibn Ali saw a man begging from the people on the day of Arafah and said, rebuking him: "Woe to you. Do you ask of any but Allah on this day, when even those in the wombs are hoped for on this day?"

Abu Jafar ibn Muhammad would not turn away any beggar on the day of Arafah. Jafar ibn Muhammad, for his part, would greatly increase on that day in supplication, prayer, charity, and the seeking of forgiveness.

### The departure from Arafah to Muzdalifah

Allah Almighty said: "Then pour forth from where the people pour forth." Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad explained that in the age of ignorance the Quraysh would pour forth from Muzdalifah, saying, "We are more entitled to the House than the rest of the people." Allah Almighty therefore commanded them to pour forth from Arafah, from where the people pour forth. Before that command, they had not been standing at Arafah at all, only at the Sacred Precinct; they were now commanded to stand at Arafah and to depart from it.

The departure from Arafah is not made until the sun has set and its disc has fully disappeared. When that has happened, the people pour forth toward Muzdalifah, and Maghrib is not prayed until they arrive there. At Muzdalifah, Maghrib and the later Isha prayer are combined behind the imam, with a single adhan and two iqamahs. The night of the Day of Sacrifice is spent at Muzdalifah, the best position being near the Sacred Precinct. Whoever departs from Arafah before the sun sets owes a camel to be slaughtered, or else its price given in charity.

On departing, one should increase in the remembrance of Allah Almighty and in seeking His forgiveness, for Allah Almighty says: ﴿ثُمَّ أَفِيضُوا مِنْ حَيْثُ أَفَاضَ النَّاسُ وَاسْتَغْفِرُوا اللَّهَ إِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَحِيمٌ﴾ "Then pour forth from where the people pour forth, and seek Allah's forgiveness; indeed Allah is Forgiving, Merciful." [al-Baqarah: 199].

He should be measured in his going. When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) departed from Arafah during the Farewell Pilgrimage, riding his she-camel al-Qaswa, he drew in her rein until her head nearly touched his foot, gesturing with his right hand and saying, "O people, tranquility, tranquility." Whenever he came upon a hill he would loosen her rein a little so that she could climb, until he reached Muzdalifah, where he combined the two evening prayers.

Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about praying the two evening prayers short of Muzdalifah. He said no — not even if a third of the night had already passed — and that whoever does so deliberately owes a sacrifice. He further said that spending the night at Muzdalifah on the night of the Day of Sacrifice is of the Sunnah. Whoever deliberately forgoes that night there, knowing it to be of the Sunnah, owes a camel, unless he returns within whatever remains of the night and stands there. Whoever left it out of ignorance, however, and the time has already passed — having stood at Muzdalifah even for a single moment — owes nothing.

It is recommended for the imam to stand at al-Quzah, the mountain upon which the standing is made; yet all of Muzdalifah is a valid standing-place. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) said: "All of Arafah is a standing-place, and all of Mina is a place of sacrifice." He also granted a dispensation to women and to the weak: they may depart from Muzdalifah by night, cast the jamarat by night, and pray Fajr at Mina. The imam and the rest of the people, however, do not depart from Muzdalifah until they have prayed Fajr and the daylight has fully broken.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) stood at Muzdalifah until the sun shone upon Thabir — the first mountain in the world upon which the sun rises. When he saw it, he said, "Shine, O Thabir; shine, O Thabir. I say as my brother Musa said: My Lord, expand for me my breast, and make my affair easy for me, and untie the knot from my tongue, that they may understand my speech; and appoint for me a helper from my family, Ali my brother; strengthen my back through him, and let him share in my affair."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: when you stand at the Sacred Precinct, praise Allah and extol Him, and recite of the verses of the Quran what you are able, then pray upon the Prophet and his family, then supplicate for yourself as much as you are able. For the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family), when he had prayed Fajr on the Day of Sacrifice at Muzdalifah, mounted al-Qaswa until he came to the Sacred Precinct, where he ascended and faced the qibla, and exalted Allah, declared His oneness, and proclaimed Him alone, and did not cease standing thus until the daylight had fully broken. Then he poured forth from Muzdalifah toward Mina until he came to Muhassir — the valley lying between Muzdalifah and Mina — where he quickened his pace a little and took the middle road that brings one out at the great jamrah.

He went on until he reached the jamrah beside the tree and pelted it with seven pebbles. Then he came to Mina, offered his sacrifice, and shaved his head. Hastening through Muhassir is a Sunnah, to be observed as the Messenger of Allah (ص) observed it. On that day the Jamrat al-Aqabah is thrown before the sacrifice; one then slaughters and shaves, and no harm comes from taking these in whichever order one begins.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that the pebbles for the jamarat may be taken from Muzdalifah or from other places lying toward Mina. Whoever is at Mina should not carry pebbles from one jamrah to another; the pebbles for all the jamarat are gathered from a single place. It is recommended that they be picked up one by one — dark and speckled, roughly the size of a fingertip — and it is disliked to break them from larger stones. Performing the ritual washing before throwing is likewise recommended. The one who throws should keep Mina on his right and the House on his left, and should throw from the floor of the valley rather than from above it. Abu Jafar ibn Ali said that when throwing the jamarat, the takbir is to be pronounced with each pebble cast.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: whoever throws the Jamrat al-Aqabah on the Day of Sacrifice before slaughtering, then comes to Mina and slaughters and shaves, then proceeds to the House and circumambulates it seven times and performs the say between al-Safa and al-Marwah seven times — this being the circumambulation of the Hajj — then returns and performs the circumambulation of the women: when all of this has been done, everything that ihram had forbidden to the muhrim by reason of his consecration becomes lawful to him again. He then returns to Mina and remains there through the days of Tashriq, throwing the jamarat each day after the sun has passed its zenith. Each jamrah is pelted with seven pebbles, beginning with the lesser, then the middle, then the greater. He has latitude to throw at any hour of the day, though being in a state of purity is preferable. The Messenger of Allah (ص) used to walk to the jamarat when he threw them.

Ali threw the jamarat both on foot and mounted, and no fixed prescription governs this. The Messenger of Allah (ص) granted the herdsmen a dispensation to throw the jamarat by night, and whoever misses throwing them by day may throw them by night. Whoever places the pebbles of one jamrah upon another should repeat the throwing, beginning with the lesser jamrah and ending with the greater, which is the Jamrat al-Aqabah. The jamarat are thrown on behalf of the sick, and whoever misses any part of the throwing makes it up when he remembers, or appoints someone to make it up for him. Whoever hastens his departure within two days leaves the remaining pebbles with him and does not throw them at the jamarat after he has departed.

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

On the matter of the sacrificial offering, Allah Almighty said: "They are the ones who disbelieved and turned you away from the Sacred Mosque, and the sacrificial offerings from reaching their place of slaughter" [al-Fath: 25]. And He, Blessed and Exalted, said: "Do not shave your heads until the sacrificial offering reaches its place of slaughter" [al-Baqarah: 196].

The place of slaughter for an offering brought in Hajj alone, or in Hajj joined to Umrah, is Mina. The place of slaughter for an offering brought in Umrah alone is Mecca. Each is slaughtered in its own place. When the Messenger of Allah (ص) brought forward his offering, he slaughtered it at the place of slaughter in Mina and said: "This is the place of sacrifice, and all of Mina is a place of sacrifice." The people then slaughtered at their own encampments.

It is preferable that a man attend to the slaughter himself and perform it with his own hand. If he is unable to do so, he should place his hand alongside the hand of the one who slaughters. If he is unable even for that, he should stand over the animal while the butcher acts in his stead. In any case, he should entrust this task only to a Muslim.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) made Ali a partner in his offering. Together, the animals the Messenger of Allah (ص) had brought and those Ali brought from Yemen came to one hundred camels. The Messenger of Allah (ص) slaughtered sixty-three of them with his own hand — each animal bound and standing, brought to him one after another — then commanded Ali to slaughter the rest. He then ordered that a portion from each be placed in a pot and cooked, and both the Messenger of Allah (ص) and Ali ate of that flesh and sipped of its broth, so that they might partake of every one of the animals, for Allah has said: "when their flanks fall, eat of them."

When Ali slaughtered his offering or sacrificed his devoted animal, he would say: "I have turned my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth, as a sincere Muslim, and I am not among the polytheists. Truly my prayer, my devotion, my life, and my death are for Allah, the Lord of the worlds; no partner has He. Thus have I been commanded, and I am among the Muslims. O Allah, this is from You and to You. In the name of Allah." Then he drew the knife across it.

The naming of Allah over the animal is obligatory, and its omission is not permitted except for one who forgets; when he remembers, he names Allah. Whoever cannot afford an offering on his own should join with others in whatever he is able to contribute, and the more he does of this, the better.

The best of offerings is the she-camel, followed by the male camel, then the female of the cattle — for it was narrated from Ali that the female of the cattle is preferable to the male. The term *badanah* applies to both camels and cattle. After these come the males of the cattle, then the ram of the sheep, which is better than the *muja* — the animal whose testicles have been bound or crushed. The *muja* is better than the *mustalim*, whose testicles have been cut away; the *mustalim* is better than the ewe; the ewe is better than the ram of the goats; and the ram of the goats is better than the female of the goats.

None of these suffices as an offering except a mature animal or a *thani*, save in the case of sheep, for which the yearling suffices.

The finest among all of them is the *mulh*: an animal whose white coat is touched with black at certain points — its muzzle, the sockets around its eyes, the extremities of its fore and hind legs, and the tip of its tail. It has been said that this is the very description of the ram sent down to Ibrahim.

Among sacrificial animals, the plump is preferred and the lean is disliked — unless the animal was bought in good condition and grew thin in the owner's keeping without any intention on his part. The animal whose horn is wholly broken is forbidden for offering; if only the outer portion is broken, such an animal is called *aqsam*. The eye and the ear are to be examined carefully, so that no defect goes unnoticed. Forbidden likewise is the animal whose udder teats — that is, the nipples of the udder — have been severed, as is the mangy animal, the aged, and the one whose ear has been cut off. The following are disapproved:

- the *muqabalah*: an animal whose ear has been cut at the front
- the *mudabarah*: an animal whose ear has been cut at the back
- the *sharqa*: an animal whose ear has been split in two
- the *kharqa*: an animal in whose ear there is a round hole

As for a defect that befalls the animal after it has been purchased and dedicated as an offering, nothing further is required of the owner — the offering remains valid and sufficient. Dedication is effected either by marking the animal or by garlanding it.

Camels and cattle are marked by piercing the right side of the hump with a piece of iron until blood flows over it, so that the animal may be recognized as an offering should it stray. Oxen are garlanded, as are sheep when presented as offerings. The camel is draped with a covering cloth, which is given in charity at the time of slaughter.

If the camel becomes disabled before reaching its destination, it is slaughtered, a sandal is dipped in its blood and hung around it as a garland, and it is left in place. Any passerby for whom eating the offering is lawful may recognize it as such, and cut from it and eat, if he wishes.

Among these offerings, a distinction governs what the owner may consume:

- **Voluntary offerings:** the owner may eat from them and feed to whomever he wishes.
- **Obligatory offerings** — those owed by vow, or given as recompense for game hunted, or for spoiling some rite of the Hajj — the owner may eat nothing of these.

In neither case, whether the offering is obligatory or otherwise, may it be sold.

Whoever's offering is disabled before it reaches its destination, and who can find no replacement, bears nothing further. If it strays, he buys another in a different place. If the replacement is an obligatory offering — that is, if he has already marked or garlanded it — he slaughters both together. If he has not yet dedicated it, the choice remains his.

If a man's offering is led astray along with the person who bought it, its owner may reclaim it if he wishes; this suffices for him, though it does not suffice for the one who slaughtered it. Alternatively, he may claim its price from the one who sold it to him.

Whoever finds a stray offering should announce it publicly. If no one comes forward to claim it, he slaughters it on the last of the days of Tashriq on behalf of its owner.

Whoever has his offering slaughtered and then has part of it stolen, the sacrifice still suffices for him.

The scholars held the presentation of the offering to be obligatory: a man takes it from Mina to Arafah, stands with it there, then proceeds to Muzdalifah, and passes through all the ritual stations before slaughtering it.

Whoever slaughters a voluntary offering may eat of it, give from it in charity, and feed from it whomever he wishes. No fixed time governs this.

Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the words of Allah Almighty, "So eat of it and feed the poor and the wretched," and His saying, "eat of it and feed the contented and the beggar who importunes." He said: the poor man is the one who does not beg of people, and the wretched is in a harsher condition than he, more sorely pressed. The contented is the one who is satisfied with whatever is given to him — he does not grow angry, cry out, or twist his mouth in vexation. The beggar who importunes is the one who passes by presenting himself.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali used to test people in this matter so that the truly contented might be distinguished from others. When a beggar stopped before him, he would order that the head of the sacrificial animal be given to him. If the man took it and went on his way rejoicing, with no vexation, he would order that meat be given to him as well. But if the man was vexed, left the head, and went his way, he knew that man was not among the contented.

They disliked that anything from the offerings be taken out of Mina, except for small practical purposes — some of the hump for medicine, or something of the hide, wool, fur, sinew, or hair, for whatever a traveler genuinely needs on the road. They favored giving the hides of offerings and sacrificial animals in charity, though they permitted keeping them for use, and they permitted giving the butcher a portion of the hide and of the covering cloth. They forbade the offering of the *jallalah* — the animal that feeds on dung and filth — and the eating of its flesh, until it has been cleansed by wholesome fodder and confined for a known period. Likewise, its milk is not to be drawn until it has been cleared. The periods are:

- She-camel and camel: forty days
- Cattle: twenty days
- Sheep: seven days
- Ducks: five days
- Chickens: three days

They forbade riding the offering in any way that would harm or weaken it, loading burdens upon it, or putting it to labor in a similar manner. Whatever neither weakens nor injures the animal carries no prohibition.

Whoever forgets to slaughter at Mina slaughters at Mecca when he remembers. The days of Mina are three, following the Day of Sacrifice. The day immediately after the Day of Sacrifice is the first of them; it is called the Day of Qarr, because one settles at Mina on that day and does not depart — one may not leave Mina then except to visit the House and circumambulate it. The second is the Day of the First Departure, as God Almighty said: *"Whoever hastens within two days, there is no sin upon him"* — meaning the hastening of departure from Mina.

The third is the last of the two days of departure.

As for shaving and shortening, God Almighty said: *"Allah has fulfilled His Messenger's vision in truth: you shall surely enter the Sacred Mosque, if Allah wills, in safety, with heads shaved and hair shortened"* [al-Fath: 27]. Shaving is what is done with the razor; shortening is what is taken with scissors from the ends of the hair, leaving some of it. It has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that shaving is better than shortening, and that the Messenger of Allah (ص) shaved during the Farewell Pilgrimage, while some of the people shaved and others shortened. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "O Allah, have mercy upon those who shave." Someone who heard him said, "And those who shorten, O Messenger of Allah?" He said, "O Allah, have mercy upon those who shave and those who shorten."

The Ahl al-Bayt instructed that, alongside the shaving, one should also take from the sides of the beard and the mustache, pare the nails, and draw the razor to the two prominent bones beneath the temples, level with the ears. It has already been noted that the bald man and the man whose head is close-shorn each passes the razor over his head. As for the woman, when she comes out of ihram, she cuts something from the ends of her hair; she does not shave her head, nor any part of it.

Whoever forgets to shave, or does not know of it, until he has left Mina should shave wherever he recalls or learns of the obligation. If he is still near Mina, he returns and shaves there. If he has traveled far from it, he sends his hair to Mina to be cast there, if he is able. It was also ordained that whatever hair falls be buried. It is narrated from Ali that he said, "Bury this hair, for everything that falls from the son of Adam while he is alive is dead matter that must be buried." Ali ibn al-Husayn would bury his hair in a hollow at Mina when he shaved, saying at that moment, "O Allah, grant me for every hair a light on the Day of Resurrection."

> ثُمَّ لْيَقْضُوا۟ تَفَثَهُمْ وَلْيُوفُوا۟ نُذُورَهُمْ وَلْيَطَّوَّفُوا۟ بِٱلْبَيْتِ ٱلْعَتِيقِ

On what the pilgrim does at Mina, Allah Almighty said: "Then let them complete their rites and fulfill their vows and circumambulate the Ancient House" [al-Hajj: 29]. It is narrated from Ali that he said the completing of the rites comprises the throwing, the shaving, the vows, the walking, and the sacrifices; and the circumambulation of the Ancient House is the Circumambulation of the Visit — the Circumambulation of the Overflowing, the Circumambulation of the Hajj, already treated in the chapter on Circumambulation. It falls on the Day of Sacrifice: the people visit the House after they have shaved and slaughtered at Mina, then return to the House and circumambulate it, then return to Mina, having been freed from everything their consecration had bound them to, as previously noted. It matters not with which act one begins, whether shaving or slaughtering.

It is recommended, thereafter, to hasten the Circumambulation of the Overflowing on the Day of Sacrifice for the one performing tamattu, while scholars permitted the qarin and the one performing Hajj alone to delay it. There is no harm in throwing the jamarat before shaving and slaughtering, or after them. The visit, however, is not made before shaving, slaughtering, and throwing have been performed — unless the pilgrim does so out of forgetfulness or ignorance, in which case nothing is required of him; he simply shaves, slaughters, and throws upon returning.

The pilgrim does not spend the nights of Mina away from Mina. Whoever spends a night away deliberately owes a blood offering for each such night; whoever does so out of ignorance or forgetfulness owes nothing, and Allah forgives them.

It is related that the Messenger of Allah (ص) shortened his prayer in Mina and commanded that Allah the Almighty be remembered abundantly during the days spent there. Commenting on the words of Allah the Almighty — ﴿فَإِذَا قَضَيْتُم مَّنَاسِكَكُمْ فَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ كَذِكْرِكُمْ آبَاءَكُمْ أَوْ أَشَدَّ ذِكْرًا﴾ — "When you have fulfilled your rituals, remember Allah as you remember your fathers, or with even greater remembrance" [Al-Baqarah: 200] — Jafar ibn Muhammad explained that when the polytheists would gather in Mina, they would boast of their fathers and recount the glories of their ancestors. Allah the Almighty therefore commanded the Muslims to replace that custom with His remembrance.

Fasting on the day of sacrifice and the days of Tashreeq was held to be disapproved. When the pilgrim throws the Jamarat al-Aqaba al-Kubra on the day of sacrifice, he is released from everything that had been forbidden to him in the state of ihram, with four exceptions:

- hunting
- women
- dwelling outside of Mina
- perfume

Once he then performs the circumambulation of the House and the say between al-Safa and al-Marwah, everything forbidden in ihram becomes permissible again — except women. When he performs the Tawaf al-Nisa, everything becomes permissible to him without exception.

The takbir during the days of Mina is to be said after every obligatory prayer, spoken after the taslim and before the worshipper rises:

"Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar, la ilaha ill-Allah, wa Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar wa lillahi al-hamd. Allahu Akbar for what He has guided us to, and Allahu Akbar for what He has provided us of the grazing livestock."

The pilgrim may visit the House on any day of Mina he wishes and perform as much voluntary circumambulation as he wishes during the day, then return and spend the night in Mina.

Concerning the departure from Mina, Allah the Almighty said: ﴿وَاذْكُرُواْ اللَّهَ فِى أَيَّامٍ مَعْدُودَاتٍ فَمَن تَعَجَّلَ فِى يَوْمَيْنِ فَلاَ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ وَمَن تَأَخَّرَ فَلا إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ لِمَنِ اتَّقَى﴾ "And remember Allah during the appointed days. Whoever hastens in two days, there is no sin upon him, and whoever delays, there is no sin upon him, for the one who fears Allah" [Al-Baqarah: 203].

Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad said that this verse refers to the stay in Mina: if one wishes to remain, that is well, and if one wishes to hasten the departure after two days, one may do so, as Allah the Almighty has stated.

Whoever departs on the second day of Tashreeq — that is, the third day after the day of sacrifice — must not leave until he has prayed the noon prayer and cast the jamarat. He may then depart any time between that moment and sunset. If sunset overtakes him before he leaves, he must spend the night, and he then becomes one of the people of the third day, free to depart at any point from the beginning of that day to its end, provided he has first prayed Fajr and cast the jamarat.

It is also mentioned that one should send his baggage ahead toward Makkah before departing. It is further recommended that whoever leaves Mina descend at al-Muhassab — known as al-Bat-ha — and linger there briefly before continuing on. The Messenger of Allah (ص) did precisely this: upon reaching al-Muhassab, he descended, remained for an hour, and then set out once more.

Whoever is among the first to depart may remain in Makkah until the people have shaved. When the people move on to Makkah, whoever wishes to enter the House should do so if he is able; whoever does not enter bears no obligation.

Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "If you are able to enter the House, enter it whenever you wish; but if you fear the crowd, do not endanger yourself." He then recalled the account of a people who perished in the crush within it.

He said that it is recommended for whoever wishes to enter the Kaaba to perform a ritual bath. Upon entering, after praising Allah as He deserves and sending prayers upon the Prophet and his family, one says:

"O Allah, You have said, and whoever enters it is safe, so make me safe from Your punishment, and cleanse my heart of arrogance, hypocrisy, ostentation, and doubt regarding Your religion, and secure me from my fear of the Fire on the Day of Resurrection. O Allah, whoever has arrayed himself, or equipped himself, or made ready, or prepared for a journey to some created being in hope of his gift and his bounty — to You, my Master, has been my arraying, my equipping, and my preparation, in hope of Your gift and Your bounty. So do not disappoint my hope today, and do not turn me away today in disappointment from Your House. You have made it sacred, and You are the most generous of those whose house is visited. O Most Great, there is no god but You."

Whoever wishes to pray voluntarily inside the House may do so, the Master said, and it is recommended to pray between the two pillars, upon the red marble — for no spot on earth is better than that spot, and upon it the Messenger of Allah (ص) prayed. He then rose and faced the back of the House in prayer, then stood and faced each corner of the House in turn, supplicating at each one, before returning to the red marble to pray upon it. He added that no obligatory prayer is performed inside the Kaaba, and that prayer upon its roof is not permissible.

Whoever intends to depart from Makkah, the Master said, should make his final act with it a circumambulation of the House — seven rounds — then bid it farewell. He places his hand between the Black Stone and the Door and says: "O Allah, I am Your servant, and the son of Your servant, and the son of Your handmaid. You carried me upon Your mount and conveyed me through Your land until You brought me into Your sanctuary. O Allah, this is Your House, and I have come to it, hoping — in the goodness of my thought of You — that You would forgive me my sins. So if You have forgiven me, then increase Your pleasure toward me and grant me nearness with You; and if You have not yet forgiven me, then from this hour forgive me, before I depart from Your House. This is the hour of my departure, if You have granted me leave, without turning away from You, nor from Your House, nor exchanging You or Your House for any other.

Guard me on my right and on my left, behind me and before me, until You bring me to my family; and when You have brought me to them, do not forsake me, and take me unto Yourself upon that upon which You have taken Your righteous servants, for You are more worthy of that than Your creation and than I."

He then clings to the House and says: "O Allah, do not make this the last of my covenant with Your House."

When he has gone out, he stands at the door of the mosque, turns his face toward the House, fills his eyes with the sight of it, raises his hands toward the sky, and says: "O Allah, send me back triumphant, prospering, shown mercy, and forgiven. O Allah, if You cause me to die, forgive me; and if You grant me life, provide me a Hajj in the coming year, and do not make this the last of my covenant with Your Sacred House."

Before leaving Makkah, it is recommended that the pilgrim purchase dates with dirhams, or whatever he can afford, and distribute them in charity, intending by this to atone for any neglect or shortcoming that occurred during his ihram and his Hajj. One departing from Hajj should not hurry his travel as though he were turning away from something he dislikes toward something he loves; rather, he should depart in tranquility. Ali would not accept the testimony of one who races ahead of the other pilgrims. One day he went out to al-Rahbah and found a crowd gathered. He asked what the occasion was and was told that a man had raced ahead of the pilgrims. Ali said: "No welcome and no greeting. You have shortened the prayer and wearied the mount at noon."

As for the individual Umrah, Allah the Almighty said: ﴿وَأَتِمُّوا الْحَجَّ وَالْعُمْرَةَ لِلّهِ﴾ — "And complete Hajj and Umrah for Allah" [Al-Baqarah: 196]. The Ahl al-Bayt, and those reciters who agreed with them, read Umrah in the accusative, giving it the force of an obligation: complete the Hajj and the Umrah. On this reading, Umrah is obligatory. Some who differed from them read "and Umrah for Allah" in the nominative, as though they closed the speech after the words "and complete the Hajj" and began a new clause — so that the obligation rested upon whatever was performed for Allah the Almighty. Jafar ibn Muhammad held that Umrah is an obligation on the same footing as Hajj, on the strength of the verse: "Complete the Hajj and the Umrah." It was narrated from Ali that he too declared Umrah obligatory. And it was narrated from al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad that he said: one Umrah to the next is an expiation for everything that falls between them. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: an Umrah in the month of Ramadan equals a Hajj.

For the individual Umrah, the one who performs it alone enters ihram from the miqat, observes every abstention that ihram requires, and forms the intention. Upon reaching the House he circumambulates it seven times, performs the say between al-Safa and al-Marwah, prays the two rakahs of the circumambulation, and is then released — and that is the Umrah. If he has brought a sacrificial animal, he slaughters it at Makkah.

Al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Perform Umrah in whichever of the months you wish — and the most excellent is an Umrah in the month of Rajab."

An Umrah performed in the month of Hajj, followed by departure, stands as an individual Umrah; but if Hajj is then performed in that same year, the pilgrim is one performing Qiran.

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

On the subjects of repulsion and siege, Allah the Almighty said, ﴿هُمُ الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا وَصَدُّوكُمْ عَنِ الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ وَالْهَدْىَ مَعْكُوفًا أَن يَبْلُغَ مَحِلَّهُ﴾ "It is they who disbelieved and barred you from the Sacred Mosque, and the offering, prevented from reaching its place" [Al-Fath: 25]. And He, Blessed and Exalted, said, ﴿فَإِنْ أُحْصِرْتُمْ فَمَا اسْتَيْسَرَ مِنَ الْهَدْيِ﴾ "If you are prevented, then whatever offering is feasible" [Al-Baqarah: 196].

Al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad explained that repulsion is prevention, and siege is illness. He related that the Messenger of Allah (ص) set out from Medina in the year of al-Hudaybiyyah with more than a thousand companions, intending Umrah. He entered ihram at Dhu al-Hulayfa, his companions entered ihram alongside him, and he drove the sacrificial animals forward, collaring and marking them. News of this reached the Quraysh — this was before the conquest of Makkah — and they mustered their forces and marched out to block his way. When he drew near Asfan, word of their movements reached him. He said:

"We have not come to fight anyone; we have come only as pilgrims of Umrah. If the Quraysh wish, I will make a truce with them for a set term and leave the way open between me and the people. Then, if they wish to enter into that which the people have entered, they may enter; and if they refuse, I will fight them until Allah judges between us — and He is the best of judges."

After much back-and-forth between the Prophet (ص) and the Quraysh — a long exchange whose details need not be rehearsed here — a truce was agreed: he would turn back that year and perform Umrah the following year, accompanied by whoever wished to come with him. He then slaughtered the sacrificial animals he had driven to the spot where he stood, the Muslims cut their hair short, and the entire company turned back.

This occasion establishes the ruling for one who is turned back from the House. What such a person must do depends on whether he has bound a Hajj, an Umrah, or both upon himself — and that obligation takes effect only once he has passed the miqat. The rulings break down as follows:

- One who is turned back before reaching the miqat simply departs, regardless of whether he had entered ihram; if he had brought a sacrificial animal, he may take it back and dispose of it as he wishes, because the offering had not yet become binding on him.
- The offering becomes binding only through its marking and its collaring, and that takes place after ihram has been entered from the miqat.
- Whoever enters ihram from a point short of the miqat has not entered a valid ihram; he has done wrong and departed from the Sunnah.

> وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا۟ رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ ٱلْهَدْىُ مَحِلَّهُۥ

As for siege, it is illness, as al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad explained in the words of Allah the Almighty. He was asked about a man beset by illness who had with him an offering he had sent ahead with his companions, appointing them a set time: if he is in Hajj, the place of slaughtering the offering is on the Day of Sacrifice; and if he is in Umrah, let him reckon the measure of his companions arrival in Makkah and the hour he has appointed for them, and when that hour comes, he cuts his hair short and is released. This, again, is only when he had entered ihram from the miqat and then fell ill afterward. But if he had not yet reached the miqat, he may turn back if he wishes or remain, because the obligation of Hajj had not yet become binding upon him. It was narrated that al-Husayn ibn Ali set out from Medina intending Umrah, and when he came to al-Suqya he fell ill, and news of him reached Ali, who was in Medina. He came to him and said: My son, what do you complain of? He said: I complain of my head. So Ali called for a camel and slaughtered it on his behalf, shaved his head, and returned him to Medina; and when he recovered, he went out and performed Umrah. Likewise, whoever is besieged by illness after entering ihram for Hajj, or intending Umrah, or both together, is obliged to shed blood, as Allah the Almighty said, ﴿فَإِنْ أُحْصِرْتُمْ فَمَا اسْتَيْسَرَ مِنَ الْهَدْيِ﴾ "If you are prevented, then whatever offering is feasible" [Al-Baqarah: 196], and that is a sheep or more; then he turns back.

When he recovers from his ailment, he goes out and makes up what he was besieged from of Hajj, or Umrah, or both together.

The one besieged by illness does not approach women until he completes what he was besieged from, while the one merely turned back may approach women and is not obliged to make up what he was turned back from. This is entirely the word of the Ahl al-Bayt. Al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about one who was besieged and then recovered, whether women became lawful for him. He said: No, not until he circumambulates the House. He was asked: Then why did women become lawful for the Messenger of Allah (ص) when he returned from al-Hudaybiyyah, though he had not circumambulated the House? He said: The two are not alike. For the one turned back, that is lawful; but for the one besieged, it is not.

On the question of Hajj performed on behalf of the incapacitated or the deceased: a woman from Khatham came to the Messenger of Allah (ص) and said: "O Messenger of Allah, my father is an old man, grown too feeble to move, and the obligation of Hajj has come upon him. Shall I perform Hajj on his behalf?" The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Yes, do so — for were there a debt upon your father which you discharged, would that not suffice for him?"

Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about a case of the same kind and permitted it, citing the report of the Khathami woman. An aged man or an aged woman who has despaired of ever being able to perform Hajj may each equip someone to perform it on their behalf, and that suffices. One who is capable of making the journey, however, is not discharged by another's going in his place; he must set out and perform Hajj himself — whether the journey comes easily or not, and even if he fears an enemy along the way — because his situation is that of one who may reasonably hope that the fear will pass and the road to Hajj will open. No fixed deadline is appointed for Hajj.

Hajj is an obligation once in a lifetime. For as long as a person still has hope of being able to perform it, the obligation remains upon him. If he finds a way to it at last and yet delays, he has wronged himself.

Hajj may be performed on behalf of one who has died, whether the deceased bequeathed it or his guardian volunteers to engage someone to perform it for him. If the deceased specified that Hajj be performed on his behalf from the third of his estate, the cost is taken from that third. If he made no such specification and had not yet performed his obligatory Hajj, the cost is taken from the whole of his estate, and any further bequests he made are then paid from the third.

Whoever is engaged to perform Hajj on behalf of another, and finds that the wage exceeds his expenses, may keep the surplus. The Hajj is credited both to him and to the one on whose behalf he performs it. If he has not yet performed Hajj for himself, he stands in this arrangement as a hired man only; if he has already fulfilled his own Hajj, that is the preferable position. A man may perform Hajj on behalf of a man, and a woman on behalf of a woman. If a woman performs Hajj on behalf of a man, it suffices him when no other can be found, or when she is more capable than the men available to him and more exact in discharging the rites. Whoever performs Hajj on behalf of another should strive to complete it in full and to fulfil all its rites.

It was narrated of Jafar ibn Muhammad that, after his son Ismail (ع) died, he hired a man to perform Hajj on his behalf, instructed him in the rites, and set out the conditions upon him. Then he said to him: "Know that if you fulfill what we have stipulated of you, it will count as a Hajj for the one on whose behalf you have performed it, and you will have a reward for keeping the condition laid upon you and for the labor of your body."

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said that Allah, mighty and majestic, admits three people into Paradise on account of a single Hajj:

- the one who bequeaths that a Hajj be performed for him
- the one who carries out that bequest after him
- the pilgrim who performs it on his behalf, provided he strives and fulfills the duty upon him

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali held that there is no harm if one who performs Hajj for another for a wage adds, on his own behalf as a volunteer, whatever circumambulation or Umrah he wishes, once he has discharged what he was engaged to do. He also said that whoever performs Hajj on behalf of another should say, from the moment of his ihram:

اللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أُرِيدُ الْحَجَّ عَنْ فُلَانٍ فَتَقَبَّلْ مِنْهُ وَأْجِرْنِي عَلَى قَضَائِي عَنْهُ

— O God, I intend the Hajj on behalf of so-and-so; accept it from him and reward me for discharging it on his behalf. That intention is to be held and pronounced at every station, at every sacred site and landmark, and in every act of Hajj, whether obligatory or recommended.

**Missed Hajj and Umrah**

Whoever performs the Hajj of tamattu — joining Umrah to Hajj — or the Hajj of qiran, and does not reach Mecca until he fears that performing the circumambulation and the running of his Umrah would cause him to miss the standing at Arafat, should hasten directly to the standing so that his Hajj may be completed. He makes it a single Hajj and performs the Umrah afresh afterward.

Whoever arrives to find that the people have already left Mina and are standing at Arafat, and reaches the standing before sunset, has attained the Hajj — even if he did not spend the night at Mina. Jafar ibn Muhammad said: likewise, if a pilgrim reaches Arafat after the people have already poured forth from it, stands there through the night in remembrance of Allah, and then comes to Jam before the people pour forth from Muzdalifah, he has attained the Hajj. But if he does not reach Muzdalifah until after the people have departed from it and the sun has risen, he has missed the Hajj.

Whoever misses the Hajj should convert his ihram into Umrah. The Hajj remains due from him on a future occasion — unless, at the time he entered ihram, he stipulated that his release would come wherever he was prevented, in which case no Hajj is owed for the missed occasion. Even so, Hajj remains incumbent on him if he has not yet performed his obligatory Hajj while being capable of making the journey. Similarly, if he does not reach the people until after the days of Hajj have passed, he should circumambulate the House and make it a single Umrah — provided he had been performing qiran or tamattu. If he had entered ihram for Hajj alone, however, no Umrah is owed, since he neither entered ihram for Umrah nor intended it, unless he returns to the miqat and enters ihram for it there.

---

**The Vow of Hajj**

Allah, mighty and majestic, said: "عَيْنًا يَشْرَبُ بِهَا عِبَادُ اللَّهِ يُفَجِّرُونَهَا تَفْجِيرًا ۝ يُوفُونَ بِالنَّذْرِ وَيَخَافُونَ يَوْمًا كَانَ شَرُّهُ مُسْتَطِيرًا" — *A fountain from which the servants of Allah drink, making it gush forth abundantly; they fulfill their vows and fear a Day whose evil is far-reaching* [Al-Insan: 6–7]. What vows are binding and what are not will be addressed in the chapters on vows, God willing.

Whoever makes a binding vow to perform Hajj must fulfill it. If he has not yet performed his obligatory Hajj, the vow suffices in place of the Hajj of Islam. Abu Abdillah Jafar ibn Muhammad said that if he intends this and pronounces it, it is credited to him. Likewise, if he performs Hajj on behalf of another while he has also vowed to perform Hajj, that act fulfills his vow and is credited to him.

Ali is reported to have said: "Whoever vows to perform Hajj and is then unable to do so, let him send his sacrificial animal and enter ihram, remaining in the state of ihram among his household until it is sacrificed on his behalf."

Jafar ibn Muhammad, for his part, addressed the case of one who makes a binding vow to walk to the Sacred House of God: such a person must walk. When he grows weary and can no longer continue on foot, he may ride and offer a sacrificial animal, for God does not burden a soul beyond its capacity. Once he has given the full measure of his effort, nothing further is required of him. A binding vow is one made in obedience to God; whoever vows to commit a sin has nothing binding upon him.

## 9. The Sword and the State: Jihad, Governance, and Justice

**The Obligation of Jihad and the Virtues Therein**

> أُذِنَ لِلَّذِينَ يُقَتَلُونَ بِأَنَّهُمْ ظُلِمُوا۟ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ عَلَىٰ نَصْرِهِمْ لَقَدِيرٌ

Allah Almighty said: "Fighting is ordained for you, though it is hateful to you. It may be that you dislike something that is good for you, and it may be that you love something that is bad for you." [Al-Baqarah: 216] He said: "When the sacred months have passed, slay the polytheists wherever you find them." [Al-Tawbah: 5] And He said: "Permission is granted to those who are fought against because they have been wronged, and Allah is indeed able to grant them victory; those who were driven from their homes unjustly, only because they said, Our Lord is Allah." [Al-Hajj: 39–40]

Al-Sadiq, Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع), said: "The earth and all that is in it belongs to Allah, to His friends, and to the believers among His servants. Whatever of it lies in the hands of the people of disbelief and misguidance, they hold only by usurpation. From this, what the believers overcome and reclaim from the hands of the polytheists is called *fay*. *Fay* means 'return'; one says *faa al-fay* — that is, the shadow returned. Allah Almighty said: 'But if they return, then Allah is Forgiving, Merciful' [Al-Baqarah: 226], meaning that they returned to their wives. Likewise, whatever the people of truth recover of what had been in the hands of the people of falsehood is among that which Allah has restored to them — that is, returned to them. From this comes His saying, 'What Allah restored to His Messenger' [Al-Hashr: 7], meaning returned to him. And this is the meaning of His words, 'Permission is granted to those who are fought against because they have been wronged' [Al-Hajj: 39]: the people of truth are meant, while those who wrong them are the people of falsehood."

Because the people of falsehood had seized what Allah had granted to the people of truth, He gave the people of truth permission to fight those who had wronged them. He made it obligatory upon the Muslims to wage jihad against the disbelievers among the People of the Book, until they either submitted or paid the jizyah in a state of subjection. He said, exalted be the Speaker: "Fight those who do not believe in Allah or the Last Day, who do not forbid what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, and who do not follow the religion of truth from among those who were given the Book, until they pay the jizyah from their hand while they are subdued." [Al-Tawbah: 29]

He likewise made obligatory the fighting of those polytheists who are not People of the Book, until they submitted. He said, exalted be the Speaker: "And fight the polytheists all together as they fight you all together." [Al-Tawbah: 36] And He said: "When the sacred months have passed, slay the polytheists wherever you find them, and capture them, besiege them, and sit in wait for them at every place of ambush. But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakah, then let them go their way." [Al-Tawbah: 5]

From those polytheists who are not People of the Book, nothing was accepted but Islam or the sword. From the People of the Book, the jizyah was accepted on the condition that they submit to the rule of Islam — until Allah guides whom He guides among them and such a person turns back to Him, or until whoever perishes upon his error does so with the proof already established against him.

> ٱنفِرُوا۟ خِفَافًۭا وَثِقَالًۭا وَجَهِدُوا۟ بِأَمْوَلِكُمْ وَأَنفُسِكُمْ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ

Allah urged jihad in His Book and on the tongue of His Messenger (ص), commanding it and calling to it with insistence. He said, exalted be His praise: "Go forth, light and heavy, and strive with your wealth and your selves in the way of Allah." [Al-Tawbah: 41] Al-Sadiq, Jafar ibn Muhammad, explained the phrase "light and heavy" as meaning the young and the old.

Allah, blessed and exalted, also said: "Indeed, Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth in exchange for Paradise. They fight in the way of Allah, so they slay and are slain. It is a promise binding upon Him in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Quran. And who is more faithful to his covenant than Allah? So rejoice in the sale you have contracted with Him, and that is the great triumph." [Al-Tawbah: 111]

Abu Abdullah, Jafar ibn Muhammad, was asked about this verse. It was said to him: "Is everyone who strives in the way of Allah among the people of this verse?" He (ع) said: "No. Allah Almighty said that He purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth in exchange for Paradise, then He promised them further, and He described those who deserve this after the verse with their qualities, saying: The repentant, the worshippers, the praisers, the wanderers, the bowing, the prostrating, those who enjoin what is good and forbid what is evil, and those who observe the limits of Allah; and give glad tidings to the believers. [Al-Tawbah: 112]" Whoever possesses these qualities is among those to whom Allah Almighty extended the promise stated in the verse. But whoever does not possess these qualities and strives seeking the fleeting gains of this world and its glory, or is coerced into jihad while not keeping his religion and not being among the people of this description that Allah Almighty set out in His Book, is among those of whom the Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Allah will surely support this religion through a people who have no share in it."

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "The strivers in the way of Allah are the leaders of the people of Paradise." He (ص) also said: "Concerning every comfort a man will be questioned, save what was in the way of Allah." And he (ص) said: "Above every act of righteousness is another act of righteousness, until a man is slain in the way of Allah" — meaning there is no righteousness above that. He (ص) said further: "There is no drop more beloved to Allah than a drop of blood shed in the way of Allah, or a drop of tears shed from fear of Allah." He (ص) declared that Allah and His angels send blessings upon the owners of horses — upon whoever keeps and prepares them in the way of Allah. And he (ص) said: "The provision spent on the horses tethered in the way of Allah is the very spending of which Allah Almighty said: *Those who spend their wealth by night and by day, secretly and openly.* [Al-Baqarah: 274]"

Ali (ع) said: "Strive in the way of Allah with your hands; if you are unable, then strive with your tongues; and if you are unable, then strive with your hearts." He also said: "Jihad in the way of Allah is a gate among the gates of Paradise." And concerning the words of Allah Almighty, *"And the garment of taqwa, that is best"* [Al-Araf: 26], he said: "It is the garment of weapons in the way of Allah."

Jihad in the way of Allah, the Master said, is an obligation upon all Muslims, by the words of Allah Almighty, *"Fighting is ordained for you."* If a group of Muslims undertakes the jihad and fulfills it sufficiently, the obligation is lifted from those who remain behind — until the fighters come to need reinforcement. When they need it, the obligation falls upon all Muslims to reinforce them until the need is met. He then recited the words of Allah Almighty, *"It was not for the believers to go forth all at once"* [Al-Tawbah: 122], understanding this to mean: when a group among them has already undertaken the jihad.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said that the bedouin among Muslims carry no obligation of jihad unless they are needed, and they have no share in the fay so long as they do not strive. Those like the bedouin today are the people of the outlying lands, the deserts, and the towns — those who do not fight well and have no desire for jihad. The Messenger of Allah (ص) granted the coward a dispensation to hold back from fighting, saying that a coward should not go on a raid, for he may be routed quickly. Rather, let him consider what he had intended to equip himself with for the raid, and let him equip another man with it instead — for he shall have the like of that man's reward, and nothing of that man's reward shall be diminished.

Those described above — the cowardly in fighting, who do not recognize their own cowardice, and who stand in the condition of the bedouin — are, if they possess wealth, obliged to spend it in the way of Allah. Allah Almighty imposed jihad upon the Muslims with both their persons and their wealth. Whoever is unable to strive with his person must strive with his wealth, in accordance with what the Messenger of Allah (ص) said concerning the coward.

If a grave calamity should fall upon the Muslims — and refuge from it is sought in Allah — then going forth in the way of Allah becomes an obligation upon all people. Allah Almighty said: "Go forth, light and heavy" [Al-Tawbah: 41], and He said: "And fight the polytheists all together" [Al-Tawbah: 36]. In such an hour the common people go forth: the free and the slave, the old and the young alike, until Allah Almighty repels what has befallen them. Jihad is not obligatory upon slaves in any circumstance other than this, unless their masters bring them out to it; nor is it obligatory upon women, nor upon boys. Yet if a woman wishes to go out with her husband, or with a close kinsman within the forbidden degrees (مَحْرَم), to tend the sick and the wounded, to serve those striving in Allah's way, and to help with whatever she is able and has strength for, there is no harm in that. If she has a husband, she may not go out except with his permission; and likewise the slave may not go out except with his master's permission.

**Supplication, Righteous Deeds, and the Etiquette of Travel in Jihad**

> وَلَمَّا بَرَزُوا۟ لِجَالُوتَ وَجُنُودِهِۦ قَالُوا۟ رَبَّنَآ أَفْرِغْ عَلَيْنَا صَبْرًۭا وَثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَنَا وَٱنصُرْنَا عَلَى ٱلْقَوْمِ ٱلْكَفِرِينَ

Allah Almighty said: "And when they went forth against Goliath and his soldiers, they said: Our Lord, pour upon us patience, make firm our feet, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people." [Al-Baqarah: 250] And Allah Almighty said: "The Messenger has believed in what was revealed to him from his Lord, and so have the believers" [Al-Baqarah: 285], up to His words: "You are our Protector, so grant us victory over the disbelieving people." [Al-Baqarah: 286]

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: No man who intends a journey appoints over his family a deputy better than two rakahs he prays at his departure, then says: "O Allah, I entrust to You myself, my family, my wealth, my religion, my worldly life, my Hereafter, my trust, and the conclusion of my deeds."
