No believer does this but that Allah grants him what he asked.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: When you intend a journey, pray two rakahs, then say this saying, and then say: "O Allah, protect me and protect what is with me, and bring me and what is with me to my destination in Your fair conveyance." And ask Him your needs for what you intend of your journey, and for those of your family you leave behind, and for whatever else.

Abu Jafar, Muhammad ibn Ali (ع), said: When my father, Ali ibn al-Husayn (ع), wished to travel or to go out to some of his property, he would pray two rakahs and supplicate with whatever was easy for him when he wished to set out. Then, when he placed his foot in the stirrup, he would purchase his safety from Allah with whatever he could — meaning that he would give charity at that moment. And when Allah brought him home in safety, he would praise Allah, thank Him, and again give charity with whatever was easy.

When the Commander of the Believers, Ali, set out on one of his journeys and had settled himself upon his mount, he said: "Praise be to Allah, who has favored us of His bounty with what we cannot number. May Allah bless Muhammad, His Prophet, and his family. Glory be to Him who has subjected this to us, for we could not have done it ourselves, and indeed to our Lord we shall return."

This last phrase is drawn from the Book of Allah — from His words, *"that you may settle yourselves upon it"* — the verse [Az-Zukhruf: 13]. Ali continued: "I bear witness that there is no god but Allah alone, having no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His Messenger. O Allah, I seek refuge in You from the hardship of travel, the gloom of return, and the evil sight in family, wealth, and children. O Allah, You are the Companion in travel, the Deputy over the family, and the One whose aid is sought in every affair. Fold up for us the far distance, ease for us its ruggedness, and suffice us in what weighs upon us, for You are powerful over all things."

He also used to say that it is part of the proper conduct of travel that when a group sets out together and keeps company on the road, they should pool their provisions and spend from them in common — for that is more wholesome for their souls and better for the bond between them.

Jafar ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq said that manly virtue (اَلْمُرُوَّة) is of two kinds: a muruwwa belonging to settled life, and a muruwwa belonging to travel. The muruwwa of settled life consists in reciting the Quran, frequenting the mosques, keeping the company of righteous people, and studying jurisprudence. The muruwwa of travel consists in generosity with one's provisions, refraining from dispute with one's companions, and speaking well of them after parting.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) forbade three things regarding riding beasts: neglecting them to the point of ruin, loading them beyond their strength, and sitting upon one when there is no need to do so. He said that many a riding beast is better than the one who rides it — more devoted to Allah and more constant in His remembrance. He also said that a riding beast has six rights upon its owner:

1. When he dismounts, he begins by seeing to its fodder.
2. When he passes by water, he offers it to drink.
3. He does not strike it except for a just cause.
4. He does not load it with what it cannot bear.
5. He does not compel it to a journey beyond its strength.
6. He does not remain halted upon it for any length of time without need.

> وَلَهُۥٓ أَسْلَمَ مَن فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ طَوْعًۭا وَكَرْهًۭا وَإِلَيْهِ يُرْجَعُونَ

It is related of Ali that he said one day: "By Him who sent Muhammad with the truth and ennobled his household through him, there is nothing by which you are afflicted but that it is in the Quran. Whoever wishes to question me, let him question me." A man rose and said: "O Commander of the Believers, my riding beast has become exceedingly stubborn toward me, and I am in dread of it." Ali said: "Recite into its right ear: And to Him has submitted whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly and unwillingly, and to Him they will be returned. [Al Imran: 83]" The man did so, and his beast became docile to him.

Once, while the people were traveling with the Messenger of Allah (ص) on one of his expeditions, they crowded the road. He commanded a crier to call out: "Whoever narrows a road has no jihad." He then said: "The owner of a riding beast has more right to the main track than the man on foot, and the barefoot man has more right to it than the one wearing sandals."

He (ص) also said: "Allah loves gentleness and helps toward it. When you ride these dumb beasts, if the land is barren, hasten upon them while there is yet marrow in them" — urging his companions to press on while the beasts still had their vigor and had not grown lean — "that you may pass out of the barren stretch; and if the land is fertile, alight with them at its resting places. Travel by night, for the earth is folded up by night and is not folded up by day. Do not encamp in the middle of the roads, for they are the runs of wild beasts and the lairs of serpents."

The Messenger of Allah (ص) further commanded those on foot to quicken their pace when marching — a light trot — and said that it drives away weariness.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said that a protection for his community against drowning, when they board a ship, is to recite upon embarkation: "In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Most Compassionate," followed by: "They have not appraised Allah with the appraisal due to Him, while the whole earth will be within His grip on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens folded in His right hand. Exalted is He and high above what they associate with Him. [Az-Zumar: 67]," and: "In the name of Allah is its course and its anchorage; indeed my Lord is Forgiving, Merciful." It is related of Ali that he added to this supplication: "O Allah, bless us in our vessel, make our passage good, and keep us safe from the evil of our sea."

**What Is Due to the Commanders of Armies from Those Under Them**

> ٱللَّهِ وَٱلرَّسُولِ إِن كُنتُمْ

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, obey Allah, obey the Messenger, and those in authority among you." [Al-Nisa: 59]

Authority is committed to the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), to whom the whole of command belongs. Whoever the Imams place over a matter is the guardian of that matter. He may not overstep what they have appointed him to, and obedience to him is obligatory — within what they have entrusted to him, and in that which is right. If, however, he commands an evil, or contradicts his Imam's command and transgresses against him, no obedience is owed to him.

It is related of the Messenger of Allah (ص) that he dispatched a company and appointed over them a man from the Ansar, commanding them to obey him. The man was hard of temper, and he grew angry with them over some matter. He said: "Gather firewood for me." They did so. He then ordered them to gather more until a great pile had been built up, and commanded them to set it alight. When the fire blazed high, he said: "Did not the Messenger of Allah (ص) command you to obey me?" They said: "Yes." He said: "Then enter this fire." Some among them were on the point of doing so out of obedience, but others held them back, saying: "We fled to the Messenger of Allah (ص) from the fire." They remained as they were until the fire burned out and the man's anger cooled. When news of this reached the Messenger of Allah (ص), he said: "Had they entered it, they would never have come out of it."

The Prophet (ص) taught that his community would be kept safe from drowning if, upon boarding a vessel, they recited: *"In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. They did not esteem Allah as He deserves to be esteemed, yet the whole earth is in His grip on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens are folded in His right hand; exalted is He above what they associate with Him"* [al-Zumar: 67], followed by: *"In the name of Allah is its course and its anchorage; indeed, my Lord is Forgiving and Merciful."* It is transmitted from Ali that he appended to this supplication: "O Allah, bless us in our vessel, make our journey excellent, and preserve us from the evil of our sea."

> ٱللَّهِ وَٱلرَّسُولِ إِن كُنتُمْ

What follows concerns what the commanders of armies are obliged to observe toward those under their command. Allah the Almighty said: *"O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you"* [al-Nisa: 59]. Those upon whom this command confers authority are the Imams, who bear it in full. Whoever holds authority over a matter is the guardian of that matter, and obedience to him in what is good is obligatory. If, however, he commands something evil, or issues an order that openly contradicts the command of his own Imam, no obedience is owed him in that.

It was narrated that the Messenger of Allah (ص) once dispatched a man in command over a group from the Ansar and instructed them to obey him. The commander grew angry over something and said, "Gather wood for me." They did so. He then ordered them to gather more, until a great pile had been assembled, and finally commanded that it be set alight. When the fire was blazing, he said, "Did not the Messenger of Allah (ص) command you to obey me?" They said, "Yes." He then ordered them to enter the fire. Some among them were inclined toward obedience and moved toward it, but others held them back, saying, "We fled to the Messenger of Allah (ص) precisely to escape the fire." They remained in this state until the fire died down and the commander's anger subsided. When word of this reached the Messenger of Allah (ص), he said: "Had they entered it, they would not have come out of it until the Day of Resurrection. Obedience is only in what is good."

When an Imam appoints a commander or assigns a man to a task, that man must obey him, carry out his orders, complete what he is charged with, and never act ahead of him in any matter. Obedience to that commander is obedience to Allah, obedience to the Messenger of Allah, and obedience to the Imam who appointed him. If those under command come to believe that the commander has exceeded his charge, or if they object to some conduct of his, they should refer the matter to the Imam. Once they have done so, their responsibility is discharged and nothing further rests with them; the matter then passes to the Imam (ع), who examines it by what Allah the Almighty has disclosed to him. If, however, a man openly breaks from obedience to the Imam and declares himself in opposition, his claim on the obedience of others ceases entirely. It then becomes obligatory for those the Imam appoints to that purpose, and for all who answer a just leader, to fight against him.

Whoever is summoned by a just Imam to a task he assigns must answer that summons, taking on whatever he is capable of performing. He must not, however, accept office under unjust rulers and the partisans of prejudice, nor lend his aid to their works. It has been narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad that he said: "Guardianship under the just, working with them, assisting them in their governance, and earning through them and with them — all of this is lawful and permitted. But guardianship under corrupt and unjust rulers, serving as their agent, supporting their authority, and earning through them and with them — this is forbidden and prohibited." The Messenger of Allah (ص) forbade assisting wrongdoers, and it was said: "The oppressor, the one who assists him, and the one content with him are partners in the sin."

> إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يَأْمُرُكُمْ أَن تُؤَدُّوا۟ ٱلْأَمَنَتِ إِلَىٰٓ أَهْلِهَا وَإِذَا حَكَمْتُم بَيْنَ ٱلنَّاسِ أَن تَحْكُمُوا۟ بِٱلْعَدْلِ

The commanders themselves carry obligations of their own. Allah the Almighty said: *"Indeed, Allah enjoins justice and charity"* [al-Nahl: 90], and He said: *"Indeed, Allah commands you to restore trusts to their rightful owners, and when you judge between people, to judge with justice"* [al-Nisa: 58].

In the covenant the Messenger of Allah (ص) gave to one of his army commanders, he directed him to fill his heart with mercy toward those in his charge — with tenderness and kindness in his dealings with them — and not to become a predator over them, pouncing upon their slips and stumbles. For these men are brothers in lineage and equals in creation. Error escapes them, afflictions befall them, and their hands work amiss and go astray. He therefore urged the commander to grant them pardon and forbearance in the measure he would wish pardon and forbearance granted to himself. He was to deal justly with his own self and with those closest to him, just as he dealt justly with others.

He was further directed not to lay bare the faults and hidden failings of the people when those failings were veiled from him, and to regard as the most hateful of men the one who brought such things to his attention. He was to keep close to the people of piety and truthfulness and to those of sound understanding, and not to be pleased by an abundance of praise, for abundant praise is a summons to vanity. He was to acknowledge the good of those who do good and overlook in the wrongdoer whatever may rightly be overlooked. Each man was to be rewarded according to his effort, whether noble or lowly, and judgment among them was to be rendered with justice — for judgment with justice is the balance of equity that Allah has placed upon the earth.

The prescribed limits were to be upheld in their proper course, and every possessor of a right was to receive his right. The covenant was to be kept, the pledge fulfilled, and haste in shedding blood — without verification and clear proof — strictly avoided. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "None may enjoin the good or forbid the evil except one in whom there are three qualities: gentleness in what he enjoins and gentleness in what he forbids, justice in what he enjoins and justice in what he forbids, and knowledge of what he enjoins and knowledge of what he forbids."

> ٱدْعُ إِلَىٰ سَبِيلِ رَبِّكَ بِٱلْحِكْمَةِ وَٱلْمَوْعِظَةِ ٱلْحَسَنَةِ

As for the call to truth before fighting, Allah the Almighty said: *"Invite to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good counsel"* [al-Nahl: 125]. Whenever the Messenger of Allah (ص) dispatched an army or a detachment, he would counsel its commander to fear Allah in his own person and to treat well the Muslims under his charge. He would say: "Advance in the name of Allah, for the sake of Allah, and upon the religion of the Messenger of Allah. Do not fight a people until you have first presented the call to them. Invite them to bear witness that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and to acknowledge what I have brought from Allah. If they answer you, they are your brothers in religion. Then invite them to move from their land to the land of the emigrants. If they do so, well and good; but if they decline, inform them that they shall be as the bedouin of the Muslims, subject to the judgment of Allah that governs all Muslims, though they shall have no share in the spoils or the booty. If they have already embraced Islam, call them to pay the jizya from a willing hand while they remain under authority. If they answer to that, accept it from them and hold your hand from them. But if they refuse, then seek the help of Allah against them and fight them. Do not kill a child, an aged man, or a woman — that is, so long as they do not fight you."

Do not mutilate, do not betray a trust, and do not break a pledge." This is what the Messenger of Allah (ص) commanded: that the polytheists be called to Islam before fighting begins.

Whoever issues that call has done well. In the same way, when relations with rebels have broken down, they too must first be called to return to the truth — to enter obedience to the just Imam and submit to sound judgment before the sword is raised. If, however, they already know full well what they are being summoned to and what is at stake, and they open the fighting before any call is made, there is no harm in meeting them on those terms.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) raided the Banu al-Mustaliq and fell upon them without issuing a fresh call at that moment — killing their fighters and taking their women and children captive, coming upon them while they were unaware — because the call had already reached them in their own tongue.

Ali said: "There is no harm in fighting before the call, for the people today already know what they are being summoned to."

Among the recommended practices in combat and before it, the Messenger of Allah (ص) sent a man of Khuzaah ahead of him as a scout in the year of al-Hudaybiyah — when he intended the lesser pilgrimage — to gather intelligence on the Quraysh and bring it back to him. He would also dispatch advance parties before his armies to uncover ambush points and probe the enemy's movements. At Uhud he had a trench dug around his army's encampment, and he instructed his companions to adopt a watchword by which they could call out to and recognize one another in the confusion of battle, when the ranks mingled. He commanded them to patience, reminded them of the reward, dealt sternly with flight, and roused them to steadfastness.

When he faced the enemy, he (ص) would say: "O Allah, You are my protection, my helper, and my defender. O Allah, by You I attack, by You I prevail, and by You I fight."

On the day of Uhud he said: "O Allah, to You is all praise, to You is the complaint, and You are the One whose help is sought."

Jibril (ع) then came down to him and said: "O Muhammad, you have called upon Allah by His greatest name."

He (ص) also said: "Seize the moment of supplication at five occasions: at the recitation of the Quran, at the call to prayer, at the descent of the rain, at the meeting of the two ranks in battle, and at the supplication of the wronged — for none of these is veiled from the Throne."

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) met the enemy, he would draw up the infantry, position the cavalry, and place the camels and the baggage-train to the rear, so that his forces would appear more numerous. He separated the tribes in the ordering of the ranks, assigning each company — each tribe — its own flank and its own banner, so that any man cut off from his tribe could look to that banner and find his way back.

Ali disliked opening the fighting until the sun had passed its zenith and the winds had begun to stir. He engaged in single combat many times, and never did he face an opponent without slaying him. Recounting the day Marhab challenged him at Khaybar, he said: "The Messenger of Allah (ص) taught me what to say at the moment of single combat: O Allah, grant me victory and do not give victory against me; O Allah, prevail for me and do not turn the tide against me; O Allah, take charge of me and do not set another over me; O Allah, make me one who remembers You, thankful to You, faithful, and obedient. I slew Marhab that day and left his spoils where they lay, for I would kill and not take the spoils."

The supplication of the Messenger of Allah (ص) on the day of al-Ahzab was: "O Aid of the distressed, O Answerer of those who cry out, lift my anguish from me, for You know my state and the state of my companions, and spare me the terrors of my enemy."

And Ali, when he put on his coat of mail for battle, would say: "O Allah, You are my protection."

> إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُحِبُّ ٱلَّذِينَ يُقَتِلُونَ فِى سَبِيلِهِۦ صَفًّۭا كَأَنَّهُم بُنْيَنٌۭ مَّرْصُوصٌۭ

Ali taught that when the Muslims stand facing their enemy and have taken up their ranks, every man must hold his post. If he sees an unguarded opening in the enemy's line, he may press into it, then return to his place. When the enemy opens the assault, the Muslims are to stand firm where they stand, as Allah the Almighty said: *"O you who believe, when you meet a company, stand firm and remember Allah much, that you may succeed"* [al-Anfal: 45], and as He said: إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ الَّذِينَ يُقَاتِلُونَ فِي سَبِيلِهِ صَفًّا كَأَنَّهُم بُنيَانٌ مَّرْصُوصٌ — *"Indeed, Allah loves those who fight in His cause in ranks, as though they were a solid structure"* [al-Saff: 4].

If the enemy is routed, he said, they are not to be pursued outright until the rout is complete and any counter-charge is secured against; only then does the whole force press forward and press hard in the slaughter.

Should there be, Allah forbid, a rout among the Muslims, the commander, and the Imam if he is present, must stand fast. The people must rally one another to return, reminding one another of what Allah has promised to those who hold firm, and warning of the fate of those who turn their backs.

Ali recalled the steadfastness of the Messenger of Allah (ص) on the day of Uhud, when most of the people had scattered from him, saying: "I remained with the Messenger of Allah (ص) upon the rock, shielding him with my own body, warding off the blows — once with my hand and once with my face — while the Messenger of Allah (ص) was wounded and many of the people had made off toward Medina, some saying that the Messenger of Allah (ص) had been slain.

Yet he stood firm until Allah the Almighty routed the polytheists away from him.

He said further that there is no harm in a single fighter charging an entire squadron, provided he has confidence in himself — no harm in advancing with the spear and thrusting with it so that his companions may rise up around him with swords, spears, and whatever other weapons they carry, so long as this is not done recklessly. He then cited the words of Allah the Almighty: *"So do not turn your backs to them. Whoever turns his back to them on that day, unless maneuvering for battle or falling back to a company, has drawn upon himself the wrath of Allah, and his refuge is Hell, and wretched is the destination"* [al-Anfal: 15].

He said: "Fleeing from the battlefield is among the gravest of sins."

Turning one's back is not condemned in itself — Allah the Almighty permitted it when done to regroup and rejoin a company. The condemnation falls only on headlong flight and rout.

Allah the Almighty had first made it an obligation upon the Muslims to stand firm against ten times their number, saying: *"If there are twenty of you who are steadfast, they will overcome two hundred; and if there are a hundred of you, they will overcome a thousand"* [al-Anfal: 65]. This weighed heavily upon them, so Allah lightened the burden, saying: *"Now Allah has lightened your burden, knowing that there is weakness in you. So if there are a hundred of you who are steadfast, they will overcome two hundred; and if there are a thousand of you, they will overcome two thousand"* [al-Anfal: 66].

A man who flees from two polytheists, or fewer, or from a force equal to or smaller than his own, falls under the ruling of the threat and commits one of the grave sins. If, however, a force withdraws from an enemy that outnumbers them beyond that threshold, after exhausting every effort and reaching the limit of their strength, no blame attaches to them.

When fighters break off from battle, they are to withdraw in good order and travel with the same discipline, keeping watch against the enemy's ambushes and stratagems. If they have driven the enemy back to their fortresses or their trenches, they are not to turn away until full effort has been spent and every capacity reached. Should the enemy lay siege to them, they are to fight.

On the slaying of the polytheists, Allah the Almighty said: *"So slay the polytheists wherever you find them, and seize them, and besiege them, and lie in wait for them"* [al-Tawba: 5], and He said: *"And slay them wherever you overtake them, and drive them out from where they drove you out"* [al-Baqara: 191]. The Imams permitted the killing of polytheists by weapon, by fire, by stones, by water, and by whatever means their killing can be accomplished, and that catapults and stone-throwing engines be set against them when they take refuge in their fortresses.

The discussion now moves from the vows a person binds upon himself to the far weightier matter of how the community meets its enemies in the field — for here, too, the measure is justice and not appetite, and the ruler is bound by revealed limits at every step.

On the killing of the polytheists, Allah Almighty said: ﴿فَاقْتُلُوا الْمُشْرِكِينَ حَيْثُ وَجَدْتُمُوهُمْ وَخُذُوهُمْ وَاحْصُرُوهُمْ وَاقْعُدُوا لَهُمْ﴾ — "Kill the polytheists wherever you find them, and capture them, and besiege them, and sit in wait for them" [Al-Tawbah: 5]. And He said: ﴿وَاقْتُلُوهُمْ حَيْثُ ثَقِفْتُمُوهُمْ وَأَخْرِجُوهُم مِّنْ حَيْثُ أَخْرَجُوكُمْ﴾ — "And kill them wherever you encounter them and drive them out from where they drove you out" [Al-Baqarah: 191]. The Imams permitted the killing of polytheists by weapons, by fire, by stones, by water, and by whatever means their killing can be accomplished, and that catapults and stone-throwing engines be set against them when they take refuge in their fortresses. The discussion now moves from the vows a person binds upon himself to the far weightier matter of how the community meets its enemies in the field — for here, too, the measure is justice and not appetite, and the ruler is bound by revealed limits at every step.

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ لَا تَقْتُلُوا۟ ٱلصَّيْدَ وَأَنتُمْ حُرُمٌۭ ۚ وَمَن قَتَلَهُۥ مِنكُم مُّتَعَمِّدًۭا فَجَزَآءٌۭ مِّثْلُ مَا قَتَلَ مِنَ ٱلنَّعَمِ

Yet the sword is not loosed without restraint. When Muslims and dhimmis are found among the enemy, along with those who must not be killed — children, women, the elderly, and the incapacitated who take no part in the fighting — no one may aim at them deliberately, and every precaution must be taken on their behalf. Whoever deliberately kills a child, a woman, an elderly person, or an incapacitated person among the polytheists or their monks has committed a sin. Where such persons are killed incidentally while the Muslims are engaged against the polytheists, with no deliberate intent, no fault falls upon the Muslim for that. Nor is anyone among the polytheists to be killed who has shown kindness to the Muslims. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said to the Muslims on the Day of Badr, "Whoever you are able to capture from the Bani Abd al-Muttalib, do not kill him, for they were brought out against you unwillingly."

Likewise, any polytheist who shows a sign of Islam is not to be killed. The Messenger of Allah (ص) once dispatched an army toward the tribe of Khatham, and when that force approached, some of the enemy prostrated themselves in submission — yet some were killed while still in prostration. The Messenger of Allah (ص) condemned this and ordered that half the blood money be paid for those who had died in that state. He then said, "I am free of every Muslim who takes up residence among polytheists."

He also forbade the killing of envoys. If it becomes known that a person who has entered the abode of Islam without any covenant of safe conduct is in fact an envoy of the polytheists, he is not to be killed — and this holds even when he claims to be an envoy but brings no proof and his mission cannot be verified. In such a case the Imam has a choice:

- He may kill him.
- He may take him captive.
- He may release him.

There is no harm in imprisoning the envoy when his conduct gives cause for concern and releasing him would pose a risk. Should the Imam become reassured on that score, however, he sets the man free and exercises his own judgment in the matter.

On captives and those granted security, Allah Almighty said: ﴿فَإِذَا لَقِيتُمُ الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا فَضَرْبَ الرِّقَابِ حَتَّى إِذَا أَثْخَنتُمُوهُمْ فَشُدُّوا الْوَثَاقَ فَإِمَّا مَنًّا بَعْدُ وَإِمَّا فِدَاءً حَتَّى تَضَعَ الْحَرْبُ أَوْزَارَهَا﴾ "When you meet those who disbelieve, strike the necks until, when you have subdued them, bind the bonds firmly. Then either show favor afterward or take ransom, until the war lays down its burdens." [Muhammad: 4]

On the Day of Badr, the Messenger of Allah (ص) took captive a group from Quraysh: some he ransomed, some he released as a favor, and some he put to death. The Imam holds an equivalent range of choices regarding prisoners:

- He may put them to death.
- He may release them as a favor.
- He may exchange them for Muslim captives held by the enemy.
- He may accept ransom — either from the polytheists or from the captives themselves, when they possess wealth in the abode of war.

Whatever is found in their possession falls within his consideration. He must weigh each case on its own terms and seek in his judgment what is most pleasing to Allah Almighty.

Among those captured on the Day of Badr was al-Abbas, the uncle of the Messenger of Allah (ص). The Prophet already knew his true intention — that he had been brought out unwillingly, along with others of the Bani Abd al-Muttalib. After his capture, al-Abbas said, "O Prophet of Allah, I had already accepted Islam." The Prophet replied, "As for that, Allah knows best. But your outward conduct was against us, so ransom yourself if you wish." He was bound in cords with the rest of the captives, and when the tightness of the bonds pained him, the Messenger of Allah (ص) groaned through the night out of tenderness for him — for nothing else was open to him but what had already been done.

On that same day, Aqeel ibn Abi Talib was also taken captive. His place with the Messenger of Allah (ص) was one of particular closeness, for the Prophet would say to him, "I love you with a love for your kinship and your standing, and with a love for the love that Abu Talib bore you." When Ali passed by Aqeel among the captives that day, he turned away from him without a word. Aqeel called out, "O my brother, why do you turn away from me when you see the state I am in?" — but Ali gave no answer. When the Messenger of Allah (ص) was told of Aqeel's condition, he went to him, and when Aqeel saw the Prophet he said, "O Messenger of Allah, do not let the transgressor Abu Jahl escape you.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) smiled and said, "Rather, Allah has already killed him." Then he said to al-Abbas, "Ransom Aqeel along with yourself, for he has no wealth and you are a man of means."

Al-Abbas said, "O Messenger of Allah, ounces of gold were seized with me — count them toward my ransom."

He replied, "That is spoil which Allah has bestowed upon the Muslims. But send for what you had left behind for yourself at Makkah."

Al-Abbas said, "I left no wealth there."

The Prophet said, "Glory be to Allah. Then where is the wealth you deposited with Umm al-Fadl on the day of your departure, telling her to guard it until you returned, and saying that if death overtook you she should do with it such and such?"

Al-Abbas stared at him and said, "I bear witness that you are truly the Messenger of Allah. By Allah, none knew of this but she and I, and none could have disclosed it to you save your Lord."

He then sent for the wealth and ransomed himself and Aqeel with it.

---

The Imam, and whoever the Imam appoints in his place, must examine each captive taken from the polytheists. The course he takes depends on what serves the Muslims best:

- If killing the fighters among them is of greater benefit, he kills the combatants.
- If enslaving them is better, he enslaves them and casts lots over them.
- If showing favor or accepting ransom is better, he does that.

In every case he acts on what is best, guided by what Allah Almighty makes clear to him.

When the polytheists seek security and submit to the ruling of the Muslims — or to that of an arbitrator chosen from among them — whatever the arbitrator decides within the bounds of what is permissible is binding and must be carried out. His ruling may be:

- That the fighters be killed
- That the offspring be taken captive
- That they be enslaved
- That they be granted the standing of dhimmis if they refuse Islam

If, however, he rules otherwise — that they be left untouched and unraided, neither enslaved nor killed nor made to pay the jizyah, or anything else that contradicts the Book of Allah Almighty and the Sunnah of His Prophet (ص) — his ruling is overturned and does not stand. They are returned to their place of safety and fought until they submit or pay the jizyah.

When the polytheists have submitted to the ruling of the commander of the armies, no one else may rule over them except with their consent. When they have submitted to the ruling of a particular man, none may judge among them but the one they named.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) once had the Banu Qurayzah submit to the ruling of Sa'd ibn Mu'adh. Sa'd ruled that their fighters be killed, their women and offspring taken captive, and their wealth divided. The Messenger of Allah (ص) upheld his ruling over them and said, "You have judged among them by the judgment of Allah from above the seven heavens."

If the polytheists have submitted to the ruling of a man who dies before he can judge, the best and clearest course is that they either accept another man to judge among them or be returned to their place of safety and fought again.

Captives must be fed — even when the captor intends to kill them, feeding remains obligatory, for Allah Almighty says: ﴿وَيُطْعِمُونَ الطَّعَامَ عَلَى حُبِّهِ مِسْكِينًا وَيَتِيمًا وَأَسِيرًا﴾ "And they feed, out of love for Him, the poor, the orphan, and the captive." [Al-Insan: 8]

As for a Muslim held captive by the enemy, ransoming him is an obligation upon the Muslims whenever they are able. Al-Husayn ibn Ali (ع) was asked upon whom the ransom of a captive falls, and he said, "Upon the people of the land for which he was fighting." Where a Muslim is taken captive under other circumstances, his ransom falls upon the Muslims at large as a binding collective obligation; and whoever ransoms him freely, as a charitable act, is rewarded — if Allah wills.

When a Muslim grants security to a body of polytheists and they come down upon his guarantee, that guarantee may not be betrayed. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "The blood of the Muslims is of equal worth, and the least of them may extend their protection." The commander of the armies presents to them the rule of Islam; if they accept it, they are not returned to their former terms of safety, and their fighters are dealt with accordingly. Likewise, if the one who grants the security is a Muslim who is a slave, a woman, or anyone else, the guarantee still holds — for the Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "the least of them may extend their protection." If, however, the one who granted the security is a dhimmi, a captive, a child, or a Muslim held in the abode of war among the polytheists, then his grant is not valid. A recognized gesture of security carries the same force as a spoken word, and it is valid in any tongue so long as it is understood and known.

When a polytheist enters Muslim lands under safe conduct carrying merchandise, a tenth is levied on whatever exceeds two hundred dirhams or its equivalent in value. When he wishes to depart, he is not permitted to leave with weapons or anything else that would strengthen the polytheists in war — such as instruments of battle drawn from the abode of Islam. Whatever he is prevented from taking out, however, is not confiscated; it is returned to him within those lands.

If a polytheist woman is granted safe conduct and enters the abode of peace, the marriage bond between her and her polytheist husband is severed. If a man accepts Islam while already in the abode of Islam, whatever wealth and children he left behind in the abode of shirk remain there as spoil, should the Muslims prevail over that territory. If, however, he accepted Islam while still in the abode of shirk and then entered the abode of peace as a Muslim, his young children are Muslim by virtue of his Islam, and his wealth belongs to him.

All of this is the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

> فَسِيحُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ أَرْبَعَةَ أَشْهُرٍۢ

On reconciliation, truce, and the jizyah, Allah Almighty said: ﴿إِلَّا الَّذِينَ عَاهَدْتُمْ مِنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ ثُمَّ لَمْ يَنْقُصُوكُمْ شَيْئًا وَلَمْ يُظَاهِرُوا عَلَيْكُمْ أَحَدًا فَأَتِمُّوا إِلَيْهِمْ عَهْدَهُمْ إِلَى مُدَّتِهِمْ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ الْمُتَّقِينَ﴾ "Except those of the polytheists with whom you made a covenant, and who then failed you in nothing and supported no one against you: fulfill their covenant to them until their term. Surely Allah loves the God-fearing." [Al-Tawbah: 4]

The Messenger of Allah (ص) concluded a truce with the polytheists of Quraysh in the year of Hudaybiyah. The Imam — and whoever the Imam appoints — examines questions of reconciliation and truce between the Muslims and the polytheists. Where he judges it to be in their interest, he may grant a truce for a defined term. The maximum duration of such a truce is ten consecutive years. He honors the agreement for as long as he sees good in doing so. Should he later conclude that fighting serves the welfare of the Muslims, he returns their covenant to them, announces that he will take up arms against them, and grants them a period of grace — setting a term before the onset of hostilities so that those among them in distant lands may be warned.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) acted accordingly when Allah Almighty revealed: ﴿بَرَاءَةٌ مِّنَ اللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِ إِلَى الَّذِينَ عَاهَدتُّم مِّنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ﴾ "A declaration of immunity from Allah and His Messenger to those polytheists with whom you had made a covenant," and ﴿فَسِيحُوا فِي الْأَرْضِ أَرْبَعَةَ أَشْهُرٍ﴾ "so travel freely in the land for four months." [Al-Tawbah: 1] He sent Ali with this proclamation, who recited it to them during the pilgrimage season and gave them due warning. Furthermore, if the Imam had received any wealth from a people in exchange for a truce and then wished to dissolve it before its term, he returns to them whatever he had taken.

Where polytheists who are People of the Book offer the jizyah, it is accepted from them and the sword is withheld; the jizyah, however, is not accepted from the Arab polytheists, who are fought until they submit or are killed. Once the dhimmis have settled upon the jizyah, no wrong may be done to them beyond it, and a fixed, known tribute is levied each year upon whatever cultivated lands and fields remain in their possession. The jizyah is likewise taken from the Magi, for they are a people with an ancient scripture. When any person of the jizyah accepts Islam, the jizyah is lifted from him, while the land tribute remains upon his land; he is then bound to pay the tithe on what that land yields. Should the land pass into a Muslim's hands, it remains tribute land wherever it goes.

The jizyah falls upon free adult men among the dhimmis alone; it is not levied upon children, slaves, or women. Dhimmis are ranked in three tiers, and each year the following is collected:

- From every man of the highest tier — the people of ease and abundance — forty-eight dirhams
- From every man of the middle tier, twenty-four dirhams
- From every man of the lowest tier, twelve dirhams

Should any man among them fall into poverty and infirmity and become unable to work, the jizyah is lifted from him, in accordance with the word of Allah Almighty: ﴿لَا يُكَلِّفُ اللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا﴾ — "Allah burdens no soul beyond its capacity." [Al-Baqarah: 286]

The land tribute, by contrast, falls upon their lands and trees regardless of the owner's standing — great or small, man or woman. The jizyah is not imposed upon any of their notable men capable of bearing it unless there is some benefit and service to Islam in doing so; in that case the Imam may set it upon such a man as he sees fit, or may instead make use of him against the polytheists in war.

The jizyah is also drawn from the proceeds of whatever they sell that is unlawful for Muslims to sell — wine, swine, and the like — since the greater part of their wealth derives from such unlawful gain. There is no harm in Muslims transacting with them and receiving such payment, whether as jizyah or in settlement of any other amount owed.

They are not to be humbled beyond their due. Their churches may not be used as lodging, and the greeting of peace is not to be offered to them first. They are held accountable for their conduct and are forbidden from building new churches in the abode of Islam, though they are permitted to maintain their existing ones and to repair whatever has fallen into disrepair. They are barred from entering the sacred precincts. All of this is the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt.

> وَٱعْلَمُوٓا۟ أَنَّمَا غَنِمْتُم مِّن شَىْءٍۢ فَأَنَّ لِلَّهِ خُمُسَهُۥ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ وَٱلْيَتَمَىٰ وَٱلْمَسَكِينِ

On the ruling concerning spoils, Allah Almighty said: ﴿فَكُلُوا مِمَّا غَنِمْتُمْ حَلَالًا طَيِّبًا﴾ "So eat of what you have taken as spoils, lawful and good." [Al-Anfal: 69] And He said: ﴿وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّمَا غَنِمْتُم مِن شَيْءٍ فَأَنَّ لِلَّهِ خُمُسَهُ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِي الْقُرْبَى وَالْيَتَامَى وَالْمَسَاكِينِ﴾ "And know that whatever you take as spoils, a fifth of it belongs to Allah, and to the Messenger, and to the near kin, and the orphans, and the needy." [Al-Anfal: 41] And He said, regarding the one who steals from the spoils: ﴿وَمَن يَغْلُلْ يَأْتِ بِمَا غَلَّ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ ثُمَّ تُوَفَّى كُلُّ نَفْسٍ مَّا كَسَبَتْ وَهُمْ لَا يُظْلَمُونَ﴾ "And whoever defrauds will come with what he defrauded on the Day of Resurrection; then every soul will be paid in full for what it earned, and they will not be wronged." [Al Imran: 161] The Messenger of Allah (ص) said of one who had misappropriated a cloak from the spoils, "I saw him in the Fire, the man of the cloak that he had stolen from the plunder."

After returning from a campaign, the Messenger of Allah (ص) was accompanied by Aqeel ibn Abi Talib. When Aqeel came in to his wife, she said to him, "I know you have been in the fighting — have you brought us any spoil?" He said, "No, by Allah, except for this needle that I came upon and took," and he drew it out from the edge of his garment and handed it to her, and she began to marvel at what he said. While they were still in that exchange, the crier of the Messenger of Allah (ص) called out, "O people, hand back the needle and the thread; let no one make off with so much as a needle or what is beyond it." Aqeel then said to his wife, "I do not think your needle is anything but lost to you," and he took it from her and cast it back into the spoils.

There is no harm in making use of spoils in the course of jihad before they are divided — such as weapons, mounts, and the like — but nothing from the spoils may be sold until the division has been made. The commander of the army may select for himself a choice portion of the spoils as he sees fit, without going beyond that. And whatever spoils the Muslims are unable to carry away, they destroy, burning the goods and the weaponry.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) sent Ali on an expedition to Yemen and sent Khalid ibn al-Walid on another, with the instruction: "If you meet, let Ali be the commander over all of you; and if you separate, each of you leads his own force." They met, and Ali assumed command. He engaged the enemy, routed them, slew the fighters, took their offspring captive, and from the spoils chose for himself a slave girl. Khalid ibn al-Walid wrote to the Messenger of Allah (ص) in complaint, and his courier delivered the letter to the Prophet. The Prophet (ص) said of Ali, "Do not find fault with what Ali chose. Ali is from me and I am from him, and he is your guardian after me" (عَلِيٌّ مِنِّي وَأَنَا مِنْهُ وَهُوَ وَلِيُّكُمْ بَعْدِي). The ruling that follows from this is clear: the Imam, and likewise the commander of a detachment, may each take for himself from the spoils, before division has been made, one thing of his choosing.

Whatever spoils the Muslims cannot carry away, they destroy, so that the enemy may not benefit from them afterward. Goods and weapons are burned, and livestock are slaughtered and burned as well. Nothing is mutilated, and no portion of the booty that cannot be transported is left behind for the enemy's use. As for Muslim property that has fallen into enemy hands, ownership of it remains with its original holder. The polytheists seizing it does not extinguish a Muslim's claim, and nothing extinguishes that claim except a willing surrender by the owner himself.

On one occasion, the polytheists raided the camels of the Messenger of Allah (ص) and his household, driving them off. Among the camels was al-Adba (الْعَضْبَاء). They also took with them an old woman who had been among the herd. During the night she rose, mounted al-Adba, and rode until she reached Madinah. The people recognized the she-camel of the Messenger of Allah (ص) and brought her, along with the old woman, to him. The old woman told him what had happened and said, "O Messenger of Allah, I vowed on my journey that if Allah delivered me I would slaughter this camel." The Messenger of Allah (ص) said to her, "Evil is the return you would make of her. There is no binding vow upon a person concerning what he does not own" (لَا نَذْرَ لِلْمَرْءِ فِيمَا لَا يَمْلِكُ). And he took the camel back.

When the Imam or the commander of the army assigns a reward to whoever slays a particular fighter — whether that reward is the slain man's spoils or something else stipulated for him — it is paid from the whole of the booty before any division takes place. The Messenger of Allah (ص) did exactly this on the day of Badr, declaring that whoever killed a man would receive such and such, and whoever took a captive would receive such and such. When Allah routed the polytheists — slain, captured, and put to flight — those through whose hands this had been accomplished came forward seeking what the Messenger of Allah (ص) had promised them.

Then Sa'd ibn Ubadah (رض) rose — he was among those whom the Messenger of Allah (ص) had stationed to guard him — and said, "O Messenger of Allah, what kept us from gaining what these men gained was neither cowardice before the enemy nor indifference to the reward and the booty. We feared only that your position might be left exposed and the polytheists fall upon your flank. If you give these men and that people what they ask, some of the Muslims will return with no share of the booty at all."

So Allah, Mighty and Majestic, sent down: ﴿يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْأَنْفَالِ قُلِ الْأَنْفَالُ لِلَّهِ وَالرَّسُولِ فَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَأَصْلِحُوا ذَاتَ بَيْنِكُمْ وَأَطِيعُوا اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ إِنْ كُنْتُمْ مُؤْمِنِينَ﴾ — *"They ask you concerning the spoils. Say: the spoils belong to Allah and the Messenger, so fear Allah and set things right among yourselves, and obey Allah and His Messenger, if you are believers"* [al-Anfal: 1]. The Messenger of Allah (ص) then commanded that those to whom he had made a promise be given, from the whole of the booty, exactly what he had promised them.

> وَمَآ أَفَآءَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ رَسُولِهِۦ مِنْهُمْ فَمَآ أَوْجَفْتُمْ عَلَيْهِ مِنْ خَيْلٍۢ وَلَا رِكَابٍۢ وَلَكِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُسَلِّطُ رُسُلَهُۥ عَلَىٰ مَن يَشَآءُ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍۢ قَدِيرٌۭ

Before the booty is divided, the army commander may award gifts from it in recognition of distinguished service, provided he has no personal share in what he distributes. He then sets aside one fifth for the Imam and divides the remaining four fifths equally among those who fought, contributing both their persons and their wealth. The horseman receives two shares; the foot soldier receives one. Whatever is taken without fighting, and without any advance against the enemy, belongs to the Imam.

﴿مَا أَفَاءَ اللَّهُ عَلَىٰ رَسُولِهِ مِنْ أَهْلِ الْقُرَىٰ فَلِلَّهِ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ﴾ "What Allah has restored to His Messenger from the people of the townships belongs to Allah, and to the Messenger, and to the near of kin" [al-Hashr: 7]. This verse follows His words: ﴿وَمَا أَفَاءَ اللَّهُ عَلَىٰ رَسُولِهِ مِنْهُمْ فَمَا أَوْجَفْتُمْ عَلَيْهِ مِنْ خَيْلٍ وَلَا رِكَابٍ وَلَٰكِنَّ اللَّهَ يُسَلِّطُ رُسُلَهُ عَلَىٰ مَنْ يَشَاءُ وَاللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَيْءٍ قَدِيرٌ﴾ "And what Allah restored to His Messenger from them, you spurred neither horse nor camel for it; but Allah gives His messengers authority over whom He wills, and Allah is powerful over all things" [al-Hashr: 6].

Al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad said: When the Imam dispatches a detachment or an army — or marches among them himself — and they engage the polytheists in battle, one fifth of what they capture belongs to the Imam, and the remaining four fifths are divided among the fighters: two shares for the horseman and one for the foot soldier. This applies when they strive as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has enjoined upon them, with their wealth and their persons. If, however, they go out for a wage and are given something in exchange for their service, then their entitlement is that of hired men, and whatever is gained belongs to the one who hired them — whether that be the Imam or someone the Imam appointed. Those who are present but take no part in the fighting receive nothing afterward except by the judgment of the Imam or of whoever the Imam has set over them; and women who are present receive no share of the booty. As for anyone who fights alongside them, the Imam — or whoever the Imam has appointed — may, at his discretion, assign him whatever portion he sees fit from the whole of the booty. Any slave of the polytheists who flees to the Muslims, or who seeks their safe conduct while his master remains behind, is free. All of this is the word of the Ahl al-Bayt.

The ruling now turns to the fighting of the people of rebellion (أَهْلُ الْبَغْيِ).

Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said: ﴿وَإِنْ طَائِفَتَانِ مِنَ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ اقْتَتَلُوا فَأَصْلِحُوا بَيْنَهُمَا فَإِنْ بَغَتْ إِحْدَاهُمَا عَلَى الْأُخْرَىٰ فَقَاتِلُوا الَّتِي تَبْغِي حَتَّىٰ تَفِيءَ إِلَىٰ أَمْرِ اللَّهِ﴾ "And if two parties of the believers fall to fighting, make peace between them; but if one of them transgresses against the other, then fight the one that transgresses until it returns to the command of Allah" [al-Hujurat: 9].

By this verse, Allah — exalted be His mention — has made the struggle against the people of rebellion an obligation upon the Muslims, just as He made the struggle against the polytheists an obligation. That obligation, however, is conditional: it falls only under the Imam, or under one whom the Imam has appointed, and it is precisely this condition that defines who the people of rebellion are. The matter is not left to individual judgment, so that any man may examine his own opinion and fight whomever he privately considers a rebel. Rather, the rebel is one who takes up arms against the Imam or against the one the Imam has appointed, or who withholds obedience, or who withdraws entirely from the Imam's jurisdiction.

When the Imam commands that such a person be fought, the people are bound to fight him alongside the Imam. And when the Imam refrains from fighting for a reason he judges sufficient, they refrain at his refraining and do not take up arms unless he grants them leave.

Ali said: "I was commanded to fight the breakers of the covenant (اَلنَّاكِثِينَ), the deviators (اَلْقَاسِطِينَ), and the renegades (الْمَارِقِينَ); the Messenger of Allah (ص) commanded me to that." The breakers of the covenant are the people of Basra, the Companions of the Camel; the renegades are the Kharijites; and the deviators are the people of Syria. "I turned the matter over on its back and its belly, its head and its eye, and found nothing in it but to fight them — or else to disbelieve in what Allah, Mighty and Majestic, sent down upon Muhammad (ص)."

It is related from Jafar ibn Muhammad that he heard his father, Muhammad ibn Ali, speak of those unjust ones whom Ali fought, and say that their sin was more grievous than the sin of those the Messenger of Allah (ص) had fought. He was asked, "How is that, O son of the Messenger of Allah?" He said, "Because those were people of ignorance, whereas these had read the Quran and knew the merit of the people of merit, yet they did what they did after clear sight." It has likewise come down that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said to Ali, "Your war is my war and your peace is my peace" (حَرْبُكَ حَرْبِي وَسِلْمُكَ سِلْمِي), so that whoever fights Ali has fought the Messenger of Allah (ص).

The Prophet (ص) described the Kharijites in these words: "They recite the Quran, yet it does not pass beyond their throats; they pass out of the religion as the arrow passes through the game. The best of men is he who kills them, and the best of men is he whom they kill." He also said to Ammar, "The rebellious faction shall kill you" (تَقْتُلُكَ الْفِئَةُ الْبَاغِيَةُ).

Muawiyah and his companions did kill Ammar, and when that tradition reached them it weighed heavily on Muawiyah, and the people of Syria were shaken by it. Amr ibn al-As then said to them, "It was not you who killed him; rather Ali and his companions killed him, for they were the ones who brought him out, and so they were the cause of his death."

Word of this reached Ali, and he said, "May Allah curse Amr. What deficient minds, to have accepted this from him. If it is we who killed him, then the Messenger of Allah (ص) killed those of the Muslims who were martyred at his side."

It was related that Ammar (رض) would take no path at Siffin without the people following him, such was the weight of what they had heard from the Messenger of Allah (ص). On the day he was slain, he was heard to say, as he gestured toward the people of Syria, "O Allah, You know that I have not fought them save by the command of Your Messenger, and that I do not know myself to be slain at their hands, as Ali told me I would be. Yet did I know that Your pleasure with me lay in kindling a fire and casting myself into it, I would do it; or in throwing myself from this beast of mine and breaking my neck, I would do it. O Allah, You know that I have not fought them save in seeking Your Face, so bring not my striving to nothing, nor give the lie to my hope." Then he turned toward the enemy.

Ammar looked toward Ali and caught sight of him. The standard was in the hand of Hashim ibn Utbah, who was one-eyed. Fixing his gaze on the planted standard, Ammar urged him forward, saying, "O Hashim, one-eyed and a coward? There is no good in a one-eyed man who does not plunge into the fray. Charge with us."

Hashim seized the standard from the ground and replied, "A one-eyed man seeks a station for his folk; he has grappled with life until he has wearied of it; there is no help for it but that he rout or be routed." Then he charged, and Ammar charged with him, crying, "Charge on, O Hashim, for death lies upon the points of the spears, and Paradise lies beneath the gleaming blades. Do you not see the wide-eyed maidens with Muhammad and his band, in the highest company?"

The two did not cease striking and thrusting. They plunged into the people of Syria and did not return until the mercy of Allah received them both as martyrs.

Two men then disputed before Muawiyah over the slaying of Ammar, each claiming, "It was I who killed him." Abd Allah ibn Amr ibn al-As, who was present with his father, said, "A wonder — that two men should quarrel over which of them shall enter the Fire. I heard the Messenger of Allah (ص) say, *The rebellious faction shall kill him*, and he said, *The slayer of Ammar and the despoiler of his arms are in the Fire.*" And Ali made mention of his own companions and of Muawiyah's companions who had fallen, and said, "Muawiyah and I shall be brought forth and shall dispute on the Day of Resurrection before the Lord of the Throne; and whichever of us prevails, his companions prevail."

As for summoning the people of rebellion to obedience before the fighting commences, that is the better course — and Ali did summon the people of the Camel and the people of the Canal. Yet if they are not summoned, and they already know what they are being called to, and the banner of guidance has been raised for them, then no blame falls upon whoever fights them before the summons is issued. Ali's own words on this point have already been related.

The people of rebellion are fought by every means available, just as the polytheists are fought. Any woman or child among them who takes part in the fighting may be fought; those who do not fight are not killed. The heirs of anyone slain among them inherit his estate — except for what he brought to the war against the people of justice and deployed against them. Whatever of that is seized becomes booty, divided as booty is divided in war against the polytheists, while their children and women remain free and are not enslaved.

There is no objection to seeking assistance against the people of rebellion from rebels of their own kind, from the corrupt among the Muslims, or from the people of the covenant. Should any of these join the people of justice in war against the polytheists, they receive shares from the four-fifths of the booty. That booty belongs to the Imam of the people of justice, not to the imam of the people of rebellion; if the latter lays claim to it, he is fought over it.

As for captives taken from the people of rebellion, the Imam may choose to put them to death, release them as a favor, hold them for ransom, or exchange them — but their free persons are not enslaved. Any slaves whom their masters brought out to war and who are subsequently captured become spoils. When the people of rebellion are routed and have no remaining faction to fall back on, they are left to disperse; they are not pursued, and their wounded are not finished off. This was Ali's practice after Talha and al-Zubayr were slain and Aishah was surrounded, for at that point their party had nowhere to retreat. Once they broke, he had it proclaimed among his companions: "Pursue no one who turns his back, kill no one who casts down his weapon, and dispatch no wounded man."

If, however, the routed force still has a faction to which it can withdraw, the pursuit continues with the sword and the pressure is pressed hard. At the Battle of Siffin, when Muawiyah's men were driven back while Muawiyah himself still held his position over them, Ali ordered that they be pursued and beset without relenting — until the moment the opposing side raised up copies of the Quran. Among his companions there then occurred the failing that occurred: he was commanding them to press on, yet when he could get nothing from them but that same failing, and they refused to continue the pursuit once the appeal to the Quran was raised, it became clear that this appeal was the stratagem and the guile by which Amr ibn al-As had contrived to save Muawiyah.

When the people of justice prevail over the people of rebellion, they reclaim whatever the rebels had severed, whatever had passed into rebel hands through usurpation, and whatever the rebels had appropriated for themselves — restoring it all to those to whom it rightfully belongs. As for merchants, women, and children found in the rebel camp: if they were present with the rebels in the field, they constitute booty, having been brought out among the rebels' own provisions. If, however, they were situated in a city, then whatever of theirs was used to sustain the war against the people of justice — or was capable of being so used, or had been brought out for that purpose — becomes booty for the people of justice upon their victory. The rule governing its division follows the same rule that applies to spoils taken from the polytheists.

Whomever the people of justice slay from among the people of rebellion — whether in open battle, in night raids, or in campaigns — no liability falls upon the people of justice for those deaths, provided the slain remained rebels at the time, nor for whatever spoils were taken from them. And if the rebels relent and return to the truth, submitting to the authority of the just Imam, then all that the people of justice had taken was taken by a right that was properly theirs — a right that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, permitted them and commanded them to exercise.

When rebels seize property belonging to the people of justice during their revolt, they are obliged to restore it to its rightful owners. Where the individual who took a particular thing is identified, it is recovered from him directly, for he took it without right. Where the taker cannot be identified, nothing remains but an oath sworn against the claimant. And where a man of the people of justice has been slain and his killer is known, the kinsmen of the slain may take retaliation from that killer, for he killed wrongfully. That act of retaliation is itself the very substance of returning to obedience — repentance, and the clearing of oneself from wrongs.

As for whatever the rebels inflict upon one another, the judgment of the people of justice does not run between them. Such matters are not examined at all, unless the one set in authority over the rebels encounters a particular case in the ordinary course of his office; in that circumstance he adjudicates it, provided it is properly established before him.

Should the sound among the rebels sue for truce, the matter is laid before the people of justice, and their leader does what he judges best for himself and for the Muslims under his command, until such time as he is able to fight them. If he grants them a covenant, he must keep faith with it. If he afterward wishes to make war on them, he must first return their covenant to them, as was established in the matter of a truce with the polytheists. He may not make peace on any terms that surrender the limits set by Allah or His sacred bounds, forfeit any right owed to Him, or leave undone any duty the religion requires. Nor is a man of religion forbidden to kill his own father when his father is among the rebels — unless he fears him, or finds no way to do so except by his own hand; if another can undertake it in his place, he leaves it to that other. All of this is the united teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt.

As for those of the People of the Qibla who must be fought and, if need be, killed: whoever denies an obligation among the obligations of Islam, or withholds a due among its dues, has repudiated a duty binding upon him. He is to be fought once he has taken up arms in that denial and refused to return to the truth. And whoever stands behind him or lends him aid shares his position — he is fought and killed until he returns to the command of Allah.

As for brigands who fall upon people intending to seize their wealth, those attacked may lawfully fight them and kill them in defense of their property — and in defense of anything unlawfully demanded of them, or of the Muslims and the covenanted people in their company. Yet fighting such men is not a binding obligation in the way that jihad is.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about a man killed defending his property. He answered that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "Whoever is killed defending his wealth is a martyr" (مَنْ قُتِلَ دُونَ مَالِهِ فَهُوَ شَهِيدٌ).

Even so, if a man chooses to let the wealth go rather than fight for it — fearing that resistance would cost him his life — that too is permitted, for surrendering property is easier than courting destruction. Whoever does stand firm, however, carries the reward of one who holds fast to what is right, and is rightly guided and rewarded, because the Messenger of Allah (ص) named the one slain in that cause a martyr.

Jafar ibn Muhammad related that a man came to Ali and said, "O Commander of the Faithful, a man entered upon my wife and stole her jewelry while I looked on, and I let him be, out of fear of him." Ali said, "But had it been Ali ibn Abi Talib, he would not have consented to that until he had risen against him with the sword."

Whatever is taken by the hands of thieves, if its owner recognizes it, is returned to him; whatever the owner cannot identify belongs to those who fought the thieves for it. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said, "The spoils of brigands are lawful" (سَلْبُ اللُّصُوصِ حَلَالٌ). The spy and the informer may be put to death by whatever means one has power over him. And if the Imam sees fit to imprison the brigand, to make an example of him, to punish him as he judges right, or to release him, that is his to do — until the man repents in prison.

The apostate stands in the position of a captive, even if he is among the polytheists; no covenant or grant of safety is accepted from him, and the Imam may take him as a slave if he chooses. Whoever was born into Islam and then turns Jew or Christian, or otherwise departs from the faith, is put to death and granted no term in which to repent — unless he repents before he is seized, in accordance with the word of the Messenger of Allah (ص): "Whoever changes his religion, kill him" (مَنْ بَدَّلَ دِينَهُ فَاقْتُلُوهُ) — meaning one who turns away from Islam.

If, however, a Jew becomes a Christian, or a Christian becomes a Jew, or a man passes from one form of unbelief into another like it, he is not molested for it. Unbelief is a single thing, one creed; such a man is not called an apostate. The apostate is the one who leaves a religion and turns to its opposite. So when a polytheist enters Islam and then reverts to polytheism, his case is brought before the ruler, who calls him to repent for three days; if he has not repented by the third day, he is put to death. This is how Ali dealt with an apostate brought before him. When apostates hold out together in refusal, they are fought as the polytheists are fought.

An apostate woman is imprisoned until she repents. An apostate bondwoman is beaten and given only what sustains her — bare food and clothing enough to cover her — while her owners are required to put her to the hardest labor until she repents or dies in that condition. She is not to be confined in a way that cuts her owners off entirely from the benefit of her service.

If apostates, together with their women, refuse Islam and fortify themselves in a stronghold or a district, they are fought as the polytheists are fought. If they are overcome and still do not repent, their women and children are taken as captives. The women are then compelled toward Islam in the same manner as apostate bondwomen are compelled. They are not to be given settled residence or given in marriage, for it is not lawful to marry an apostate woman. As for the children among them, when they reach the age of discernment, Islam is set before them, and their way thereafter is the way prescribed for apostate women and men.

Heretics (الزَّنَادِقَة) are put to death even if they repent, for no term for repentance is granted to them; after their execution, they are burned with fire. This was the way of Ali when a party of people was brought before him who had gone to excess in what they attributed to him, claiming that he was a god — exalted be Allah far above what they claimed; there is no god but He. He questioned them, and they professed it. He called them to repent, and they persisted, saying, "If you kill us, you will bring us back to life." At that, a mighty anger seized him. He commanded that a pit be dug; it was dug, and a fire was kindled in it. He said, "Today I shall glut you with flesh and fat." He then ordered his freedman Qanbar, who struck their necks, and cast them into the pit and burned them in it. Looking upon what he had witnessed that day, Ali said:

"لَمَّا رَأَيْتُ الْيَوْمَ أَمْرًا مُنْكَرًا، أَضْرَمْتُ نَارًا وَدَعَوْتُ قَنْبَرًا"

— *When I beheld this day a thing most foul, I kindled a fire and I called upon Qanbar.*
